# Clinical Case Studies: Twenty Cases That Went Wrong Before They Went Right

A patient arrives with a complaint. A team of experts meets, builds a differential
diagnosis, ranks it by likelihood and by danger, and commits to a working diagnosis.
A therapy is chosen and begun. A nurse applies it, follows the patient, and watches
the numbers and the patient. And then the therapy does not work. The patient does not
improve, or improves and then slides back, and something that was present from the
very first hour turns out to have been pointing at a different disease the whole
time. The team reopens the case, finds the clue it had read without seeing, changes
the approach, and the patient recovers.

That arc is the whole of this book. It is told twenty times, in twenty different
specialties, in twenty different settings, and each time it fails in a different
way. A collapse in a garden that was written off as a faint. Breathlessness after a
flight that was treated as pneumonia. A cough that outlasted two courses of
antibiotics. An ankle that was hot. A stomach ache that was stomach ache. A woman
who would not sit still, a woman who woke up shaking, a woman who fainted while
shopping, a woman of eighty-four who was agitated, and a lady of eighty-two whose
urine was not an infection. Each case turns on a detail that was there from the
beginning and that nobody read, and in most of them the person who read it was the
nurse.

The twenty cases are constructed composites. No case describes a real patient, no
detail identifies anyone living or dead, and no institution is described in a way
that could be recognized. They are built to make a piece of reasoning visible, and a
real ward is messier, slower and far more surprising than anything on these pages.
Where a number appears it is an illustration of reasoning rather than a fact to act
on, and it must be confirmed against the current guideline and the local protocol
before anyone relies on it, because the numbers differ between countries, between
editions, and between the laboratory and the ward. This book is educational material.
It is not medical advice, it is not a protocol, and it is not a substitute for
training, supervision, or the judgment of the clinician who is actually with the
patient.

The book opens with the cases themselves, grouped into five parts by the system they
concern, and closes with two appendices. The first draws the twenty failures
together into ten patterns of reasoning that go wrong, and the second collects the
twenty nursing observations that opened each case, because in every one of these
stories the nurse is not the person who executes the plan. The nurse is the person
who watches it fail, and who notices that it is failing.

It is meant to be heard in order once, and returned to chapter by chapter
afterwards.

# Table of Contents

The collection comprises twenty chapters and two appendices. Here they are, in listening order.

Chapter one. The Woman Who Fainted in the Garden. Chapter two. The Breathless Woman After the Flight. Chapter three. The Asthma That Would Not Budge. Chapter four. The Hot Ankle.

Chapter five. The Stone That Was Not a Stone. Chapter six. The Bellyache Too Severe to Touch. Chapter seven. The Indigestion That Would Not Settle. Chapter eight. The Girl with the Stomach Ache.

Chapter nine. The Swelling That Was Not Heart Failure. Chapter ten. The Bruises That Were Not Bruises. Chapter eleven. The Acidosis That Was Called Sepsis. Chapter twelve. The Woman Who Would Not Sit Still.

Chapter thirteen. The Woman Who Woke Up Shaking. Chapter fourteen. The Migraine That Was Treated as a Stroke. Chapter fifteen. The Fits That Stopped When She Was Distracted. Chapter sixteen. The Fit That Was Not a Fit.

Chapter seventeen. The Cough That Outlasted the Antibiotics. Chapter eighteen. Five Days of Fever. Chapter nineteen. Agitation in a Lady of Eighty-Four. Chapter twenty. The Urine That Was Not an Infection.

Appendix A. Ten Ways a Reasonable Team Gets It Wrong. Appendix B. The Nursing Observation That Opened the Case.


# Chapter 1 — The Woman Who Fainted in the Garden

A collapse is a short answer to a short question, and short answers are read too quickly. This chapter follows a woman who fainted in her garden, and the fact that her body had been offering an answer for six weeks while everyone around her was asking a different question. The patient is Margaret Ashby, who is 68, a retired school administrator, lives alone on the edge of a small town, and who was brought to the emergency department three times in six weeks after being found on the ground with no memory of the fall. Nothing here describes a real patient. The case is a constructed composite, assembled to make one failure visible, and it identifies no one living or dead.

This material is educational, and it says so again here because the habit it teaches is a habit of attention. It is not medical advice, it is not a protocol, and it is not a substitute for training, supervision, or the judgment of the clinician who is with the patient. Even the age and the interval quoted here are illustrations of reasoning and not thresholds to act on, so the numbers your own service uses to judge a recurrence should be taken from the current guideline and the local protocol. The failure this chapter examines is not carelessness. It is a frame: once a working diagnosis is chosen, each later finding is read as confirmation, and the one detail that would have broken the frame is the detail nobody thinks to ask about.

Margaret Ashby had been a school administrator for most of her working life, and the manner of it still showed in the way she spoke to doctors and in the way she apologised for taking up an afternoon. She lived alone in a bungalow with a small garden behind it, and the garden was the reason she was found on the ground three times, because it was where she hung the washing and stood in the afternoon to look at the hedge. The neighbours had a key for the back door and knew where she kept it. On each occasion a neighbour had found her within the hour, she had no memory of falling, and the last clear thing she remembered was standing up.

The third attendance contains a line that nobody read as anything but housekeeping. Asked what she was taking, Margaret Ashby said her blood pressure tablet and nothing else, and then added that she had started buying new eye drops for her glaucoma over the counter at the pharmacy, because the repeats had stopped being sent and the drops were not something she was willing to run out of. She said it while pulling her cardigan down over her arms, as a piece of domestic administration rather than as a symptom. The note was filed with the rest, below the blood pressure and below the end of the medication list, and it was not read by anybody again.

Each of the three attendances ended in the same way. The heart was listened to, the blood pressure was taken, and an electrocardiogram, or ECG, was recorded that showed a slow rate, a delay in the electrical conduction of the upper chambers and a broad complex in the lower chambers. The report said that nothing was acute, and the delay in conduction was attributed to her age and to the antihypertensive that had been increased at the last clinic. Margaret Ashby was given a diagnosis of fainting, advice to rise slowly, and a review appointment. On the third visit she was asked again about her tablets and gave the same answer, and the line about the eye drops sat below the end of the medication list where nobody looked.

All three attendances began with a call from a neighbour and an ambulance, and all three ended with a review appointment and a letter saying that this was a common and benign problem in a person of her age. Nothing about that was unreasonable, because she was found on her feet or on her side, she was not injured, and she could not remember the fall. The pattern that mattered was the repetition, and repetition in a person who lives alone is easy to explain away, because every time she went out she was careful, and every time she was careful she did not fall. The neighbours were well meaning and quite sure that she had stood up too quickly.

After the third attendance she was admitted for planned cardiac evaluation, and that admission is where the case can be watched properly. The team was a cardiology registrar, Simon Ridpath, a consultant cardiologist, Patricia Neve, and a nurse practitioner, Anna Crowther, who ran the falls assessment service and had been involved with Margaret Ashby since her second collapse. They met at the bedside on the afternoon of the admission with a list of possible explanations and a decision to make before the morning. Their task was not to find a rare diagnosis. It was to work out whether this was a benign problem that needed a plan and a bit of reassurance, or something that would kill her without warning.

The differential they built was, in order of likelihood, a vasovagal episode, a postural fall in blood pressure and a seizure. Below those they put a disturbance of the heart's rhythm, a narrowing of the aortic valve and a metabolic cause such as a low blood sugar. The dangerous end of that list was the rhythm disturbance, and specifically a very slow heart rate producing what are called Stokes-Adams attacks, in which the heart pauses for long enough to switch the brain off briefly and the person collapses without warning. The finding that would have revealed it was a slow pulse during a collapse, and a history in which the collapse arrives with no warning at all.

There was one fact that did not fit, and it was visible in the very first set of notes. Margaret Ashby had no prodrome. A vasovagal episode usually announces itself with warmth, sweating, nausea, a dimming of vision and a feeling that something is about to happen, and she had none of that on any of the three occasions. The registrar reasoned that the warning is often less obvious in older people, that the episode had come while she was standing in the garden, and that a vagal response to standing would explain the setting. The consultant agreed that a missing warning in a woman who was already a little slow was thin evidence against a common diagnosis, and that the evidence for it had better be in the history. The reasoning was fluent, and it accounted for the missing prodrome by making the missing prodrome less important.

What they decided was a plan for a postural problem and for a possible problem with the blood pressure tablet. Margaret Ashby was asked to drink more and to take more salt, she was asked to rise in stages from sitting and from lying, and the antihypertensive that had been increased at the last clinic was reduced. A falls assessment was carried out with an eye to the bathroom, the height of the bed, the floor and the route from the bedroom. She was reassured that this was a common and benign problem, and she was given a letter that said so, and she asked whether she could carry on going into the garden and was told that she could.

She took the reassurance and used it, and the effect of that on the case was considerable. She told the registrar that she had felt faint the week before but had not gone down, and had not mentioned it to anybody because she was sure it was the tablets, and because a person told that her faints are nothing in particular does not report feeling faint. The letter went into her handbag, she stopped telling her daughter-in-law about the garden, the family stopped offering to drive her, and the isolation that had been protective became the condition in which the fourth collapse was found by a neighbour rather than by anyone who knew her well. She had told the truth at every attendance, and the reassurance she was given was a reason to stop telling it.

The nursing was where this case was actually kept, and Anna Crowther treated it that way. She began by writing down every medicine Margaret Ashby could take, including those kept in the kitchen, and asked separately about anything bought without a prescription, because she had learned that the answer is usually in the second question. She carried out the falls assessment with the observations that belong with it, and set up telemetry and a lying and standing blood pressure chart, in which the blood pressure is taken while the patient lies flat and again after she has been standing for a set period, because a postural cause should show itself as a fall in the standing pressure. She asked what Margaret Ashby had been drinking, and wrote down that she had been drinking very little since the last clinic, because advice to drink more is only as good as what actually goes in.

The chart came back with nothing on it, in the sense that mattered. Margaret Ashby's standing blood pressure did not fall in the way a postural cause requires, and the difference between the lying and the standing pressures was small, though the size of the fall that counts as abnormal depends on the patient and the circumstances, so take the number your own service uses from the current guideline and the local protocol. Anna Crowther wrote that a normal lying and standing chart did not explain a collapse that had happened three times, and that she would raise it on the ward round. The registrar agreed that the chart made a postural cause less likely, which was true, and then said that what was left was the faint itself, which was not.

The telemetry ran overnight and recorded something the team had already half seen and not wanted to see. Margaret Ashby's heart rate fell during sleep into the low forties, and lower still, with pauses long enough to set off the recorder's alarm. A resting rate that low is worth a second look in an older adult, and the point at which it becomes dangerous depends on the person and the setting, so the number that should alarm a team should be taken from the current guideline and the local protocol. The registrar read the tracing as a slow heart rate in a relaxed older woman, and the consultant noted that sleep slows the heart and that it fitted with the slow rate on the ECG in the emergency department.

Meanwhile Margaret Ashby was having the worst night of her life without knowing it. She had not been in the garden since the third collapse, and she had begun to leave the back door unlocked so that a neighbour could get in, which she described as sensible and her daughter-in-law described as alarming. She had also begun to sit on the edge of the bed in the dark before standing, and to count to ten before she got up, which nobody had suggested and nobody had noticed. The ward round that morning described her as bright, orientated and apologetic about the disturbance, and she asked whether she could go home that afternoon, and she said she was sure it was nothing.

The collapse happened on the second morning, in the corridor outside the day case area, in front of Anna Crowther, who had come back to finish the lying and standing chart. Margaret Ashby had been walking with a frame and had stopped to turn around. She said the word "floor", and then she was on the tiles and she was not moving. She was grey rather than flushed, and there was a brief shaking of the hands that stopped on its own. Anna Crowther did not shout for help first. She put two fingers on the neck, and she looked at her watch, and she counted.

The rate she counted was very slow, in the region of forty beats per minute, with pauses in which the pulse was not there at all. A pulse that slow during a collapse, in a person whose tracing already showed a delay in conduction, is a different problem from a faint, and the rate and the pause that should call for an emergency response are set by the current guideline and the local protocol in every unit that has one. The team arrived within a minute, found the patient still unconscious but with a pulse, lifted her onto a blanket, turned her onto her side and took her to a bed. Anna Crowther said, to nobody in particular, that there had been no warning of any kind.

The temptation in the corridor was to treat the episode and move on. There is a strong pull towards putting the oxygen on, filling the bed, checking the blood sugar and writing a note that says the patient collapsed and recovered, because that note ends the event. The registrar, who had been in the department for years, felt that pull and also felt something else: the particular discomfort of a patient who has collapsed on her own ward after three collapses discharged with reassurance. He found himself hoping that the count would turn out to be something else, and he was careful afterwards to write in the record that he had hoped for it.

What Anna Crowther wrote in the nursing note that morning was short and is the reason this case exists. She recorded that there had been no prodrome and no warning, that the patient said the single word "floor" and then stopped, that the face was grey and cold and not flushed, that the shaking of the hands was brief and did not follow the pattern of a seizure, that the pulse during the collapse was very slow with pauses, that the patient was conscious again very quickly and completely oriented, and that the pulse stayed slow for a while afterwards. She also recorded that she had asked three colleagues whether they had ever seen that grey face with a slow pulse, and that they had not. It was the only note in the record that described the collapse instead of summarising it.

And then she did the thing that no protocol required of her. She went back to the notes from the third attendance and read them the way you read a note when you no longer believe the first reading, which is to say slowly, and without the frame you brought to them. The blood pressure was stable, the blood sugar was normal, and below the end of the medication list there was a line about new eye drops for glaucoma, bought over the counter at a pharmacy, added because the repeats had stopped being sent. The nurse practitioner read that line three times and then went to find the patient.

Anna Crowther asked the question she had not asked in six weeks, and asked it in a way that did not suggest there was an answer. She asked what Margaret Ashby put in her eyes, then what else she used that was not on the prescription list, and then when the drops had changed. The answer was that the eye clinic had changed the drops before the first collapse, some months earlier, and that she had not been back since because the repeats had stopped. Anna Crowther rang the eye clinic and then the community pharmacist, and both told her the same thing about the drops: they belonged to the group of drugs that block beta receptors, they were used to lower the pressure inside the eye, and enough of the drug was absorbed through the eye to slow a heart that was already slow.

A beta-blocking eye drop is absorbed through the eye into the circulation, and its effect on the heart is small in most people and considerable in people who already have a slow heart rate or a delay in conduction, and Margaret Ashby had both of those things. She had been given a stronger preparation some months before the first collapse and had taken it without interruption since. The drops had not caused her conduction problem, but they had been making a heart already conducting badly stop altogether at the wrong moment. The mechanism was not difficult to understand once it had been said out loud, and it was not difficult to explain to the family.

The team met again that afternoon with the drops in front of them, and the reasoning turned inside out inside an hour. Patricia Neve asked what would have to be true for the eye drops to be innocent, and nobody could answer it, and she asked instead what would have to be true for them to be guilty, which was easy, because it was already written in the notes from the first hour. The plan changed in three parts. The eye drops were stopped and the eye team were asked to find another way to control the pressure inside the eye. The conducting system was assessed properly, with a longer recording and a review by the electrophysiology team. And the collapse on the ward was written up as an event in its own right, so that it belonged to the record.

The assessment showed what the telemetry had hinted at: the heart was conducting badly, with pauses long enough to switch the brain off and a rate that fell too far at night. The team then faced a second question, whether the pauses were caused by the eye drops alone or whether the conducting system was permanently faulty. Stopping the drops answered the first part of that question and not the second: her resting rate improved, but the tracing still showed a fault that would not have been there in a healthy heart, and she had already had three attacks that nobody had thought to observe. Patricia Neve explained the choice in plain words: the drops had been the trigger and the wiring the underlying fault, and the pacemaker would take over the job the faulty wiring could no longer do.

Anna Crowther was part of that decision rather than a witness to it, and she argued for one specific thing. She wanted a written plan for what the ward would do if anyone collapsed again, in which the first action was to count the pulse and the second was to write down what was seen before anything was moved or given. The procedure was small, it was written on the card at the end of the bed, and it took effect that afternoon. She said that a ward full of competent people had spent six weeks being kind to a patient who was getting steadily worse, and that the least she could do was make sure the next collapse was written down.

The pacemaker was done as a planned procedure under a local anaesthetic, and Margaret Ashby was monitored overnight and then went home. The follow-up was unremarkable, and the point of the case is not that a pacemaker is a triumph but that it was the answer to a problem that had been filed under the wrong heading for six weeks. The syncope did not recur. She went back into the garden. The daughter-in-law stopped being alarmed, and Margaret Ashby went back to apologising for taking up afternoons.

What Anna Crowther watches for now is different from what she watched for before. She watches for a pulse that is slow at rest rather than for a fall in blood pressure, and for any new medicine that affects the heart rate, including anything bought without a prescription, and she asks about that at every admission and clinic visit. She asks about drops in the eyes and ears and anything taken for a headache, and what the family keeps in the kitchen cabinet. And she asks who else is in the house, because the woman who fainted alone for six weeks was found by a neighbour, and the next time it might not be. And she asks about the drops, because the drops are the medicine that nobody puts on the list.

Two things changed in the service, and both of them were about records rather than about people. Any patient admitted after a collapse now has a full medication history taken by nursing staff that includes everything bought without a prescription, and that history is read on the ward round. Her eye pressure was controlled in the end by a different preparation, and the eye clinic review asked the general practice for the same list at every annual check. Any collapse on a ward is recorded as a clinical event with the pulse counted and the observations described, so that the next person sees the episode rather than a summary of it. Nobody in this case did anything wrong: the registrar, the consultant and the nurse practitioner were each reasoning correctly inside a frame built from a reasonable first answer, and any team meeting a woman who fainted in a garden would have built the same frame.

The general lesson is about absences, because this diagnosis was made by what was not there. There was no warning before the collapse, and that absence was worth more than any of the positives, because it is what separates a falling blood pressure from a failing heart. A faint that announces itself is usually benign, and a collapse that arrives without warning in an older person, with a slow pulse, is a cardiac problem until it has been shown otherwise. The showing is done with a pulse count and a tracing, and not with reassurance.

The second lesson is about what happens in the minute the patient is on the floor. The instinct to treat is a good instinct and it should not be delayed, but it is not the only thing that should happen at the same time. Someone should be counting, someone should be watching the face, and someone should be writing. A collapse on a ward in front of a witness is one of the few gifts a diagnosis is ever given, and the price of it is that somebody has to stop moving the patient around and start describing what they see.

The third lesson is the plainest. A medication list built only from the prescription record is not a medication list. It is a record of what has been ordered, which is a different thing from what has been taken, and it will not contain the eye drops bought over the counter at a pharmacy, the painkillers, the antihistamines, the herbal remedies and the borrowed tablets that make up a great deal of what a person actually swallows. Asking is cheap. The nurse who asked was the person who had been reading the notes, and the answer had been in the notes all along.

It is worth saying how much earlier this could have been caught, because the point of a teaching case is the turning point and not the post mortem. At the second attendance, one question about what she put in her eyes would have found the drops. At the first attendance, a tracing that showed a delay in conduction in a woman who collapses without warning should have raised a slow heart rate rather than been filed as nothing acute. And at the third, the nurse who wrote the line about the eye drops could have read it aloud, because the number of times a person collapses is a reason to look for a cause rather than to repeat the same advice.

To summarize, Margaret Ashby fainted four times, and the diagnosis was wrong for six weeks because it was built on a reasonable first answer and then defended. The clue that would have broken the frame was on the page from the first hour, in a line about eye drops bought over the counter, and it went unread because nobody asks about eye drops. The nurse who found it did two things no protocol required of her: to read the old notes again, and to count the pulse during a collapse instead of managing it. The general lesson is that an absence of warning is a finding, that a collapse in front of a witness is an opportunity rather than an event to be tidied away, and that a medication list from the prescription record is a list of what was ordered rather than of what was taken. Every number in this chapter is an illustration of reasoning and must be confirmed against the current guideline and the local protocol. This book is educational material, it is not medical advice, and the patient in front of you always outranks the page.

# Chapter 2 — The Breathless Woman After the Flight

Sophie Turner described her chest pain to the triage nurse as a knife, on the left side, and she said it caught her every time she breathed in. She said it exactly like that, without stopping to find better words, and the nurse wrote the sentence down as it was spoken rather than as it would appear in a textbook. Sophie Turner was 31, she taught primary school, and she had been unwell for nine hours. Her age and the number of hours between landing and collapse are illustrations of the reasoning and not thresholds to act on, so the numbers your own service uses should be read from the current guideline and the local protocol. The nurse was Joanne Mercer, new enough in that department to still do things the old way.

Nothing in this chapter describes a real patient. It is a constructed composite, assembled to make one particular failure visible, and it identifies no one living or dead. The failure it examines is a frame that formed in ten minutes and held for the two days it took to notice it, and it held because every test the team asked for came back reassuring while the patient got no better. The lesson of this case is that a clear chest radiograph is a result and not an answer, and that a result which does not fit the patient in front of you is a reason to ask a better question rather than a reason to stop asking.

The flight had been long, with a change and a long delay in the middle, and Sophie Turner had spent most of it folded into a seat with her knees up and her feet swollen by the end of the first hour. She had drunk a bottle of water and had not moved. She had also started her period on the aeroplane, and not long before she flew a check-up had given her a new combined oral contraceptive, and her mother had died of a clot in her late forties. None of these is a diagnosis, and no single one of them would have made anybody stop and think, but they are the details that a breathless patient is asked about, and they were asked about on that morning, because Joanne Mercer had been taught to ask about the legs.

Joanne Mercer took Sophie Turner's temperature, her pulse, her blood pressure and her oxygen saturation, and wrote the numbers where she could not misread them later. The saturation was 92 per cent on air, the pulse was 110 beats per minute and regular, the respiratory rate was 24 breaths per minute, and the temperature was 37.2 degrees Celsius. Every one of those numbers is an illustration of reasoning rather than a fact to act on, and the values your own service treats as abnormal should be read from the current guideline and the local protocol. The blood pressure was 120 over 75 millimeters of mercury, the chest was clear on both sides, and the heart rate was the only thing on the sheet that did not fit a person with a cold.

What Joanne Mercer did in the next few minutes is the reason this case ends well, and she did it because she had been taught to ask a breathless woman about her legs. She asked about the flight, how long Sophie Turner had been sitting still, whether she had been drinking, and whether she smoked. She asked about the contraceptive, when it had been changed, whether she had ever been told not to take it, whether she had ever had a clot in her leg or in her chest, and whether she had had any surgery. She asked about her family, and Sophie Turner told her that her mother had died of a clot in her late forties, suddenly, and that she had been warned about it at the time. Then Joanne Mercer looked at the leg.

The left calf was swollen, tight and tender halfway between the knee and the ankle, and the ankle on that side pitted to the touch as well. Joanne Mercer measured both calves at the same point with the same tape and found the left one to be two centimeters larger than the right, and wrote the measurement in the notes rather than writing that the leg was swollen. The difference between two calves is a measurement and not an impression, and the difference worth acting on varies with the patient and the setting, so confirm it against the current guideline and the local protocol. Sophie Turner said the swelling had come on during the flight, as though apologising for it, and she had crossed her legs for most of the journey and had not thought about the calf since.

The triage sheet in that service had the observations at the top and a space for a description underneath, and the shape of the sheet decided what happened next. Joanne Mercer wrote her description below the line, where a description belongs, because that is what she had been taught. She wrote the measurement of the left calf, that there was no cough, no sputum and no fever, that the mother had died of a clot, that the flight had been long, and that the contraceptive was new. Then she handed the sheet to the registrar, who read the top of it, asked a question about the chest, and moved on. Nothing about that was unusual, and nothing about it was a decision; it is simply that a description written in the bottom third of a page is a description nobody reads.

The registrar was Priya Raman, and she took the history again from Sophie Turner, and the history sounded like pneumonia. The patient had felt tired and achy for a couple of days before she flew, she had a sore throat on the aeroplane, and she had been coughing a little since. She had no fever and no rigors, and she had been sick twice in the night. The blood tests showed a white cell count at the upper end of the reference range, a finding that supports many diagnoses and settles none, and the range that matters should be read from the current guideline and the local protocol and from the laboratory's own interval.

The dangerous possibility was named at triage and it was named again on the ward round, because a clot in the lung has to be considered in every breathless patient and considered early. Priya Raman asked aloud what would have to be true for this to be a clot rather than an infection, and the consultant, Douglas Kerr, answered with a list. Breathlessness out of proportion to what the chest shows, a heart rate faster than the chest radiograph would explain, pain in the chest that is sharp, one-sided and worse on breathing in, and a swollen calf on the same side as the pain. Two of those were present, and one was present in a way that had not been noticed at all, and the consultant put the clot at the bottom of the list because the patient was young and well and had a clear chest radiograph.

Douglas Kerr's reasoning was not foolish and it was not careless. Pneumonia is common in adults and it is serious, and a breathless woman with pleuritic pain and a raised white cell count is a chest infection until she is shown otherwise. The chest radiograph had been reported as showing no consolidation, and the registrar had said that the patient was too unwell for that film to be true, and that a chest infection can hide behind a normal picture early on. The consultant accepted that. The consequence was that the clear film was read as reassuring rather than as a question, and the swollen calf went into the notes and stayed there.

So the working diagnosis was pneumonia, and the treatment followed from it: antibiotics, oxygen, a chest radiograph and observation, with the plan being to review her in the morning and to expect her to be no worse and no better at that point. The team ranked the alternatives in the order they would think of them: pneumonia first, a viral infection of the lung second, an anxiety reaction to travel third, and the clot at the bottom of the list despite being the one that could kill her quickly. The description at the bottom of the list was not that it was impossible. It was that it was unlikely, and that was the difference that mattered.

The nursing on that ward was done with care, and the care made the failure more visible rather than less. Joanne Mercer took the observations, kept the fluid and symptom chart, and did something that the ward had been doing for years without anybody having a name for it, which was to measure the oxygen saturation before and after the patient had walked. She explained it to Sophie Turner as a way of seeing how hard the lungs were working. Sophie Turner was not an unwell-looking young woman. She was frightened, she was apologising for taking up a bed, and she had a bag of marking to do that night.

The chart recorded that her saturation while sitting was in the low nineties and that it fell after a walk to the toilet and back, and that it came back up over the following minutes. The saturation at which a team should worry is set by the current guideline and the local protocol, and it moves with the patient, the position and the effort, so the number that matters should be taken from the same place rather than from this chart. The team looked at it and saw a young woman who was anxious, who had been lying down all afternoon, and who became breathless when she stood because a long flight had deconditioned her. The desaturation was filed as anxiety, and it was the clearest thing on the chart.

The first night was unremarkable in the way that a well-run ward makes things unremarkable. She slept, she was not sick again, the line came down because she was drinking, and she was able to keep the antibiotics down. In the morning she said she felt slightly better and no more than slightly better, and she asked whether she could go home to mark her books. She had no fever, her chest was still clear, and her saturation was better sitting up than the day before. The registrar wrote that this was what a chest infection on treatment looked like, and she was not being careless, only faithful to the diagnosis she held.

Two days in, she was no better. The antibiotics had been given without any evidence that they were needed, and there was still no fever, no cough, no sputum, and the chest radiograph was still clear. The white cell count had fallen towards normal while she remained breathless, which should have been the most informative result of the admission, because a count that falls as the patient does not improve was never measuring an infection. Her saturation fell again when she walked to the toilet, further than it had the day before. How long a patient should be observed before the diagnosis is revisited, and what should happen to the treatment then, are matters for the current guideline and the local protocol.

The team discussed this on the morning round, and the discussion was honest, which is how these cases usually get lost. The consultant said that a chest infection could still be the answer, that two days of treatment was not enough to be certain, and asked for the radiograph to be repeated and the antibiotics continued. The registrar wanted to send her home with oral antibiotics and a review, and wanted to know why the saturation dropped when her patient walked. What nobody said was the sentence that would have cracked the case: a patient no better after two days of treatment for a disease the chest film does not show does not have that disease. The length of a treatment before it is judged to have failed belongs to the current guideline and the local protocol, not to the mood of a ward.

Joanne Mercer went back to the triage sheet after the round, and she read it from the top, which she had not done since she handed it over. She read the observations, and then she read the description underneath, and she found her own words. The left calf, the measurement of it, the mother who had died of a clot, the new contraceptive. Then she went and measured the calf again, at the same point, with the same tape, and it was still larger than the right by the same amount, and she telephoned the registrar and asked her to come and look at a leg rather than at a chest.

Priya Raman came and looked at the leg, and then asked Sophie Turner to tell her again about the flight, her mother, the contraceptive, and whether she had ever had a leg that swelled. Sophie Turner remembered, after a moment, that she had had exactly that on a flight some years before, put it down to sitting badly, and mentioned it to nobody at all. The chest was clear, the radiograph was clear, the white cell count was falling, the saturation fell when she walked, and the pulse was fast with nothing in the chest to account for it. Priya Raman wrote all of it down at the bedside, and none of it was the sentence that would have cracked the case.

That is a constellation, and a constellation is a different kind of evidence from any single sign inside it. A structured score was used to organise the reasoning rather than to replace it, because a score counts features known to travel together and puts a number on the whole, so that the weight of the pattern is not lost in the noise of any one item. The threshold that would have triggered a scan is set by the current guideline and the local protocol, and a rule built for one population deserves care when it is used in another. What the score did was make it impossible for anyone to keep saying that the radiograph was reassuring, because the score did not care about the radiograph.

The argument in favour of waiting was not stupid, and it was made by good people. If she had pneumonia, treating her as though she had a clot would expose her to the risk of bleeding for nothing, and she was young, she had a clear radiograph, and a great many young women with breathlessness and pain on breathing in have neither of the two dangerous things. The argument against waiting was that the failure of treatment is itself information, and that a patient who is no better is saying that the diagnosis is wrong, which is a finding and not an embarrassment. The balance was not a close one once the calf had been measured twice.

So the treatment changed that morning, and it changed on the strength of the history and the examination rather than on the strength of the radiograph. Anticoagulation was begun, because the risk of a clot was high enough to treat while the investigation was arranged, and because the harm of waiting had begun to look larger than the harm of treating. The antibiotics were stopped. The left leg was measured and the measurement written down every morning, and the limb was kept comfortable, so that a swelling that grew would be seen by somebody other than the patient.

A computed tomography scan of the vessels in the chest was arranged the same day, with an ultrasound of the leg, and the ultrasound showed a clot in the deep veins of the calf, and the scan showed that clot sitting in the branches of the pulmonary artery. The extent was not the worst, there was no strain on the right side of the heart, and she was young and previously well, and that combination was the reason the outcome was as it was. Priya Raman wrote in the notes that the diagnosis should have been considered on the first day, and Douglas Kerr agreed and did not defend the decision, because the failure was not in the knowledge, which was available to everyone, but in the reading of it. The scan also settled the question the ward round had argued about.

Speaking to the family is part of this case and not a small part. Sophie Turner had been told she had a chest infection, had spent her first night on the ward in a bed she was going to be told she did not need, and had marked her books on her knees in the day room while a line was in her arm. The registrar sat down with her and her partner and said what had happened, in plain words: the breathing was not an infection, a clot had come up from her leg into her lung, the chest film had been clear and had been read the wrong way, and the note about her calf had been there from the beginning. People often describe this as the moment they lose trust in a service, and what restores it is not an apology but a specific account of what happened.

She recovered over weeks rather than days, which is the usual shape of it, and the anticoagulation was monitored with regular blood tests by the team that manages it. The swelling in her calf came down over several days with the leg elevated and the anticoagulation working, and the breathlessness settled, and she could walk to the end of the ward and back without the saturation falling. She was taught what to watch for, she was told explicitly what to do about the contraceptive, and she was told to say, to anybody who asked, that she had had a clot and to say it before anybody prescribed her anything. The first thing she was taught to watch for was the breathlessness returning, and the second was a swollen leg that would not go down.

Joanne Mercer was asked to write up what she had done, and what she wrote was not a list of nursing actions. It was an argument for a particular habit: the observation that changes the plan is the one written in a form that can be compared with tomorrow. A note that says the calf is swollen is a description. A note that says the left calf measured two centimeters larger than the right, at the same point, is a measurement, and the difference worth acting on is set by the current guideline and the local protocol rather than by a remembered number. A measurement can be repeated, and a repeated measurement can be trusted. She also wrote about the form, and about how a sheet that puts the description below a line keeps it below the line, and how the medicine that changed this case was found in a space designed for something else.

The service changed two things. The whole triage sheet is now read at the medical review, and if a description has been written below the line it is read out loud rather than left in the bottom third of the page. And every breathless patient now has both calves measured and the measurement written down, with a rule that any difference is reported rather than noticed. Both changes cost nothing, and both of them depend on somebody reading a piece of paper carefully.

The other lesson is about the pain itself, because the two conditions feel different and the difference can be heard in the words. The chest pain of an infection settles in over a day or two and is a deep, dull ache made worse by coughing, and it comes with a fever, a cough, and a change in the sound of the chest. The chest pain of a clot is sudden, sharp, one-sided and worse on taking a deep breath, and it is out of proportion to the general condition of the patient, who may look well and complain of one specific pain. Neither is reliable on its own, but the way a patient describes the pain is a piece of evidence, and it should be written down in her words rather than translated into a diagnosis at the bedside.

And then there is the chest radiograph, which deserves its own word because it is the reason this case went wrong. A chest radiograph is a result. It is a shadow on a film at one moment, taken by someone else and read by someone else, and it can be normal in a disease that has not yet made a shadow and normal in a disease that never will. It became a clear film, and the clear film was allowed to end the question rather than to narrow it. The reason it could end the question was not the film but the agreement that had formed around it, in which everybody had agreed that a chest infection was the right answer and the film would be made to look like it.

This failure was not anybody's carelessness, and it is worth saying that plainly, because the case is more useful for it. Every person in it was working hard and reasoning correctly inside a frame built by sensible people in a sensible order. Priya Raman had a breathless woman with pain on breathing in and a raised white cell count, pneumonia was the right first answer, and the anticoagulation she did not give on the first day would have been a serious error in a different patient. Joanne Mercer had written a good history and a good note and put them where the form asked her to put them, and the fault was in the form and in the reading of it. Any team would have built this frame, and any team could have made the correction, which is to treat the failure of treatment as a finding and to read the whole page.

There is a lesson for anyone who travels, and it is not a request to be frightened of aeroplanes. A long flight in a young woman who takes a combined oral contraceptive is not by itself a diagnosis of anything, and the overwhelming majority of the people who fly have nothing at all to worry about. What matters is that the combination is known, that it is written in the notes, and that anybody who sees a breathless traveller thinks about it for long enough to ask about the legs. Sophie Turner had no idea that her mother had died of a clot, which is the most human thing in this case, because she had been a child at the time.

One last thing about the nursing, because it is easy for a nurse in a story like this to become the person who noticed and not the person who reasoned. Joanne Mercer did not have a flash of inspiration at the bedside. She had a habit of taking a full history of the parts of a person a chest film cannot see, and a habit of writing what she measured instead of what she thought, and she had been mildly uncomfortable about a young woman who was not getting better, and she had not let the discomfort go. That last habit is the one to take from the case, because it is the one that most people lack.

To summarize, Sophie Turner was breathless with a sharp pain in the chest nine hours after a long flight, and she was treated for pneumonia for two days by people working hard inside a reasonable frame. The clue was on the triage sheet from the first ten minutes: a swollen calf on the same side as the pain, a mother who died of a clot, a new contraceptive, and a fast heart rate that the chest film could not account for. The nurse who found it had measured the calf and written the number down, and a measurement that can be repeated can be trusted. The lessons are that a clear radiograph is a result and not an answer, that the chest pain of a clot and the chest pain of an infection can be told apart by careful listening, and that the note nobody reads changes nothing until somebody reads it. Every threshold, score and measurement in this chapter is an illustration of reasoning, and each must be confirmed against the current guideline and the local protocol before it influences the care of a real patient. This book is educational material, it is not medical advice, and the clinician with the patient always outranks the page.

# Chapter 3 — The Asthma That Would Not Budge

The most ordinary sentence in respiratory medicine is that somebody's asthma is not controlled, and the most ordinary thing to do after hearing it is to prescribe more of the same. Rachel Okonkwo came to the respiratory clinic with that sentence already attached to her, and she had been carrying it since she was nine years old. She was twenty-seven, she was a nursery nurse, and by the time she sat down in the consultant's room she had already been told, more than once, that hers was a difficult asthma to control. What follows is a constructed composite rather than a record of any real person, no detail here identifies anyone living or dead, and the case has been assembled to make one particular piece of reasoning visible, because the reasoning is the point and the person is only the illustration.

She had come because of three things happening at once and none of them getting better. She was attending the clinic three or four times a week for breathlessness, she was reaching for her reliever inhaler four times in a day, and she was waking in the night with the sense that she could not get enough air. Three or four visits a week is the level at which most services would regard asthma as out of control, though the threshold that matters in your own clinic is the one written in the current guideline and the local protocol, and it has tightened considerably over the years. She had worked out her own theory, which was that this was simply what being an adult with asthma was like, and that the little ones at the nursery who wheezed through every playgroup had been lucky and would grow out of it. She had been managing on her own for most of her adult life, and she had never been given a plan she could find written down anywhere.

The referral letter was two lines long and it had been written the way referral letters are usually written, which is to say by someone who believed they already knew what the problem was. She was referred to the respiratory clinic as difficult asthma, and the box on the form for other conditions had been left empty, because there was nothing in the notes that made the general practitioner think of diabetes, of a hormone problem, of a heart that was not pumping properly, of a thyroid that had stopped doing its job, or of anything at all in the throat. The clinic that received her was a specialist asthma clinic, which meant that everyone in the building was expecting to see the same thing she had been told she had. A referral is a frame, and a frame that arrives before the patient does is already half an hour into the consultation.

Before any of that was discussed, the team had to hold two readings of the story at once, because one of them is comfortable and one of them is safe. The first is what the referral says, which is uncontrolled asthma, and uncontrolled asthma is one of the few respiratory diagnoses that can kill a young person, so it cannot be softened. The findings that would reveal that danger are a peak expiratory flow well below the patient's own best, attacks that do not settle, breathlessness that is present at rest, and waking at night with the conviction that this time something bad is happening. The second reading is the one nobody had written down, which is that the breathlessness may not be coming from the lungs at all but from the throat, and the findings that would reveal it are a high noise on the way the breath goes in as well as on the way out, a voice that is hoarse between attacks, and attacks that stop as suddenly as they began.

Dr Helen Marsh, the respiratory consultant who saw her, heard the story with the unfamiliar parts held slightly apart from the familiar ones. She was careful to say out loud what everyone in the room was privately thinking, which was that some people's breathing is worse at the level of the throat than it is in the chest, and that this condition is often mistaken for asthma and often exists alongside it. That was where the thought went, into a folder marked for later, and the folder was not opened again for six weeks. The working diagnosis on the day was poorly controlled asthma, and the reasoning behind it was audible and reasonable, because this is a woman with a lifelong diagnosis, a great many symptoms, and nothing unusual on examination today.

Nothing about her examination that day was dangerous, and every one of those findings was also what you would expect in a well woman between attacks. She was not febrile, her chest was clear to listen to, she was not distressed, and she was not cyanosed. Her pulse was regular and her saturation in the room was 96 per cent, and the number at which a saturation of this kind should begin to worry you is a matter for the current guideline and the local protocol in your own service rather than for anybody's recollection. The consultant wrote in the notes that the examination was unremarkable, which was true, and then wrote that the problem was poor control, which was an interpretation, and the notes did not distinguish between the two because notes rarely do.

What the file showed after eighteen years of asthma was a folder of letters and a box of inhalers, and it contained no measurements at all. Rachel had never had her breathing measured by a device in her life; she had been diagnosed as a child in a way that nobody now could reconstruct, and every decision about her treatment since had been made by asking how she had been and then believing the answer. She had never had a chest radiograph as an adult, her airways had never been tested to see whether they narrow when they are made to narrow, and nobody had ever asked her what her best reading on a peak flow meter had been. Her record consisted of the diagnosis, the inhalers, the number of visits, and the word asthma, repeated with great consistency and for a very long time, and the absence of anything else had become as hard to see as the diagnosis itself.

Two more things were said in that first consultation and neither of them was pursued. Rachel's voice was hoarse, and the consultant attributed it to a cold she had had the week before, and the nurse wrote in the notes that the voice sounded hoarse. And Rachel said, almost as an aside while she was putting her coat on, that she had stopped going to the choir she had sung in for six years because she could not hold a note any more, and that singing was one of the things that set her off, along with talking and coughing. What she could do was walk to the shop and carry the shopping home without trouble, and it was talking and singing that took her breath away. It was all in the record from the first hour, and it was in the record because a nurse asked her how the attacks started and how they ended.

The third thing she gave was the one that should have stopped the clock. She said that the attacks came on suddenly and went just as suddenly, often before she had found her inhaler, and that a great many of them never needed anything at all. She said, with the particular embarrassment of a young woman who has told nobody, that during an attack she has an overwhelming urge to pass urine. Neither of those is a feature of asthma, because asthma does not switch on and off like that and does not cause an urgent need to pass urine. The team noted them, and the notes read, in the flat way that notes read, that the patient describes a variable pattern with urinary urgency, and then the team went on to the treatment.

What else could produce breathlessness, a noise on breathing out, and waking at night in a young woman with a long-standing label of asthma? The list was built and ranked twice, once by how likely each thing was and once by how much damage it would do if it were true and missed. The likely candidates were the asthma itself, badly controlled; a breathing pattern disorder of the kind that comes with panic, which she did not have the features of; and the narrowing of the upper airway that everyone had mentioned and then set aside. The dangerous candidates were the ones that are ruinous when present, among them a swallowed object sitting in a bronchus, a collapsed lung, a heart that was failing without saying so, and an infection in the lung arriving quietly for weeks. The asthma was the most likely by a long way and the laryngeal problem was the one everybody could say the name of and no one could explain.

The treatment that started that day was entirely correct for the diagnosis they were working with, and it was started for reasons that were sensible. The inhaled corticosteroid was stepped up, a spacer was provided, a written technique check was put in place with a date beside every item on it, and she was asked to keep a peak flow diary, twice a day, before anything else. The reason for checking technique before adding more medicine was the honest one, that a corticosteroid which is being inhaled badly is not a treatment at all, and it is kinder and cheaper to discover that before adding anything further. She was asked to come back in six weeks, and how long to allow before a response can reasonably be judged is set out in the current guideline and the local protocol, which differ between services and between editions.

Carla Mendes was the respiratory nurse who would spend the next six weeks with this patient, and the nursing in this case is not background to the medicine, it is where the case is actually decided. Carla did the technique teaching at the bedside, which in this clinic means sitting down with the patient, watching a dose go into the spacer, and then watching a second one, because a person can perform perfectly for a clinician and badly alone when nobody is watching. She checked the spacer and the mouthpiece, the rinsing afterwards, and the order in which the two inhalers were taken. She explained what the numbers on the diary sheet meant and how to record them, and she asked the question she would go on asking again and again, which was how does an attack start and how does it end.

The answer the first time was the answer most people give. It comes on, it goes on, you use your inhaler, it settles. Carla wrote that down and moved on, because there was no reason to press on it. She also checked at each contact whether the treatment was actually being taken, and she did it without suspicion, by asking what a day looked like, and what came back was a nursery nurse with two small children, an awkward shift pattern, and a diary filled in every single day without a gap. The inhaler in Rachel's bag was empty more often than any treatment schedule could account for, and Carla found that encouraging rather than worrying, because an empty canister is the most visible proof of adherence there is.

Six weeks of perfect technique and perfect adherence changed nothing, and that sentence is the one the whole case turns on, so it is worth stating plainly rather than telling this as a story of gradual deterioration. The diary was complete. The technique was checked and re-checked and checked again, and it was good every single time. The frequency of the visits, the waking in the night and the use of the reliever did not improve, and the reliever was being used as often in the sixth week as in the first. Carla was left holding an empty inhaler box and a patient who had done everything that had been asked of her, and there is no comfortable place to put that.

The failure was felt by everyone, and it was felt in a particular way, because nothing had gone wrong in the doing of it. The consultant's first feeling was that the treatment had not had long enough, and the second was that the next step was a long-acting bronchodilator and then a tablet that worked in a different way, and the third, which she was honest enough to say aloud, was that some people simply had difficult asthma and were difficult. The nurse's first feeling was that the diary must be wrong, because the story and the numbers did not sit together, and her second was the one she disliked most, which was that a young woman who filled in a peak flow diary with this much care might be filling it in with what she hoped was true. The third feeling, which arrived later and which nobody acted on, was that the treatment might be working perfectly and the disease might not be what they thought.

There was one set of numbers in the folder that did not fit, and the fit was visible on the face of the diary. Her best reading had been recorded once, on the first day she used the meter, and the readings over the following six weeks sat between 380 and 420 liters per minute, and they moved very little from day to day, including on the days when she had written that she had had an attack. Which numbers count as low, and how much day-to-day variation is expected in a patient whose asthma is genuinely uncontrolled, is written in the current guideline and the local protocol rather than in anybody's memory, and the personal best remains the more useful guide in most services. What the diary showed was a chest that was not behaving the way an uncontrolled asthmatic chest behaves, and nobody asked what it was behaving like instead.

What turned the case was not in the diary and not in the numbers, it was a sound. Carla had heard it many times without naming it, on the telephone when Rachel rang to say she was having an attack, in the corridor outside the clinic, and in the room itself. She had written it in the notes every time, in the words she had been given, as wheeze, because wheeze is what this patient had. On the telephone she heard it more clearly than anyone else could, because a microphone held against a mouth filters nothing at all. What she heard was a high squeak when the breath came in as well as when it went out, and she had been hearing the outgoing half of it for eighteen years and had never once questioned the incoming half.

At the six-week review Carla said it out loud, which is the hardest thing in medicine and the most useful. She said that the noise was arriving on the way in and not only on the way out, and that a sound which arrives with the breath going in is not made in the lungs. The consultant stopped. Then Carla asked the question she had been asking for six weeks, about how the attacks start and how they end, but this time she asked for the sound rather than for the story, and Rachel, who had given the same answer before, sat up and said that the noise came on at the start of an attack and stopped completely when it was over, and that her chest was clear inside a minute or two. A noise that begins and ends with the episode, and is not made in the lungs, is made at the larynx, and the larynx does not tighten in asthma.

So the clues were these, and every one of them was in the file before the review. The hoarse voice between attacks had been written down and explained away as a cold. The inability to sing had been offered as a passing complaint and never followed. The attacks that settled in minutes without any inhaler had been read as a fast response to treatment, which was the opposite of what they were. The urge to pass urine during an attack had been recorded as an odd symptom and never asked about again. The noise on the way in had been heard by a nurse on the telephone and written down as wheeze. And there had never been an objective measurement of this patient's asthma in eighteen years, and that absence had been read as reassurance rather than as a gap.

The reason all of that was missed is not that it was subtle, and it is not that any of it was invisible at the time. It is that a name, once it has been attached to a person for eighteen years, stops being a description and becomes the explanation. From the first visit every observation was read through asthma, so the hoarse voice was a cold, the noise was a wheeze, the fast settling was a good response, and the missing measurements were there because she was stable. A diagnosis in that position is self-confirming, and the more years a label has been carried the harder it becomes to see past it, because each year adds a little more apparent evidence that it is right.

The nurse was not outside this, and it is worth saying so plainly. Carla had listened with a filter on, the filter being the expectation that this patient wheezed, and a filter applied to the ear works exactly as one applied to the eye works. She had also asked about the attacks in the language of the diagnosis, which is to say she had asked how often they were happening, and frequency is the wrong question when the point is what is making them happen. What she had not done was ask what the noise was, or take the patient's own account of her voice seriously, or notice that a woman who cannot sing but can carry her shopping has a problem that is not in her lungs. Any team with a nurse who asked that question would have found it, and the correction was available a long time before the review.

The next step was to stop reasoning from the notes and go and measure. The first test was breathing tests with a flow volume loop, which records the shape of the breath as it comes in and as it goes out rather than producing a single number, and the shape was not the shape of asthma, because the limb of the curve showing breathing in was flattened and the airway was narrowing on the way in. The second was a laryngoscopy, in which a small camera is passed to the back of the tongue so that the voice box can be watched while the patient is having an attack, and it showed the vocal cords closing when they should have stayed open, which is what makes the squeak and the sense of being strangled. The third was a challenge test of the airways, which asks whether they narrow when they are made to narrow, and in her case they did, and that is how the team established that the asthma was real.

She had two conditions and not one, and they had been sharing a symptom and a name for eighteen years. She had genuine asthma, with airflow that narrowed and then widened, and she needed to keep taking her inhaled corticosteroid for it. And she had inducible laryngeal obstruction, in which the voice box closes when it should stay open, and which had been counted as asthma attacks for eighteen years and treated with escalating amounts of a drug that could never have helped it. This is one of the commonest and most instructive patterns in chronic respiratory disease, which is two conditions, one complaint, one name, and a treatment that only ever reaches one of them.

The change in treatment had two halves and only one of them was a prescription. The inhaler treatment for the asthma continued unchanged, because that asthma was real and the team had measured it, and stepping it down would have been as wrong as stepping it up. The larger change was breathing and speech therapy, which for this condition is the treatment that makes the difference, and what it involves is teaching a person to recognise the early tightening, to breathe out rather than in, to use the voice differently, and to stop the episode becoming a fight. It works because the closing of the voice box is driven by the effort of breathing hard against a narrowing, and the skills change the shape of that effort. It is not a talking cure and it is not quick, and the improvement usually shows in weeks rather than days.

Carla's role changed as well, and this is the part of the story the clinic kept afterwards. She became the person who listened to the sound rather than the person who recorded it, and she taught Rachel to describe an attack in a fixed set of words, so that what happened could be compared with what happened last time. She kept checking the technique, because that was still worth doing. She watched the voice, which had been a minor line in the notes and became the most useful observation in the file, and she telephoned the consultant when it was worse rather than waiting for the appointment, because a hoarse voice on the day of a review is a finding. And she began to ask the question about how the attacks start and how they end as a routine for every patient with a breathing problem, so that the next one did not have to be unlucky.

Rachel improved, and the improvement was not subtle. The attacks came on less often and, more importantly, they stopped themselves, because she learned the early signs and used the breathing skills and the squeak resolved in a couple of minutes instead of lasting the rest of the evening. She went back to the choir, which after some years is a considerable thing to say about a case. She was not woken at night by the end of the second month of therapy. She still had asthma and still needed her inhaled corticosteroid, and she was not troubled by that, because she had been told what it was for and what it was not for.

There are three lessons here and they are related to one another. The first is that two conditions can share one symptom and one name, and that when they do the second one stays invisible for exactly as long as the first one is confident. The second is that the absence of a response to correct treatment is itself a finding, and not a failure of the treatment or of the patient, and it deserves the same seriousness as a result that comes back unexpected. The third is that the sound of breathing is worth listening to, that a nurse is often the only person in the building who hears it, and that a noise which arrives with the breath going in is not a noise the lungs make.

To summarize, Rachel Okonkwo's asthma was real and so was the disorder that had been counted as asthma for eighteen years, and the mistake was not a mistake of treatment but a mistake of naming. The nurse heard the decisive thing with her own ears, which was that the noise of an attack arrived on the way in as well as on the way out, and she heard it properly only once she stopped hearing it as asthma. Correct treatment that produces no response is itself a finding, and the correct answer to it is to reopen the question rather than to escalate the therapy. The management that changed her life was breathing and speech therapy alongside continued treatment of the asthma that was real, and she was able to sing again within a few months. This case is a constructed composite and describes no real patient, and it is educational material rather than medical advice, a protocol, or a substitute for the judgment of the clinician who is with the patient. Every number quoted here is an illustration of reasoning and must be confirmed against the current guideline and the local protocol before it informs any real decision.

# Chapter 4 — The Hot Ankle

The ankle told Denise Ryan before the man did. It came into the triage room at the end of a raised leg, held the way a leg is held when it hurts to have it touched, and the skin over it was red and shining and stretched so tightly that it caught the light along the top of the foot. I cannot put my heel on the floor, the man said, and he said it as though reporting something he had been asked to report. Barry Heslin was sixty-two years old, he had worked on the docks until six years earlier when his knees had decided to stop, and he had driven himself to the emergency department because his wife was at an appointment of her own and he had not wanted to wait for a lift.

The story took five minutes and it had a shape. Three days earlier, without any injury he could remember, the ankle had begun to ache in the evening after a day on his feet. By the second day it was hot and swollen enough that his sock had left a red band around it, and by the third day he had not slept for more than an hour at a time. He had taken the anti-inflammatory that a neighbour had given him and had not been much the better for it. He had soaked the foot twice a day in water as hot as he could stand, with salt in it, because that was what his mother had done to his father's feet, and he had walked on it as much as he could because he had been told that if you keep a joint still it will set like cement.

Two things about him did not fit the story everyone was about to tell. He had never had gout, not once in his life, and he had never had a kidney stone either, which the history took and put down because it did not fit anything anybody was thinking of. He had had type 2 diabetes for some years, managed with tablets and one injection, and he had been due for a review of it for eleven months and had not gone, because the tablets worked well enough and because a man who has felt well for a year does not think of himself as unwell. His wife had mentioned twice that his feet looked wrong, and he had told her that his feet had always looked wrong.

Denise Ryan took the observations herself, and they are the part of this story that mattered most. His temperature was 38.9 degrees Celsius, and the threshold that counts as a fever is set in the current guideline and the local protocol for your own service, and it is not the same number in every place. His pulse was 112 beats per minute, and the rate at which a pulse should concern you is a matter for the current guideline and the local protocol, and it shifts with age and with whatever illness is behind it. His blood pressure was 104 over 68 millimeters of mercury, and a blood pressure means nothing at all except against the reading your own service works to, which is set in the current guideline and the local protocol. His respiratory rate was 24 per minute, which is the number at which most services begin to watch closely, and the threshold for watching is written in the current guideline and the local protocol.

A hot joint in a man of sixty-two has a short list of causes, and one of them is an emergency measured in hours rather than days, which is an infection inside the joint. The findings that reveal it are the ones this man already had, and they are easy to write down and easy to file under something more comfortable: a temperature above the level your service calls a fever, a pulse faster than the pain alone would explain, and a joint that hurts more when it is moved than when it is left alone. A hot joint is a joint that needs a needle in it, and the finding that says so is the fluid, which nobody had looked at. Nothing in the history ruled it out.

This is a constructed composite rather than a record of any real patient, and no detail of it identifies anyone living or dead, and the case has been put together to make one particular piece of reasoning visible. What follows is what the team did, what they felt while they were doing it, and what was written down at the time. The reason it is worth telling is that the mistake here was not an unusual one, and the evidence that would have prevented it was sitting in the notes from the first hour. It is written to be argued with rather than obeyed.

Dr Aiden Fowler, the registrar on that shift, took the history again with the nurse sitting in, and then they built the list properly, which is what a team should do. Ranked by likelihood came gout, because the story fitted it almost too well: one joint, hot and red and swollen, a beginning over a day or two, no injury to explain it, a middle-aged man, a weekend of shellfish and more red wine than he usually drinks, and a joint that had gone from nothing to agony in the space of two days. Nothing about the presentation was wrong for gout, and a presentation that is entirely correct for a disease still has to be weighed against the cost of being wrong about it. Alongside gout on that list sat cellulitis of the foot, a fracture nobody had seen, and a hot joint of another cause.

Ranked by danger the list was different, and infection in the joint was second on it. Fowler said the dangerous possibility out loud in front of Denise, because that is the correct habit and because he had been burned once before by a man he had sent home with a hot ankle. The findings that would have supported it were all present: the fever, the fast pulse, the diabetes, and the whole foot being involved rather than the joint alone, because infection in the soft tissues of a foot that cannot feel itself does not stay tidily inside one joint. He wrote that down in the notes. He then wrote that a first attack of gout in a man of sixty-two with a dietary trigger was the most likely diagnosis, and the second line of the note absorbed the first. He also wrote down the observation that would have separated the two, which was to move the ankle and to see where the pain was.

The treatment began the way the treatment for gout begins. An anti-inflammatory, and rest, and ice, and the leg raised above the level of the heart, and a review in a couple of days with a safety net if things got worse. The reasoning was audible and reasonable on the evidence as it stood: if this is gout, then the anti-inflammatory will take the inflammation down, the joint will settle inside a day or two, and the swelling will go with it, and if it does not, then the diagnosis is wrong. There was a nursing plan as well, and it was a good one, with observations, a temperature chart, and a measurement of the limb at the same point on the leg every day.

What the nurse did in the first hour is where this case is decided, and it is worth describing in the order she did it. She marked a point on the skin of each leg just above the ankle with a pen, so that the measurement would be taken in the same place by whoever came on next, and she wrote the first measurements down herself: thirty-three centimeters on the affected side and thirty on the other. How a change in a limb measurement should be judged is set out in the current guideline and the local protocol, and in most services the direction of the change matters more than the number itself. Then she asked him the question that nobody had asked him, which was what have you been doing to your foot.

The answer was that he had been doing what people do, which is a great deal. He had soaked the foot twice a day in water as hot as he could stand, with salt in it. He had been taking the anti-inflammatory from the neighbour. His wife had been rubbing a cream from the pharmacy into the ankle twice a day.
