He had been walking on it constantly, because he had been told that if you keep a joint still it will set like cement, and because there was a job he had to do in the garden. Denise wrote all of it down, and what she had not yet understood was what she was looking at: the skin was taut and shining because it had been stretched, because the water had been hot enough to scald, and because he could not feel it being hot.

She understood it twenty minutes later, while she was looking at the sole of the foot with a good light. The skin had been scalded by water that he had not known was scalding, because the feeling in his feet had gone years ago. Years of diabetes had taken the feeling out of the bottom of his feet, which is one reason a person with diabetes can walk on a broken foot without stopping, and another reason a hot foot in such a man is far more serious than it looks. She wrote that down as well. Both observations were in the notes for the rest of the day, and both were read as evidence of a severe attack of gout rather than of a foot burned by a man who could not feel it happen.

The night shift took the observations four times, and the numbers did not do what the treatment had promised. The temperature did not come down. The measurement of the leg went up by two centimeters, and the skin at the top of the foot became more tense, and at two in the morning he asked for more pain relief and said that he could feel his heart beating in the ankle. Denise came in and found him sitting on the edge of the bed with the leg held out again, in exactly the position she had first seen him in, and she thought that he did not look any better, and she went back to the chart and looked at the numbers instead of at the man.

By the second morning he was worse, and the failure was visible in the observations rather than in the complaint. His temperature was higher. The swelling had crossed the ankle and was now up the lower leg, and it was not the shape of a joint any more but the shape of a limb. He could not bear weight on the foot at all, and when she took the ankle between her hands and moved it, even a little, he shouted, which is not what a man with gout does when his ankle is moved. He had not slept. He asked, in the tone of a man apologising for the inconvenience, whether this was normal for gout.

Then Denise asked him to turn the foot over. She wanted to look at the sole, because that is what you do with a foot, and because she had asked him about the garden and he had said that he had been out there barefoot at the weekend. On the heel of the foot there was a small puncture no longer than a nail head, with a red rim around it and a tender spot in the middle, and the skin around it was the same red as the rest of the foot. He looked at it and said that yes, he had stood on a thorn, and that he had forgotten about it, and that he had not looked at the sole of his foot in three days because there was no reason to.

The failure was felt by everyone in that room, and it was felt in a particular way, because the treatment had been reasonable and the notes were tidy and nobody had done anything careless. Fowler's first thought was that the man had ruined it by walking on the foot, and his second was that the anti-inflammatory had not had long enough, and his third was the one he was least proud of, which was that this was what gout looked like when it was severe, and people did get very unwell with it. He wrote that the foot was to be raised, the anti-inflammatory continued, and the patient reviewed in forty-eight hours. Denise's first thought was that something in the chart was not adding up, and her second was that she had been reading the chart instead of the man.

She sat down on the end of the bed and did the thing that nurses do and doctors do not always do, which was to put the observations in a row and read across them. A man with diabetes and a hot foot and a temperature of 38.9 degrees Celsius and a pulse of 112 beats per minute and a foot that is hot from the toes to the calf and a joint that hurts more when it is moved than when it is left alone and a hole in the sole of the foot. That is not gout. Gout is a loud and dramatic inflammation of one joint, and it has no portal of entry, and it does not make a man look like that. This is an infection, and it has been sitting in that foot for three days while everybody was looking at the ankle.

So the clues were these, and every one of them was in the notes on the first day. The temperature of 38.9 degrees Celsius was written down as a fever caused by the pain of the attack, and pain does raise a temperature a little, which is exactly why it was such a good explanation. The fast pulse was the pain. The swelling of the whole foot was the inflammation of a gouty ankle spreading into the soft tissues. The exquisite pain on the slightest passive movement was what a gouty ankle does. The inability to bear weight was what a gouty ankle does. The diabetes was in the notes as a background condition, in the third line, in the way that diabetes is always in a set of notes.

And the hole in the sole of the foot was in the notes too, because nobody had looked for it. The reason all of this was missed is not that any of it was hidden. It is that the diagnosis was chosen from the story and then the observations were read through it, so that anything which fitted gout counted as evidence for gout and anything which did not was explained away, one at a time, with a reason that was individually reasonable. The frame was a man with a hot ankle. The frame that should have been in place was a man who is systemically unwell, and that frame was never adopted, because nobody ever wrote down what would have made the team wrong.

What happened next was fast, and it was fast because of the nurse. The joint was aspirated that morning, and the fluid was sent for culture and examined in the laboratory, and antibiotics were begun. The orthopaedic team was called, and the registrar rang the consultant to say, in words that were unusual for him, that he was sending a man to theatre rather than sending him home. The nurse practitioner took the blood the team wanted, cleaned and dressed the foot, and then did the thing that kept the case safe while the arrangements were made, which was to keep him still, keep the leg raised, and keep telling him what was happening.

The interval between the first day and the moment of the operation was short, and it was the interval that mattered. An infected joint in a foot that belongs to a man with diabetes is not a condition that waits for a clinic appointment or for a week of anti-inflammatory tablets; the window in which the joint can be drained and saved is small, and it has narrowed as the surgery has become more reliable. How long that window is in your own service is written in the current guideline and the local protocol, and it is the kind of number to check before deciding that a hot ankle can look again on Monday. There is no way of making that window longer by waiting.

At the operation the joint was opened and washed out, because infection in a joint of a diabetic foot does not settle on antibiotics alone, and the fluid that came out was cloudy with cells in it rather than the clear fluid of a gouty joint. The surgeon found the track of the infection running up through the soft tissues from the heel to the joint, and the hole in the sole was the way in. The culture grew the organism, and the organism was one that lives on skin and in soil, which was the last piece of the story and the piece that fitted the thorn in the garden. The material removed was sent on too, so that the laboratory could name what they were dealing with.

He recovered, and the recovery was slow in the way that recoveries are slow after a joint has been opened and washed. The fever settled within a day or two of the operation and never came back. The swelling went down over the following weeks, and the ankle became a joint again rather than a balloon. The hole in the sole healed, and it was the one that took longest, because the skin of the sole of a foot that cannot feel its own heat takes its own time about anything. He walked out on crutches in the end, and he said that walking back through the dock gate without thinking about his feet was the strangest pleasure he had had in years.

Then physiotherapy, and the slow business of teaching a foot to take weight again, and the flat-footed shuffle that a person who has been off a leg for six weeks learns to walk with. He was seen again in the clinic at the end of that period, walking without crutches and without limping. The ankle kept a small restriction of movement that would improve over the following months. His diabetes was reviewed properly in the aftermath, because a foot that got infected in the first place is a foot that belongs to a man whose sugar was running high, and his blood glucose was 13 millimoles per liter when he came in, which is a number to be read against the target set in the current guideline and the local protocol for a man of his age rather than against a number remembered from an old clinic letter.

What Denise Ryan watches for now, and teaches, is written into the discharge teaching and into the note that went back to his general practice. A man with diabetes should have his feet looked at, not occasionally but routinely, by somebody who is looking for the skin and not only for the pulses. He should never soak a foot in hot water, because he cannot tell how hot it is, and the scalding that produced the shiny red skin of this foot would have happened again. He should never walk barefoot in a garden or on a beach, and he should be taught to look at the soles of his own feet with a mirror once a week. And he was told again, and told again, that a foot which becomes hot, red or swollen is a reason to be seen the same day.

The general practice received a note saying that the man who had presented with what was assumed to be gout had in fact had an infected joint, and asking whether his feet were examined at his reviews, and the answer was that they were not, because nobody examines a foot that is not the complaint. The emergency department changed its own practice in a small way and a useful way: every hot joint now has its temperature, its pulse and the presence or absence of pain on passive movement recorded in the notes, so that the next person to read the file can see the pattern without having to reconstruct it. The review of his diabetes was arranged at the same time, with a foot check that happens whether or not the foot complains. One unlucky attendance had become a list of things that would be done differently.

There are three lessons here, and the first of them is short enough to be a rule of thumb. Gout does not make a person systemically unwell. A man with a hot ankle and a temperature of 38.9 degrees Celsius and a pulse of 112 beats per minute does not have gout until somebody has proved that he does, and the way to prove it is a needle in the joint and the fluid in the laboratory. The second lesson is that a hot joint is a joint that needs a needle in it, and that the needle is not an escalation of the treatment but part of the assessment.

The third lesson is that the diabetes is the reason it went wrong, not once but three times over, because diabetes is why the foot could be scalded without him feeling it, why a small puncture could become an infection unnoticed, and why an infection in that foot was far more dangerous than in a man whose feeling and blood supply were intact. Underneath those three there is a fourth thing, which is that the dangerous alternative was named on the first day, in the notes, and then never revisited, and a team that has written down what would make it wrong has already done most of the work of being safe. It also decides how long the question can be left open, because a man with diabetes and a hot joint cannot be sent home with a promise to come back if it gets worse. Diabetes is also why the injuries of a hot foot are silent and the signs of a serious one are easy to explain away.

Denise Ryan was left with a lesson that had nothing to do with the surgery and everything to do with twenty minutes spent with a man and a bowl of water. She had asked him what he was doing to his foot, and she had written down what he said, and she had then read what he said as evidence of a severe attack of gout rather than as evidence of a man with no feeling in his foot trying to treat himself with hot water. The habit she took from it was to read what a patient does to a limb as a symptom in its own right, because the things that people do to a painful foot are often more informative than the things that can be measured from it. She had noticed too that nobody had examined the sole of the foot, and that the reason was assumption rather than oversight. A nurse who asks the question nobody asked can be the reason a diagnosis is made in time to act on it.

To summarize, Barry Heslin's ankle was not gout, and the reason was written in the nursing observations from the first hour, in a fever, a fast pulse, a whole foot rather than one joint, and a small hole in the sole of a foot that could not feel a thorn. The nurse practitioner who watched the treatment fail found the decisive observation and put the findings in a row, and what she read across them was an infection in a joint rather than a crystal disease. The approach changed within hours, the joint was aspirated and drained, antibiotics and surgery followed, and the leg was saved with physiotherapy afterwards. The lessons are that gout does not make a person systemically unwell, that a hot joint is a joint that needs a needle in it, and that the diabetes was the reason this went wrong rather than an incidental detail of the history. This case is a constructed composite and describes no real patient, and it is educational material rather than medical advice or a substitute for the judgment of the clinician who is with the patient. Every figure given here illustrates a way of thinking, and each must be checked against the current guideline and the local protocol before it is allowed to shape a real decision.

# Chapter 5 — The Stone That Was Not a Stone

Alan Whitfield arrived at the emergency department at nine in the evening with pain in the upper abdomen that had started eight hours earlier and had never stopped. He had been vomiting for most of that time, and he was 51 and supervised a distribution warehouse. He had driven himself in, because his wife was on an evening shift. What he said about the pain matters more than anything else in this chapter, so it is worth hearing. He said the pain was not in one place, that it went through to his back, and that it did not come and go. Then he was sick into a bowl, and the registrar stopped writing and let him finish.

This is a constructed composite, and none of it describes a real person; the details are assembled from the way these presentations arrive, and real patients are always messier than any illustration. The department was busy that evening, with the night team still in handover and the formal ward round not yet begun. How many hours a pain has lasted, and how old the patient is, both feed the decision about waiting or acting. The figures that count as urgent differ between conditions and between services, so confirm them against the current guideline and the local protocol.

Hannah Ferris was the senior sister on the resuscitation side, and she took the history and the observations herself. She asked him to put his own finger on the pain, and he laid his hand flat over the upper middle of his abdomen and said that it went back towards his spine. She asked whether it came in waves, and he said no, that it had been steady from the start, and that leaning forward helped it while lying flat made it worse. She asked about his urine, and he said he had passed a small amount in the morning and nothing since. She wrote it in her own words, and she asked the questions that the form did not have.

Dr Marcus Aldridge was the emergency department registrar, and he examined Alan Whitfield with the curtains drawn. He palpated lightly before deeply, so that he knew how much the patient objected to. There was tenderness in the upper abdomen and a little guarding, but the abdomen was not rigid and had no rebound tenderness. He looked for the hernia, the old scar and the groin, and found none. He put a hand under the loin and asked whether it hurt when he pressed there. It did not, and that was the moment the stone became a little less likely, and nobody said so.

The list he made was a stone, a perforated peptic ulcer, pancreatitis and a bowel obstruction, ranked by how often each came through the door in this shape. A perforated ulcer was the dangerous one, and he said what would have shown it: a rigid, board-like abdomen, an onset that was sudden rather than drifting, and free air under the diaphragm on the film already taken. A stone was the most likely by a long way, because it is the commonest cause of severe pain with vomiting in a man of this age. Pancreatitis sat in the same list, and the finding that would have separated it from a stone was already written in the nursing notes. It was read as radiation, because radiation is what stones do.

The treatment was chosen quickly, and it was not wrong on the evidence available at that hour. He was given analgesia strong enough to control the pain, an antiemetic, and intravenous fluid for the vomiting and the dehydration. A request was put in for a kidney ultrasound that would confirm the stone and show whether it was obstructing. Mr Julian Ashworth, the surgeon on call, was telephoned for advice, because the registrar wanted a surgical opinion on the abdomen. He agreed with the plan, and the ultrasound went on the morning list.

What Hannah Ferris did next is the other half of the treatment, and she did it without being asked, which is why it worked at all. She started a strict fluid balance chart, so that every litre that went in and every millilitre that came out was written down with a time against it. She measured the urine from a bottle and recorded the volume every hour, because a man who is vomiting and is being given fluid needs to know whether his kidneys are working. She also set the pain score going, at rest and on movement, because pain that is being treated needs to be judged rather than described.

In the first hour the entries on her chart began a pattern she could not yet describe but could see. What came out was small amounts of vomit, and the urine she measured went onto the chart at the end of his bed and not onto the observations the team would read in the morning. Alan Whitfield sat up on the trolley with his arms folded over the edge and said that this was the position that made it easiest. He stayed like that most of the night without being asked to move, and when she asked whether he had tried other positions he said sitting forward was the only thing that helped.

Dr Aldridge came back at twenty past eleven and found what he expected: a man still in pain. He checked the observations and the abdomen, found both unchanged, and wrote that the patient was stable. The interesting part is not that he was wrong but that the diagnosis had become a frame rather than a hypothesis. Once a stone had been chosen, every observation that arrived was read as a stone. The vomiting was what stones make people do, and the failure to change was read as a large stone, or a stoic patient, or insufficient analgesia. What nobody did was ask whether a stone would explain a man who had passed almost no urine.

The ultrasound was still on the morning list, and when the registrar considered bringing it earlier he found the request had gone in as routine, and that moving it would mean waking the imaging staff. He decided against it because the patient was stable, which was reasonable, and it is the kind of decision a busy night produces without anyone noticing a decision has been taken. The handover between the evening team and the night team was where the working diagnosis became fact, because what compresses best is a simple noun. Stone travelled from one pair of hands to the next until it was no longer a possibility but a thing happening to a man on a trolley.

The observations through the night were unremarkable, and Hannah Ferris wrote them on the hour without drama: the pulse regular, the blood pressure steady, the temperature not raised, the breathing normal, and the oxygen saturation good on air. His pain score sat high and did not move, and the score on movement was worse than the score at rest, and she recorded both each time. He was sick at eleven and again in the small hours, and each time she recorded the volume and the colour and settled him back into his position. How often a patient is observed, and how long a deterioration takes to show, differ between wards, so check the frequency and the interval against the current guideline and the local protocol.

The hourly urine volumes were the other thing she wrote down: small, and staying small, hour after hour, while the fluid going in did not slow. By the small hours the arithmetic on the chart was stark, because the column for what went in was full and the column for what came out was nearly empty. She did not think of it as a discovery but as a discrepancy, of the kind explained by a patient drinking less than he says or a drip running slow, and she checked both. Neither was true. He was simply passing very little urine, and the chart said so, in her handwriting, with nobody reading it.

By three in the morning the night team had settled into the fact that this was a stone, and the settling was almost physical. The handover had said stone, the ward round would say stone, and the man was as comfortable as a man with a stone could be. The temptation at that hour was not to be clever but to be comfortable: to increase the analgesia so the night would pass, to accept the vomiting as a stubborn feature of a stone, and to treat the chart as a nuisance rather than a finding. The intervals at which a patient who has not improved is reassessed, and at which a scan is brought forward, are local decisions, and should be confirmed against the current guideline and the local protocol rather than assumed.

At about half past four the registrar came back, because Alan Whitfield had been sick again, and this time he stayed longer. The pain score was unchanged, the vomiting had continued, the abdomen was as it had been, and the observations were still unremarkable. He said that the analgesia needed increasing and that the scan needed to be brought forward. Hannah Ferris said that it had been in the notes all night that he had passed almost no urine, and she said it while holding the chart, without any edge in her voice. She did not say that she had been waiting hours for somebody to look, because a colleague who has kept a record for eight hours has more claim to be heard than one who has just arrived.

He took the chart and read it from the top down. At the bottom, in her handwriting, was the total, and the total was a fraction of what had gone in. He asked how long the chart had been running, and she said since before his drip was attached. He asked who had asked her to start it, and she said nobody had. Then he said the thing that changed the case, and he said it to her. A stone blocking one kidney should not stop the other kidney from working, he said, and whatever this man had, it was not simply a stone.

A stone that obstructs produces colic, because urine cannot pass, but only the kidney on that side is affected. A urine volume that has collapsed while fluid is going in freely is not the picture of one obstructed ureter. It is the picture of a body that is not distributing the fluid it is being given, which points to the circulation, or to an organ that has swollen and blocked itself, and both are graver than a stone. The nurse had been writing numbers onto a chart for hours without interpreting them, and the registrar had been reading a story for hours without checking a number. Neither of them had touched the same evidence.

Then they went back to the bed, and Hannah Ferris took him through the movements again, slowly, because watching an answer is worth more than writing one down. She asked him to point to the pain again, and he put his hand over the upper middle of his abdomen and said it went through to his back. Then she asked the question nobody had asked: why had he not moved about, when everybody with a stone is up and down the ward unable to settle? He said that this pain was like being held, and that holding still was the only thing that helped.

The examination that followed undid the diagnosis. There was no tenderness over the loin when the kidney was pressed from behind, which in a stone large enough to cause this much vomiting is unusual. The urine dipstick showed no blood, and an obstructed stone causing pain this severe would usually be bleeding somewhere in the system. The pain was not coming in waves but was steady at the level of the upper abdomen. The abdomen was not rigid and there was no temperature. Each of these had been available from the first hour and each had been read as a stone, because the stone had been chosen first and the absences were treated as confirmation.

Nobody blamed anybody, and that was not politeness but accuracy. It was a competent team reasoning correctly inside a frame that was wrong, and everybody who touched the case was doing what any reasonable colleague would have done. The frame had been built properly, from a common disease, from a presentation that did fit it, and from the absence of features that pointed elsewhere. What a frame does is turn those absences into support. Any team could have made this correction, and any team might have made it a day earlier, because the correction was not a matter of cleverness but of attention. Attention is a property of the system rather than of the people inside it.

Then somebody asked the question nobody had thought to ask in eight hours, which was whether Alan Whitfield had ever had anything like this before. Hannah Ferris went to look at the whole folder rather than the top sheet. In the notes from four years earlier, filed at the back, there was a report from an abdominal scan, read, signed and filed. It said, in a line nobody had looked at since it was typed, that there were gallstones in the gallbladder and that they were not causing symptoms at the time. Alan Whitfield had had stones in his gallbladder for four years and nobody had ever told him, because a report that says not causing symptoms is filed in the way a report nobody needs is filed.

The bloods taken on admission were reviewed, and the pancreatic enzymes that had not been thought to ask for were added and sent. An urgent ultrasound was arranged with the imaging staff. The enzymes came back raised, and the ultrasound showed a gallbladder full of stones and an enlarged, inflamed pancreas, and the diagnosis was named: acute pancreatitis, with gallstones as the cause. Mr Ashworth came back to the department, and when he was told the working diagnosis had changed he did not ask why the team had been so certain for so long. He asked instead what would be needed to change the plan now, which is the better question.

It is worth setting out exactly how the mistake was made, because a mistake without a cause cannot be avoided a second time. The first cause was that the diagnosis was chosen before the examination had finished, so that the examination became a search for support rather than a test of a proposal. The second was that absences were read as reassurance, so that a quiet abdomen and a urine without blood became evidence for the stone rather than against it. The third was that a nursing chart was treated as a record of nursing rather than as clinical information. The fourth was that a result filed four years ago was never read again, though it would have changed the diagnosis.

The plan was rebuilt around the pancreatitis pathway, and the rebuild was more than a new label on a chart. The intravenous fluid given to correct a dehydration became the treatment in its own right, because the deficit in this illness is large and the inflamed pancreas leaks fluid around it. The fluid was titrated to the urine that came out. The analgesia was continued and actively reviewed, because the pain of pancreatitis is severe and does not resolve by itself. The surgical team took on the question of the gallbladder, because the pancreatitis will return while the stones remain, and that question was deferred until Alan Whitfield was well and recorded as a plan. The volume of fluid given in this illness differs between patients and between protocols, so confirm it against the current guideline and the local protocol.

Hannah Ferris rebuilt the nursing plan on the same chart, because the chart had already proved it was worth having. Urine output became the number the ward was watching, measured and written down every hour, because in this illness the kidneys and the circulation are what the fluid is being given for. The blood pressure, the pulse and the breathing were watched as signs of a patient being overwhelmed, and the temperature was watched because a fever here means something has gone wrong in another direction. She set herself thresholds for calling the registrar, and told Alan Whitfield what she was watching for, and that the number she cared about most was the one in the bottle on the trolley.

The change was visible within hours, which is the best evidence that the diagnosis had been wrong rather than the treatment. The pain came under better control, the vomiting stopped, and by the morning the urine output had risen from the trickle of the night before to a volume the registrar looked at twice, the first entry in the whole admission that looked the way it was supposed to look. The temperature stayed down, the pulse settled, and the abdomen was less tender than it had been at midnight. The target for urine output in this illness is written down in the current guideline and the local protocol, and it is not a figure to be trusted from memory, so confirm it against both before you rely on it.

The ultrasound and the surgeon's review together made the plan concrete. The gallbladder was full of stones, the pancreas was swollen, and there was fluid around it, and the surgeon's view was that the stones had passed through the ducts and set the pancreas alight, a day before Alan Whitfield reached the department. The stones themselves were not the emergency; the emergency was the inflammation and what it did to the vessels around the pancreas and to the fluid balance of the body. He would come back for the gallbladder to be dealt with once the pancreas had settled, because a patient whose stones remain after a first attack is a patient who will have another. The interval at which that surgery is done depends on the recovery and on the service, so confirm the timing against the current guideline and the local protocol.

He recovered over the following week, and the recovery was slow and dull, and dull was what the ward wanted. He began to eat, and he and Hannah Ferris developed a routine in which she weighed him, measured his urine, and asked him about his pain. On the day he was discharged they went through the plan for the gallbladder and the signs that would bring him back in a hurry, and she wrote them on a sheet he would put on the fridge at home. She made sure he could say them back before she let him go. The team took the case apart afterwards, and what came out of it was not a search for whoever had missed something, but a list of the places where a question could have been asked and was not.

The chart is the part of this story that generalises most easily, and it is worth saying why it worked. It was started for no reason at all, other than that the nurse who started it believed a patient being given fluid and vomiting ought to have a record of what went in and what came out, and that this was not somebody else's decision to make. It was kept in hours rather than in days, so that it showed a trend rather than a total, and a trend is what reveals a mismatch. Nobody had asked for it and nobody had reviewed it for most of the night, and it did its work anyway, because a number written down at the time is still there in the morning, and a number only in somebody's head is not. The teaching in that is not that nurses hold the answers and doctors hold the diagnoses, but that a record nobody is asked to keep and nobody is asked to read is doing nothing at all.

Alan Whitfield was unremarkable about the whole thing, and his last comment before he went was that the thing he would have wanted somebody to notice was where the pain went. He had said it at the beginning and it had not been picked up, and he had spent the night beside a chart he could not see that somebody was keeping for him. He also said that the worst hour was not the pain but being sick for the fourth time and being told to wait for the scan, because waiting felt like not being taken seriously. Both remarks were about the experience of being ill rather than the biology of the illness. They were the reason the ward agreed that every patient with abdominal pain would be asked out loud where the pain goes, and what makes it worse and what makes it better.

None of this was a failure of care, and it is worth saying so plainly, because the story of a wrong diagnosis is usually told as a story about somebody being wrong, and it was not. The pattern is one of the commonest there is: a common disease in a common shape, an examination performed to confirm rather than to test, absences read as reassurance, a chart kept but not interpreted, and a result filed where nobody would look again. What makes these dangerous is that they arrive together and reinforce one another. By the small hours the evidence for the stone was in the room and the evidence against it was in the room too, and only one of them was being read. The nurse had written it down, the surgeon had seen it, and the registrar had the notes in his hand more than once.

The teaching of the case is simple and it is about the examination. The direction in which pain travels is part of the examination, not a detail to be collected once the diagnosis is known; it is one of the things that makes it. A stone in the ureter produces pain that comes from the side and goes down towards the groin, and it comes in waves, so that a patient with one cannot sit still. An inflamed pancreas produces a pain high in the middle of the abdomen that goes through to his back, that is relieved by leaning forward, and that a patient protects by staying still. The reason a team misses this is not that the sign is obscure but that it is asked about only in the first ten minutes, when the diagnosis is still a possibility.

The countermeasure, then, is not to be cleverer but to be more systematic at the moment the diagnosis is chosen, and any team could do it. Write down the working diagnosis and the two findings that would prove it wrong, and go looking for those two findings deliberately, in the first hour, while they are still cheap to find. Treat an absent sign as information rather than as the absence of information, and read the nursing chart as clinical information rather than as a record of nursing. Ask the patient where the pain goes, and what makes it better and what makes it worse, and take the answers at face value when they disagree with the expected pattern. And when a treatment has not worked by the end of a night shift, treat that not as a failure of the treatment but as the strongest single piece of information available, because the patient who has not improved has not disappointed anybody, he has corrected somebody.

To summarize, Alan Whitfield's pain was not a stone at all but acute pancreatitis caused by gallstones that had been sitting in a report filed four years earlier. The team treated a common disease competently and wrongly for eight hours, because the evidence that pointed elsewhere was quiet rather than dramatic. The failures were procedural rather than personal: a diagnosis chosen before the examination had finished, absences read as reassurance, a chart kept without being interpreted, and a result filed where nobody would look at it again. The nursing observation that turned the case was a strict fluid balance chart started without being asked, together with the posture of a man who would not move and whose pain went through to his back, because a stone blocking one kidney should not stop the other from working. Every number quoted in this chapter is an illustration of reasoning only, and each must be checked against the current guideline and the local protocol before it informs the care of a real patient. This chapter is educational material, not medical advice and not a protocol, and it does not replace the training, supervision or judgment of the clinician who was with the patient. And the lesson that travels furthest is a small one: where the pain goes is part of the examination, and a chart kept for no reason is often the chart that carries the answer.

# Chapter 6 — The Bellyache Too Severe to Touch

Samuel Nkemelu came through the doors of the emergency department at half past seven in the evening with a plastic bag in one hand and a referral letter in the other, and the first thing Ngozi Duru noticed was that he apologised for taking up a bed. The second thing she noticed was his pulse. She counted it twice while she was talking to him and got a different answer each time, and she wrote it in the box where the pulse goes, because there was a queue behind him and no reason on the form to say anything more. The referral letter was folded in his shirt pocket and she read it while she was wheeling him into a bay. It was three lines long, and two of them said that he had been vomiting since lunchtime.

This is a constructed composite, and no part of it describes a real person; the details are assembled from the ordinary way these presentations arrive, so that the reasoning can be examined rather than the history of anyone living or dead. The letters that come with him are the letters that come with everybody: a name, an age, a problem, and the names of the tablets he takes, written down by somebody in a hurry who had never met him. How old a patient is, and how many hours the symptoms have been going on, both feed the decision about waiting or acting. The intervals and the ages that count as urgent differ between conditions and between services, so check them against the current guideline and against the protocol your own service works to.

The team that night was a surgical registrar, a consultant who could be called, and the emergency nurse who took the admission, with an anaesthetist who came in early because the working diagnosis was surgical. The registrar was Harriet Bexley, who had a way of asking a patient to lie still while she examined him that made most of them try harder than they needed to. The anaesthetist was Nina Karlsen, who came because an obstruction in a man of seventy-one might turn into an operation within a day. The nurse was Ngozi Duru, who took the history, the observations and the triage sheet, and who is the reason this case turned out the way it did. She was not a person who carried out orders on this admission. She was the person who asked the questions nobody else asked, and on this night she was right.

Samuel Nkemelu was 71 and had spent thirty-eight years as a civil servant, and came by taxi because his wife was unwell herself. The pain had started at lunchtime the day before and never stopped, and it was worse over the last few hours, and when she asked him to describe it he said it was the worst pain of his life, and then apologised for saying that, as though it were an exaggeration. The vomiting had begun about six hours after the pain and had continued, and the fluid he brought in the plastic bag was the colour of coffee grounds, which she noted and moved on from, because there were eleven other people waiting. He had had a heart complaint for as long as anybody could remember, and took tablets for it every morning, and she wrote heart complaint and tablets in the notes and did not ask which tablets, because the working diagnosis was obstruction and the tablets were not the working diagnosis.

Dr Bexley examined him on a trolley with the curtains drawn, and asked him to point to the pain first, which he did, and his finger moved a little rather than settling anywhere. The abdomen was soft, she noted, and barely tender, with no guarding, no rebound tenderness, no distension and no obvious scar, and the bowel sounds were present. She looked for a hernia and found none, and no lump and no sign of a strangulated hernia. She pressed gently in the right lower quadrant and he said it hurt a great deal.

The tenderness was out of all proportion to what his abdomen gave back to her hands. She wrote that down. She did not draw any conclusion from it, because at that point she was working down a list. The list was obstruction.

The differential she made out loud for the nurse was bowel obstruction, pancreatitis, a perforated ulcer, and bowel ischemia, and she ranked obstruction first because he was vomiting, and because he had had abdominal surgery a long time ago and adhesions are the commonest cause in somebody with a scar. Pancreatitis was second, and she said what would have shown it: a raised level of the pancreatic enzymes in the blood and a gallbladder full of stones on an ultrasound. Perforation was third, and she said what would have shown that: a rigid abdomen and free air on the film. The dangerous one, the one she put at the end of the list and thought about longest, was ischemia of the bowel, and she said what would have revealed it: pain far worse than the examination, a belly that stays quiet, blood in the vomit, and a pulse that is not regular. She had said all four of those things out loud in the bay, and the nurse had heard them, and the pulse had been in the triage sheet for an hour by then.

The treatment was chosen on the evidence available, and the choice was the ordinary one. He was kept nil by mouth, given intravenous fluid for the dehydration that twelve hours of vomiting had produced, and given an antiemetic. A nasogastric tube was passed and put on suction, because a patient who vomits into a bed cannot be examined, and because emptying the stomach takes the vomiting away. The plan was a computed tomography scan of the abdomen in the morning.

A scan in the morning was reasonable, because the scanner was not staffed at night, because he was not peritonitic, and because his observations were unremarkable. The reasoning was sound, and the whole of it rested on one assumption that nobody questioned. That assumption was that the pain was coming from something in the abdomen that the abdomen could tell them about. The point at which prolonged vomiting becomes a finding in itself rather than a symptom depends on the condition, so check that interval against the current guideline and the local protocol.

Ngozi Duru started a fluid balance chart and a pain score chart, and she did both without being asked, because fluid was running into him and a nasogastric tube was coming out of him. She wrote his observations on the hour, and the pain score at rest and on movement beside each observation, because a patient who rates his pain as the worst he has ever had deserves a number on the chart rather than a word in the notes. She also wrote the things nobody had asked her to write, which was the colour of the vomit, the fact that he had apologised four times in an hour, and the fact that the pulse she counted at triage came out differently the second time. She wrote them because she wrote everything, and because the things which look like small observations at the time are often the things that turn out to have been the whole story.

Harriet Bexley came back at ten o'clock with the night registrar, and she did the thing that doctors do and nurses watch, which is to examine the patient. The abdomen was as it had been, soft and barely tender, and she wrote that it was unchanged, and she read the fluid chart and the observations and wrote that he was stable. The pain score chart was on the bed, and the score beside each set of observations was high and did not move, and she did not pick it up, because the numbers on it belonged to the nurse who had written them and those on the observation chart belonged to everybody. That is how a nursing chart becomes decoration, and it usually happens in the space of one week. How a pain score is recorded and what counts as a safe score are set by the local protocol, so confirm the scale and the thresholds against it rather than working from memory.

Dr Karlsen came at half past ten to do the pre-operative assessment the registrar had asked for, and she asked about the heart complaint and was told again that there were tablets every morning, and she asked what the tablets were for and Samuel Nkemelu said he could not remember, and that the box was at home and his wife had it. She wrote heart complaint, tablets, names unknown, and moved on. She examined him, wrote that he was fit for theatre, and told the registrar that the morning list would take him. Then she looked at the pain score chart on her way out, and said to the nurse that a man who says his pain is the worst of his life has an unusual abdomen, and the nurse said yes, and the registrar was on the telephone at the time and did not hear the remark.

The night settled. He was sick twice more, and each time the nurse recorded what came up and how much of it there was, and the nasogastric tube was still draining, and the observations held their shape. The score on his pain chart was the same at eleven and at midnight as it had been at eight, and the nurse wrote that down and thought about it, because a pain that is exactly the same every hour is not a pain that is easing, and she had learned to be suspicious of a flat line on a pain chart. He asked her twice whether the scan was really going to happen in the morning, and she said it was, and she said it warmly, and she believed it. She did not know yet that the belief she had expressed to him was the belief the whole team was holding, and the belief which would have to be broken.

At about two in the morning the registrar was called to the ward because the nurse had noticed that he was not making sense. He was not raving or agitated, but he was answering questions a little to one side, his blood pressure was lower than it had been and his pulse faster. The nurse had also sent a venous blood gas, and the lactate had come back higher than she expected. The registrar came and checked him over, and thought first of sepsis in a dehydrated man with an obstruction, then of a lactate that did not fit dehydration.

She had a third thought, and she did not say it aloud: something was wrong with the bowel in a way that obstruction did not explain. He wrote down the numbers, and did not ring anybody, and did not bring the scan forward, because he could not think what he would do differently at three in the morning. The nurse did not go back to sleep, and she went and read the triage sheet again. The lactate level and the other blood gas values at which a team should escalate differ between protocols, so take the figures from the current guideline and from the local protocol.

She read it from the top, which is what she did at the end of every shift, and this time she read it with a purpose, because she wanted to see what she had written at seven in the evening. She read the pulse box, where she had written a number and then, a few lines lower, a second number, because she had counted again when the first one did not feel right. The two numbers were different by more than you get from counting at two different moments in a heartbeat. She sat with that for a while. Then she put the two readings from the triage sheet beside the ones she had taken since, and they were all different from each other, and none of them was a regular rhythm. She went and looked at her patient, and she counted the pulse herself, with two fingers and her watch, and it came and went, and she wrote it down as irregular.

That was the observation that reopened the case, and it was worth asking why nobody else had made it. The pulse had been written in a box on a form at seven in the evening, and read by nobody, because the triage sheet is a summary of a person and this particular line did not look like a summary of a person, it looked like a number. The registrar had seen the abdomen and the blood pressure and the vomiting and had found in all of them an obstruction, and the nurse's irregular pulse had no place in that story, so it went into the story as nothing. And the other half of the observation, the one the nurse had been building since ten o'clock, was the gap between what the man said and what his abdomen said, and that gap was visible only if you held the pain score chart and the abdominal findings side by side, which is nobody's job and therefore everybody's.

Ngozi Duru went and found Dr Bexley, and said to her that she thought the pain and the abdomen did not go together, and that she had counted the pulse on triage twice and written down two different numbers, and that she had counted it again tonight and it was irregular. She said it without drama and in the order she had discovered it, and she said the words pain out of proportion. She did not say bowel ischemia, because that was not a nurse's diagnosis to make, and the registrar had a name for it and the nurse did not. Dr Bexley listened to the whole of it, and then said, almost to herself, that a man with an irregular pulse and pain out of proportion and a quiet abdomen had a bowel that was dying, and the registrar at the end of the bed said that she had said exactly that on admission, and everybody in the room heard the same sentence arrive twice, eight hours apart.

That is how these cases turn, and it is worth noticing what actually happened, because it was not that one person was cleverer than the others. On admission the registrar had ranked ischemia fourth and had said out loud what would reveal it. At two in the morning the nurse had put the three parts of that same sentence together: the pain, the abdomen, and the pulse. The information had never left the room.

What had left the room was the connection between the parts. A connection is not carried in a box on a triage sheet, and it is not carried in a pain score either. The nurse had not been given a job to make the connection. She made it anyway, at two in the morning, because she was the one still holding the whole picture, and the team had handed the picture over in pieces.

The scan was brought forward, and the night registrar went to the imaging staff and asked for it to be done as soon as it could be done rather than on the morning list, and the answer was that it could be done within the hour. They did not argue about it, and they did not spend twenty minutes deciding who should have thought of the pulse. The blood gas was repeated and the lactate was higher again, and the nurse wrote the two results side by side with the times beside them, and the line that went up was not a line that could be explained by a man who had not eaten since yesterday morning. The registrar rang the consultant at home and then rang the anaesthetist, and the consultant was awake in three rings, and the anaesthetist said she would come in, and the registrar wrote in the notes that the plan had changed and that the patient was going to theatre. She also asked the ward to stop the drip that had been running, because the anaesthetist wanted the stomach empty, and she asked for the nasogastric tube to be left in place. How quickly the scan should be brought forward in a patient who is deteriorating is a local decision, so confirm the interval against the current guideline and the local protocol.

In the twenty minutes before the team moved, the registrar took a history again, and this time she asked the questions she had not asked at seven in the evening. She asked what the tablets were, and Samuel Nkemelu said his wife had the box and would bring it, and the nurse telephoned the wife, and the wife read out the names, and one of them was a drug used to thin the blood for a heart condition called atrial fibrillation, which is a rhythm of the heart that is not regular and which allows clots to form in the chamber. The referral letter had not mentioned any of it, because the letter had been written by a general practitioner who had seen a man with vomiting and had put the two things that mattered to him on the line above the line. Nobody had asked the man what his tablets were for, and the man had said heart complaint, and heart complaint is not a diagnosis.

The abdomen was opened in the small hours, and the bowel was found to be black and dead in a length running from the small bowel into the colon, and the cause was a clot that had come from the atrium of the fibrillating heart and lodged in the artery that supplies the bowel. The length of dead bowel was removed, and what remained was joined, and the anaesthetist took him to the intensive care unit afterwards, where he stayed for a night with the circulation watched closely. The surgery was the part that fixed the bowel. It did not fix the thing that had caused it, because the heart still fibrillates and still holds the place where the clot came from, and that second problem was solved on the ward rather than in theatre, and the anticoagulation decision was made with the surgical team and the patient, who was seventy-one and had previously declined a blood thinner because he did not want to be on a drug for the rest of his life.

Recovery from this operation is slow, and slow is the point of the story. For days the ward's work was not diagnosis but protection: the fluid balance maintained, the wound watched, the lungs kept clear, and the nutrition built up carefully while the bowel that had been sewn back learned to work again. Ngozi Duru wrote the nursing plan for him herself, around the things that had gone wrong the first time rather than the things that had gone right. Her plan said what his pulse was expected to be, what his abdomen should feel like, what his temperature should be, and what she should do if the wound leaked or he became confused again, and she put the numbers beside the actions. She wrote, in the space at the bottom of the page for anything else the nurse should know, that this man had described pain out of proportion to his examination, and that if he ever again said something that did not match what his body showed, it should be believed first and checked second. The observations expected in recovery, and the rate at which they are taken, are set by the local protocol, so check them against the current guideline rather than against a general idea.

He left the hospital after a long stay, thinner and weaker than he had been, with a frame and a plan for his tablets that he had not had before. He said that the part of the night that frightened him was not the pain but the hour when he could not make sense of himself and the young woman was speaking to him and he could not answer her. He said that he had been the most frightened of his life in that hour, that nobody had told him what was happening, and that he had thought he was dying and the ward had not noticed. He said it without any bitterness, and the registrar who was on the ward that week wrote it in the notes, because it is the kind of thing that is worth writing down and almost never is.

Nothing that happened to Samuel Nkemelu was the fault of any individual, and it is worth saying so clearly, because the story of a nearly fatal delay is usually told as the story of somebody being slow. The nurse had not been slow. The registrar had not been slow. The team had been working hard and reasoning carefully on a presentation that fits bowel obstruction almost perfectly, and the facts that did not fit had been present from the first hour and had been present in writing.

The nurse had counted an irregular pulse and written it down and had not been asked what it meant. The abdominal examination had found a quiet belly and had been recorded in those terms. A referral letter had been shortened to three lines by a general practitioner doing a good job with the time available to him. The information about the heart had been left out of this one.

The two lessons in this case are both about reading what is written rather than what is expected. The first is that pain out of proportion to the examination is a diagnosis, not a puzzling symptom, and that a quiet abdomen in a patient who is screaming is a warning, not reassurance, because the bowel that is dying is the bowel that has not yet had time to inflame the tissues around it. A patient whose pain is the worst he has ever had and whose abdomen can be pressed without much difficulty is not reassuring to examine, and the abdomen is examined last and relied on most. The second lesson is about the irregular pulse on the triage sheet. An irregular pulse is not a line to be scored out of a form and read past. It is a piece of information, it was there at seven in the evening, and this patient's treatment turned on it, and it reached the registrar only because a nurse went back and read a form she had already filled in.

The countermeasure for both is small and unglamorous. When the pain and the examination do not agree, that disagreement is the finding, and it should be written down where it cannot be skimmed past, because every diagnosis in the list was argued on the abdomen. And when a triage sheet records something unusual, it should be passed on at the time, in words, to whoever is taking the patient, because a box on a form is not a handover, and the information that changes a case is almost never the information that was expected. Any team could have made this correction, and any team might have made it earlier, because the correction was not a matter of cleverness but of attention to what the form already said.

To summarize, Samuel Nkemelu did not have a bowel obstruction but acute ischemia of the bowel, caused by a clot thrown from an atrium that had been fibrillating for years and about which nobody in the hospital knew, because the pulse that would have said so was written in a box on a triage sheet at seven in the evening and read by nobody until two in the morning. The failure was not in anybody's skill or in anybody's care. It was a working diagnosis that explained almost everything, a pain score chart that everybody could see and nobody read, an abdominal examination that was treated as more reliable than the account of the pain, and a piece of paper that summarised a man in three lines and left out the part that mattered. The nursing observation that turned the case was an irregular pulse counted twice at triage and again at two in the morning, read alongside a pain score that had not moved all night, and the two together said that the pain was coming from somewhere the abdomen could not show. Every number given in this chapter is offered as an example of the reasoning rather than as a figure to work from, and each one must be verified against the current guideline and the local protocol before it is applied to a patient. The chapter is teaching material rather than advice, and it does not stand in for the training, the supervision, or the judgment of the clinician who was at the bedside. And the lesson that travels furthest is that pain out of proportion to a quiet abdomen is a diagnosis in itself, and that an irregular pulse on a triage sheet is information, not a line to be ruled.

# Chapter 7 — The Indigestion That Would Not Settle

Harold Pettigrew was sixty-nine years old, a retired postman, and this case is worth telling because of a number on a set of scales in a hallway. It is a constructed composite, assembled for teaching, and it does not describe a real patient; the details have been changed so that nobody could be recognised, and the only thing reported faithfully here is the reasoning. The reasoning that organises the story is simple to state and hard to carry out in a busy week: weight loss in an older person is a sign a clinician has observed, not a symptom the patient has reported, and the two behave in completely different ways. Pettigrew never told anybody that he had lost weight. He said he had been busy and had been worrying about his daughter, and both of those statements were true, and neither of them was the finding.

He came to the surgery on a wet Tuesday in the autumn with six weeks of discomfort in the upper part of his stomach and of heartburn behind his breastbone. The burning came in the evening after his meal, and it came again when he bent to tie his laces or to lift the heavy bowl the cat had knocked over, and lying flat after ten o'clock made it worse than sitting up did. There was no vomiting, no difficulty swallowing, and no blood in the mouth or in the stool, and he had never had an ulcer. He had come because his daughter had said that he was eating differently. When the general practitioner asked him about his weight he said he had not really noticed it, and then he said that his daughter had been going through a difficult year and that he had not been eating properly. The duration of a complaint like this is itself information, and how many weeks of it should prompt a wider look is set out differently in different services, so confirm the interval that matters against the current guideline and the local protocol before you build a plan on any particular number of weeks.

The alternative that had to be named out loud in that room, and was named out loud, was a cancer of the stomach, because it is the diagnosis that kills quietly and the one that is easiest to postpone. What would reveal it, if it were there, would be weight falling without any change of appetite or intention, iron deficiency anaemia on a full blood count, a firm mass felt in the upper abdomen, and at endoscopy a heaped and ulcerated area rather than the shallow reddened patches of gastritis. A bleeding ulcer was the other danger worth carrying in mind, and the finding that would give that away would be pain waking him in the night, or vomiting blood, or a black stool. Pettigrew had none of those at the first appointment, and that was genuine good fortune rather than good reasoning, because the first appointment contained everything that would have settled the question and nobody asked for it in the right form.

Dr Adrian Musgrave examined him and found a soft abdomen with no tenderness and no mass, and he wrote a note that read like a note about reflux. Before he wrote the treatment he weighed him on the practice scales, as he weighed everybody who walked in, and recorded the number. It was the only number in that consultation that was going to matter, it was written in the same hand as everything else, between the blood pressure and the plan, and nobody looked at it again that day. A weight on its own is an unremarkable number, and how much change matters depends on the person's own baseline, on age, and on whether the change was noticed or merely measured; check the current guideline and the local protocol for the threshold your own service treats as significant rather than assuming one from this account.

Dr Musgrave ranked his thinking out loud, and the ranking was reasonable. The most likely explanation was oesophagitis, the inflammation of the tube that carries food from the mouth down to the stomach, which fitted his symptoms, his timing and his habit of eating late and lying down afterwards. Behind that came gastritis, an inflammation of the stomach lining, and behind that a peptic ulcer, a sore in the stomach or in the first part of the small bowel, which can bleed and can perforate. Fourth on the list, and the one that could not simply be set aside on the evidence available, was the cancer he had named aloud, and it sat fourth not because the evidence against it was strong but because there was an explanation for everything else. The working diagnosis was gastritis with oesophagitis, and the reason for that choice was audible and reasonable: the heartburn had the textbook shape, the examination was reassuring, and a man who is uncomfortable for some weeks and otherwise well is rarely in danger today. The hole in that reasoning was not in the ranking but in the explanation he had been given for the weight, which made the weight invisible rather than irrelevant.

The treatment was acid suppression, which is the right treatment for reflux and for gastritis and which many people find genuinely helpful, and it was begun straight away. He was told to stop eating late in the evening, to leave a good gap before bed, to raise the head of the bed, and to give up the bottle of cider he had been having with his supper and had not mentioned until it was asked about. He was told he would be reviewed in six weeks with a gastroenterology consultant, and that he should come back sooner if anything changed. The advice about returning sooner came on a printed sheet on which the items about bleeding and about weight sat among two dozen unemphasised phrases, and he folded it into his wallet behind his bus pass and did not read it again. The interval, and the set of warning signs that should bring a patient back before it ends, is set out in the current guideline and the local protocol for your own service, so confirm it there rather than working from memory.

Meanwhile a district nurse had been asked to visit, because the suggestion that weight and food intake should be recorded at home had come from the practice nurse as a routine thing for a patient who was not clearly improving. Her name was Carole Winslow, and she was to hold the thread of this case without ever holding the title. On her first visit she asked about the day, the food, the sleep, the bowels and the medicines, and she put him on the scales in his kitchen and wrote the number down with the date beside it. She also gave him a food diary, a single page with columns for what he ate and when, and explained that it was not a test of him but a way of seeing the pattern. He took the diary seriously and filled it in faithfully for week after week, and she read every page, and she noticed something in it that nobody else was looking for.

What she noticed in the diary was that the food had been narrowing. Week by week the entries became shorter, and what was left on the lines was bread and tea, with butter if she was lucky. There was no meat, no vegetables and no fruit, and there were days when the entry said simply nothing eaten. When she asked about it he said that he had started a new diet, and he said it cheerfully, and he explained that cutting out the heavy fatty food had helped the heartburn and that he was going to keep it up. She asked whether he had noticed any change in his weight and he said he thought he had lost a bit, and then he said that he was not really a man who weighed himself. The new diet was not a diet. It was a symptom, and it was the symptom of a disease that was slowly taking his appetite away, and he had relabelled it as a success and was proud of it.

What she wrote in her own notes that day matters more than what she said. She wrote the weight, and the previous weight, and then in the free text she wrote the sentence that would turn out to be the hinge of the whole case: that he appeared to be losing weight steadily, that he was eating very little, and that this was not being accounted for. That sentence went into her district nursing record, where it was visible to anyone who opened the file. It did not reach the surgery, and it did not reach Dr Musgrave, and the reason was not that anybody had decided it was unimportant. The reason was that nothing in the way the service was organised asked for it to travel anywhere, and a note that stays in the file where it was written is a note that has been recorded but not used. Nothing in that was a decision by any person; it was simply the shape of the service, and any team working inside that shape would have behaved the same way.

For the length of the planned interval the arrangement did what arrangements do, and nothing happened, and nothing happening felt like the treatment working. Pettigrew took his acid suppression and was, by his own account, somewhat better in the evenings, so the improvement was as real as it was uninformative. He did not come back, so the advice about returning sooner never had to be tested, and Dr Musgrave did not see him at all. Carole Winslow visited repeatedly through that period and each time she weighed him and each time the number was lower, and each time she told herself that this was the kind of complaint that settled down in the end.

There was a moment in that period when the service could easily have gone on doing what it was doing, and it nearly did. Dr Musgrave had a list of patients waiting, a review date already in the diary, and no new information reaching him, and the tempting thing was to see him at the planned appointment and, if the heartburn had improved, discharge him. Carole Winslow had a chart of falling numbers and a patient who was cheerful and a theory he was pleased with, and the tempting thing for her was to accept the explanation, because accepting it was comfortable and because she had no route to offer anything else. What saved them was not a flash of insight but the shape of the arrangement: the review appointment was still in the diary, and a man who came back early and unwell could not be sent home to wait for it.

He came back to the surgery himself, without an appointment, eight weeks after the first consultation rather than six, and he came because the pain had begun to wake him in the small hours of the night, which it had never done before. He was sick in the morning for the first time since this started. He could feel a lump in the upper part of his stomach when he pressed on it, and his clothes had gone loose enough that his daughter had commented on it. His skin was pale, there was a small firm swelling under the skin at the base of his neck on the left side, and his blood pressure was lower than the one recorded in the autumn. None of this was expected by anybody, and the return arrived as a bundle of new findings rather than as the single change that should have triggered a rethink. The interval that should have applied to a man of this age with a falling weight is set out in the current guideline and the local protocol.

Dr Musgrave listened to all of it and understood, before he laid a hand on him, that the case had changed shape. What he had spent the intervening weeks treating was a symptom he had never actually confirmed, and he now had a man of sixty-nine with a mass, a falling blood pressure, a new lump above the collarbone and a history of night pain, and those together were not reflux. He said so to the patient in plain words, which took some doing, and he said that the earlier plan had been built on the wrong idea and that nobody had done anything wrong in the sense of the word he could use. He examined him again thoroughly, and then he did something he had not thought of doing before: he went back through the interim notes, including the district nursing record, and he found Carole Winslow's sentence about the weight.

What Carole Winslow did that afternoon was the part of the case that mattered most. The practice had asked her to bring in the weight chart and the food diary so that the entries could be copied into the notes, and she came with both, and she found him in a room with a doctor who was talking about a mass. It was she who read the numbers out loud, because nobody else in the room had seen them together: the weight at the first visit, the weight at every visit since, the fall of four kilograms across the whole period, and then the diary with its shrinking entries and its repeated blank days. And it was she who said the sentence that changed the case, which was that a loss of four kilograms in a man of sixty-nine with no reason to lose it was not something to be explained away but a test that had to be done, and that she had written it down at the time and nobody had asked her to send it anywhere. Whether a loss of four kilograms in a man of that age counts as significant is a question with different answers in different services, and the number that should be acted on should come from the current guideline and the local protocol rather than from this account. What does not depend on the answer is the shape of the argument, which is that the number was real, that it was moving in one direction, that it was written down, and that it was never read by anybody in a position to act on it.

The gastroenterology consultant, Dr Hester Danbury, saw him that afternoon and did not leave the diagnosis until the week after next. She took a full history and examined him, and she asked the question that nobody had asked since the autumn: what was he managing to eat, and how had that changed. She asked it because the answer would decide whether this was still a benign peptic disease, and she wanted the answer before she reached for the tube rather than after. She also asked about smoking, about his appetite and about his family history, and what he had done about the pain when it woke him, and she heard the answer that he had done nothing because he believed the diet was working. Her working diagnosis was a cancer of the stomach with an obstructing component and probable spread beyond the stomach, and she said so to Dr Musgrave in those words and in front of the patient, because she had learned that a person who is going to have cancer would rather hear it early and clearly.

A full blood count was taken before anything else was done, and it came back as she expected: a haemoglobin well below the reference range for a man of his age, with small red cells that go with a chronic loss of blood, which is the picture of an iron deficiency anaemia rather than of a recent bleed. That number is an illustration of reasoning rather than a threshold, and reference ranges for haemoglobin differ between laboratories and between populations, so the value that matters should be confirmed against the current guideline and the local protocol. An endoscopy was arranged for the following morning as an urgent one, and the reason for the urgency was written into the note. The reason was that a man of sixty-nine with a mass, iron deficiency anaemia and a history of night pain has a stomach that must be looked at.

She did it herself the next morning, and the finding is the one that changes everything. Instead of the shallow reddened stomach she had expected, there was an area in the lower part of the stomach that was ulcerated and heaped up at the edges, with a raised and irregular rim and a floor that was not clean granulation but dirty, necrotic tissue. That is not the shape of anything that acid suppression will ever heal, and an experienced endoscopist can often tell from the look of it whether a change is invading more deeply than the stomach wall. She took biopsies from the edge and from the base, sent them to the laboratory as urgent, and told the ward staff and the recovery nurse what she had found, so that the news would be given by a team and not by accident on a round.

The histology came back as an adenocarcinoma, a gland-forming cancer of the stomach, which is the commonest cancer of that organ and the one that behaves in this way. The diagnosis was given by Dr Danbury with the gastroenterology consultant present, in plain language, and with the next steps written down so that nobody had to hold them all in their head at once. Pettigrew asked whether he would be all right, and she answered honestly, telling him what the treatment could achieve and what it could not achieve without softening either half of it. Staging followed with a computed tomography, or CT, scan of the chest, the abdomen and the pelvis, and it showed a tumour in the stomach with enlarged lymph nodes around it, a small volume of fluid in the abdomen, and disease in the liver. The case went to a meeting where the images were discussed and a plan was agreed: he would have a form of surgery to control the bleeding, the obstruction and the vomiting, and treatment for the cancer that had spread would be given alongside it. The meeting was not about blame, and there was nothing to blame, and what it was about was the fact that a man with a mass and iron deficiency anaemia had spent a planned interval on an acid suppression tablet and a food diary.

He went to theatre within days, and the surgeon explained beforehand what the surgery would do and what it would not do, because the difference between controlling a tumour and curing it is the hardest conversation in this kind of medicine and it has to be had properly rather than hopefully. He came through the operation and the days after it, the recovery was slow in the way that this recovery always is, and it was run day to day by the nursing team on the ward. He needed a great deal of fluid, the wound was watched, his pain was managed, and by the time he was eating he was being taught how to manage his own symptoms at home. The cancer treatment was explained to him simply enough that he could repeat it to his daughter, who had been the one to notice the loose clothes.

Carole Winslow was there for the discharge, and she did the things a district nurse does, but the conversation was a different one from the one she had had in the same kitchen a few months before. She asked him what he wanted to be told if the pain came back, and what she should telephone her about, and wrote down the numbers she would need. She gave him a sheet again, this time with the warning signs that mattered for him rather than the generic ones, and she asked him to promise that he would ring rather than wait. Then she did something that had not been part of her job before and became part of it afterwards: she went back to the surgery and asked what the service intended to do about unexplained weight loss in an older patient, and she and the general practitioner wrote down what it would do.

The rule they wrote says that an unexplained loss of weight in an older adult is a finding that travels, rather than a note that waits. The weight is to be recorded with the same care as a temperature, to be compared with the previous number rather than with a memory of it, and a fall which is not understood is to send the patient back to the door of the service rather than forward to the next appointment. The person who notices does not have to be the person who treats, and the weight is discussed where the nurse's chart is part of the record. Whether the threshold is a particular number of kilograms, a proportion of body weight, or a rate of loss over a given period is set out in the current guideline and the local protocol, and those thresholds should be checked rather than invented.

There is a second lesson underneath the first, and it is the one that generalises furthest. A symptom that is explained away has not been explained; it has been tolerated.
