Pettigrew's new diet, his busy month, his worry about his daughter, the improvement in his evening heartburn on the tablet: each of these was a reasonable thing to believe, and together they formed a frame through which everything else was read and found compatible. The frame was held by the doctor, by the patient, by the nurse who liked him, and by a service that had a slot for reviewing reflux and no slot for reviewing a falling weight. The thing that would have broken it fastest was the most ordinary thing in medicine, which is somebody writing down a number and somebody else reading it.

The general practitioner's difficulty is not ignorance, and the difficulty is not unusual. Weight is a noisy measurement, because it changes with the season, the shoes, the time of day and the mood of the person on the scale, and a number that moves for ordinary reasons is a poor basis for alarming anybody. The safeguards against this are procedural rather than personal. The number is written where the next clinician will see it without needing to be asked, and the service must have somewhere for the nurse to send an observation that worries her. A community nurse who has found something troubling and has no route to send it will learn, over time, to stop finding things that trouble her, and the amount of change your own service acts on should come from the current guideline and the local protocol.

The people carrying this case were not careless, and none of them can honestly be said to have failed. Dr Danbury asked the question that had been avoided, and she asked it early enough for the answer to matter. Carole Winslow noticed what was in the diary and in the numbers and refused to let it be explained away, and the refusal was not cleverness but attention. What made the difference was not brilliance. It was that somebody weighed him on the same scale, wrote it down, and said out loud what the number meant.

To summarize, this case turned on a number that everybody had seen and nobody had read, and the diagnosis that was wrong was reasonable at the moment it was made and then became a frame that made everything after it invisible. The patient was given a treatment aimed at nothing in particular, so that nobody could say what success would look like or when failure had arrived, and he was given a review date that functioned as a licence to wait. The overlooked clue was never hidden: it was a weight falling steadily, a food diary narrowing to bread and tea, and a symptom the patient had relabelled as a success, all of it recorded by a community nurse who had nowhere to send it. The correction came when the chart was brought into the room and read aloud, when the abdomen was examined again, and when the diagnosis was named out loud so that the treatment could finally be aimed at it. The lessons are that weight loss in an older person is a sign that somebody observed and must be carried to somebody who can act on it, and that a symptom which has been explained away has not been explained but tolerated. Any team with a nurse who is heard, a record that is read, and a plan with a target could have made this correction, and nothing in it required a cleverer physician or a luckier patient than the ones who were there.

# Chapter 8 — The Girl with the Stomach Ache

The observation chart in the emergency department had a column for pulse and a column for blood pressure, and on that Thursday night both of them were quietly disagreeing with the rest of the note. Amara Nwosu was twenty-four years old, worked as a barista, and had come in with two days of abdominal pain and vomiting which she had treated at home with oral rehydration salts bought from a pharmacy. This is a constructed composite, assembled for teaching, and it does not describe a real patient; the details have been changed so that nobody could be recognised, and only the reasoning is reported faithfully. What organises the story is a question that takes moments to ask and was never asked at all, and a set of observations that everybody had been taught to read as dehydration. The nurse who settled her into the cubicle was called Pauline Craddock, and she is the person who watched the treatment fail and then asked the question.

The triage assessment recorded that she was in considerable pain and vomiting everything she drank, that she had been off work for two days, and that she had tried to keep herself hydrated with the rehydration salts. She had no diarrhoea to speak of, which she noted herself, and she was dismissive of the possibility that anything serious was happening, because she was young and she had a job and she could not afford to be ill. The pain was low in her abdomen and she described it as cramping and one-sided, though when the nurse asked her to point she put a finger on the right lower side and then moved it about without settling anywhere. She had asked for something for the pain and something for the sickness, and she had not mentioned anything else that seemed worth mentioning. How long a woman should be observed for vomiting and abdominal pain, and which observations should be repeated during that observation, is set out differently from place to place, so confirm the interval and the set of observations against the current guideline and the local protocol.

The alternative that had to be named when she was described, because it kills quickly and hides itself, was a ruptured ectopic pregnancy, a pregnancy that has implanted outside the cavity of the uterus and has torn through as it grows. What would reveal it would be a pregnancy test that came back positive, a pulse that stayed fast and a blood pressure that stayed low despite fluids, pain that was sharply one-sided, pain at the top of the shoulder when blood irritates the diaphragm, and a period that had not arrived. Not one of those findings is hard to look for, and none of them requires experience to recognise. What they require is that somebody thinks of them, and the reason nobody thought of them is the subject of this case.

The emergency department registrar was Dr Duncan Wetherby, and he built the differential in front of the patient, saying the words out loud, which is how a differential should be built when there is time. The most likely explanation was gastroenteritis, an infection of the stomach and the bowel, and there was a good deal behind it: vomiting, a working week spent handling food, a shared kitchen at work, and the general chaos of a young woman's month. Behind that he put a urinary tract infection, and behind that a bowel obstruction, and behind that appendicitis, because the pain was one-sided and she was sick. The ectopic pregnancy sat on the list as well, and he said so, and he said what would make him act on it. Then he ranked the list and it came out with gastroenteritis first, and the reason was that he had a story that fitted almost everything she had told him.

The reason is the part of the consultation that nobody can reconstruct afterwards without wishing it had gone differently. She had not mentioned any bleeding, because she had not thought it relevant and because she was embarrassed and wanted to be seen and sent home, and she had not mentioned that her periods had stopped, because she had put that down to the stress of a new job and a shared flat and a rota she hated. There was no box on the triage sheet for either, and the registrar had not asked for either, so the two facts that would have changed everything were absent from the record rather than from the patient. He was not being careless. He was asking the questions that the story he had already heard called for, and the story he had heard was a story about a virus.

The treatment was sensible for the diagnosis. She was given oral rehydration, which is the right treatment for the vomiting and the dehydration and which she had already started herself, and an antiemetic, and analgesia for the cramping, and a plan to discharge her once she had kept something down and her observations had settled. There was no blood taken for a pregnancy test, because on the day as it had been presented there was nothing to point at. The plan was to keep her under observation for a few hours, give her something to read, and let her go home with a leaflet about the warning signs of dehydration and what to eat when the vomiting had settled.

What happened over the following hours is written in the notes by the nurse who was with her, and it is the part of this case that anybody reading the record afterwards would pick out first. The nursing care was observations, a cannula into a vein so that fluids could be given if needed, and the questions a nurse asks when she gets a patient settled, the ones that are not on the form. Those questions are the ordinary ones: when the pain started and where it is now, how much she has been able to drink and how much she has kept down, and what her bowels and her urine are doing. They also include what she has taken for the pain and when, whether she has any medical history and any regular medicines, whether she is allergic to anything, and who is at home with her.

Somewhere in the middle of that, while she was settling her and while the antiemetic was being given, the nurse asked a question that had not been asked: when was her last period. It came out easily, in the way that things do when nobody has made them sound important. She had not had a period for eight weeks. The gap between periods, and the length of time that should make a clinician ask about a pregnancy, are set out in the current guideline and the local protocol, so confirm the interval your own service uses before you treat a gap as normal. She had put the gap down to stress, and she had been certain that she could not be pregnant, because she had an intrauterine device, a small device fitted inside the uterus to prevent pregnancy, which had been fitted some time before and had never given her any trouble. She said the words no period and I cannot be pregnant without any sense that she was handing over two pieces of information that did not belong in the same diagnosis at all.

The treatment did not work, and the way it failed was not in the complaint but in the chart. She stopped vomiting, which was good, and the pain eased slightly with the analgesia, which was also good, and the observation chart told a different story the whole time. She was pale and clammy, and the sweat on her forehead dried before she had finished speaking. Her pulse was 118 beats per minute and stayed there, thin and hard to feel, and her blood pressure was 88 over 56 millimeters of mercury, and when the nurse sat her up to measure it she became grey, her eyes rolled, and she had to be caught. She was worse sitting up than lying down, and that single fact, more than any number on the chart, told the nurse that this was not a young woman with a stomach upset.

How fast is too fast and how low is too low are questions with different answers for a young woman, for a woman on the oral contraceptive, and for a woman in her earliest pregnancy. The values that count as abnormal also change with age and with the medicines a woman is taking, so the readings that should be acted on should come from the current guideline and the local protocol rather than from this account. What mattered in that bay was not the exact number but the direction. A pulse that did not settle, a pressure that did not rise, and a patient who could not be sat up without going grey were the findings that mattered.

There was a short period in that emergency department when everybody was tempted to be reasonable. The registrar was tired, it was late, the patient was improving on the treatment, and the path of least resistance was to give the antiemetic a little longer, to let the observations settle, and to discharge a young woman with a leaflet about gastroenteritis. He was tempted, because a young woman with vomiting and a racing pulse is one of the commonest presentations in the building, and because the numbers can be explained by the vomiting, and that explanation is not wrong, it is simply incomplete. The nurse was tempted too, in a different way. She was tempted to let the second set of observations go in the bin and rely on the first, because the first had looked like a frightened young woman, because she had a queue of other patients, and because she had been taught that her job was to observe and report. Both temptations were reasonable, and both were the product of the same design: the observations are taken at a moment, a moment that is chosen can be chosen well or badly, and a chart that opens with a reassuring set of numbers tends to keep them unless somebody looks again.

What the nurse did was go back and do the thing the nursing notes had been doing all evening, which was to sit down with the patient and take a proper history, as an equal rather than as a form to complete. And what she found, when she went through it properly, was that the period had stopped before she changed jobs, and that the patient was certain she could not be pregnant because of the intrauterine device. The nurse said the sentence that ended the story of gastroenteritis: that a coil does not prevent pregnancy, and that a pregnancy which happens with a coil in place is much more likely to be in the tube rather than in the uterus. She asked for a pregnancy test while she was still talking, and she asked for it as though she expected it to be positive, and she told the registrar what she had found and what she thought, in that order, so that he could act while she kept talking to the patient.

The test came back positive, and the diagnosis of the next few minutes was not gastroenteritis at all. An intrauterine device makes a pregnancy much less likely overall, but it does not make it impossible, and the pregnancies that do occur with one in place are far more likely to be in the fallopian tube, where there is no room for them to grow and no way for them to grow safely. So the picture of a young woman with one-sided abdominal pain, vomiting, a fast pulse, a low blood pressure and a positive pregnancy test was an ectopic pregnancy until proven otherwise, and the proof was going to be the scan and the theatre. The nurse went to the registrar and said so, and the registrar stopped what he was doing, which was writing the discharge letter.

What changed in the next little while was the ordering of priorities, and the change was almost entirely a matter of what came first on the list. The patient was no longer a woman with gastroenteritis who might need a drip and a leaflet. She was a woman who was bleeding, and the bleeding needed to be found and stopped, and the findings that would guide the treatment were an ultrasound scan, which can show a pregnancy outside the uterus and can show fluid in the abdomen, and a haemoglobin, which had come back at 8.1 grams per deciliter, well below the range for a young woman. That haemoglobin is an illustration of the reasoning and not a threshold to be acted on in the abstract, and the value that should trigger action is set out in the current guideline and the local protocol.

What the team did next was resuscitation, which is a set of decisions about what to do while the diagnosis is being confirmed, and the decisions were made in parallel rather than in sequence. She was moved to a place where she could be watched, a wide bore cannula was put into a vein in each arm, blood was taken for cross-matching and for a full blood count and an arterial blood gas, and the surgery team was called rather than informed. Blood transfusion was prepared and begun, because a woman with a haemoglobin of that level who is tachycardic and clammy has lost blood and needs volume and red cells and not only salt and water. The anticoagulation that a ruptured ectopic of this kind is treated with was given as well, and every one of these decisions rested on the finding in the nurse's observation rather than on a result that was still to come.

The operation was done without delay, because an ectopic pregnancy that has ruptured is a surgical emergency measured in minutes rather than in days, and the delay that matters is the delay before anybody decides to operate. The surgeon found blood in the abdomen and a pregnancy in the right fallopian tube that had torn, and the tube was removed and the bleeding was controlled. Her blood pressure came up on the ward as the volume was replaced, and her pulse came down, and the young woman who had looked like a stomach upset turned out to be a young woman who had nearly died. She had lost more blood than her body could replace quickly, and the observation chart that had been failing for hours was the only reason anybody knew that.

The recovery was run by the ward nursing team and it was slow in the way that recovery after emergency surgery for blood loss is slow, because the body has to make blood again and the iron has to be replaced. The pain was managed, the observations were repeated often, and she was helped to sit up in stages rather than being put upright in a chair, because the blood pressure would not hold otherwise. She asked repeatedly whether she was going to be all right and whether she was going to lose the job, and the nurses answered her honestly each time, and she slept for most of that day. The line that had been put in during the night stayed in only as long as it was needed and no longer, because a line in place is a route for infection and nothing about her treatment required it to stay.

Before she went home the nursing team taught her the things she needed to know, and the teaching was as important as the operation. She was taught to recognise the emergency signs of internal bleeding: severe abdominal pain, a shoulder that aches at the top, dizziness when she stands, a pulse that feels racing, and bleeding heavier than a period. She was taught to have a pregnancy test before taking any new medicine, and to tell any clinician that she might be pregnant before an operation or a scan, and she was taught that if she ever feels that something is wrong in the lower abdomen she is to be assessed the same day rather than waiting for a routine appointment. And she was taken through the reasoning of what had happened to her in plain words, because a woman who understands why the coil did not protect her is far more likely to use the information next time.

She was also told that the intrauterine device could be reviewed and replaced, and that if she wanted to become pregnant she should have it taken out and her contraception planned properly with the family planning service, and she was given an appointment and a telephone number for the ward she had been on. The specialist team saw her in the clinic and explained the follow-up, which is a blood test in the following weeks to be certain the pregnancy hormone is falling, a repeat pregnancy test to be certain she is not pregnant again, and a review of her abdomen. The hospital did not say much about how she had nearly died, and neither did she, and the conversation kept returning to the same two facts: the gap in her periods and the coil. What she took away was that the device had not failed, and that a pregnancy can happen with it in place, and that nobody had done anything wrong.

The general lesson is stated more sharply in this case than in most, because the teaching is not really about ectopic pregnancy at all. It is about the order of questions. No woman of reproductive age with abdominal pain and vomiting has gastroenteritis until a pregnancy test is in the notes, and that is not a rule about the diagnosis but a rule about the paperwork, because the notes are what the next person will read. The test is quick, it is cheap, it is not controversial, and it is the single question most likely to be the answer, and yet it is the one most often skipped in the rush of a busy night, because the story of vomiting in a young woman arrives already explained by everybody who has heard it before.

The second lesson is about the observation chart, which is usually the most boring document in the building and was, in this case, the most informative one. The observations said that this patient was not getting better, and the reason they could be trusted is that they were taken in a fixed way: the same measurements, in the same order, at set intervals, by people who wrote down what they saw rather than what they expected. A nurse who takes a proper history produces evidence, not paperwork. The questions she asked about the pain, and about the food, and about the bowels and the urine, and about the medicine she had taken, and about the operation she had had as a child, and about the possibility of a pregnancy, were the diagnostic instruments of this case, and the reason the case failed is that they were asked late.

None of this was a failure of care by any individual, and it is important to say so, because the case reads as a story about a woman who was careless and it is not. Dr Wetherby was reasoning correctly inside a frame that had been built by his own first question, and any team would have built that frame from the information he was given. The nurse was not slow; she was thorough in the end because she had a moment in which she was not interrupted. And the patient was not careless about her own body; she was twenty-four, she was embarrassed, she was tired, and she wanted to be told it was a stomach bug. What the service learned was not a lesson about anybody but about the shape of the pathway.

The change that came out of it was structural. Any woman of reproductive age who attends with abdominal pain and vomiting now has a pregnancy test requested before she is given a diagnosis of gastroenteritis, and the result is recorded in the notes before the discharge letter is written. The escalation triggers on the observation chart were rewritten to include the combination that mattered here: a fast pulse and a low blood pressure that do not settle with fluids in a patient with abdominal pain, and any patient who cannot be sat up without becoming grey. The nursing documentation was changed so that a long gap without menstruation is recorded as a finding rather than as a negative answer, and the paperwork no longer offers a box for a family history that will be filled in later. The interval that counts as a missed period, and the dates that should be asked about, are set out in the current guideline and the local protocol, and they should be checked rather than invented.

There is one more thing in this case that deserves to be said slowly, and it is about waiting. For several hours this woman sat on a trolley in a cubicle being treated for a stomach upset, with a line in her arm and a leaflet about rehydration, while blood was collecting in her abdomen. She was not neglected; she was attended to, the observations were taken, the staff were kind, and every one of those things was true and none of them was the thing that saved her. What saved her was a nurse who asked a question she was not required to ask, and who asked it while the rest of the team were busy with a patient who was more obviously ill. The lesson is that the questions nobody is required to ask are often the ones that change the diagnosis, and that a service is only as good as its willingness to let a nurse act on an answer nobody requested.

One more thing is worth recording about how this case ended. The nurse who asked the question was not a person who happened to be near the patient; she was a colleague whose judgment the team had learned to trust, and Dr Wetherby acted on what she said at once, without evidence, because he had watched her all evening and had seen her change her mind about this patient once already. That is how a nurse becomes part of the diagnosis rather than part of the delivery of it. The habits that made the difference were unglamorous: taking the history properly the first time, writing down what the patient said rather than what she was asked, asking the questions that were not on the form, and being willing to be the person who said the awkward thing out loud in a quiet voice.

To summarize, this case turned on a question that takes moments to ask and was not asked for hours, and on an observation chart that was telling everybody the diagnosis was wrong while everybody was reading it as dehydration. The patient presented with a story that fitted gastroenteritis almost perfectly, and the two facts that contradicted it, a long gap in her periods and an intrauterine device she believed made pregnancy impossible, were not withheld by her so much as never invited. The failure belonged to a frame and to a pathway rather than to any person, because the pathway had a well-built route for the common presentation and no route at all for the unusual one, and because the observations had no trigger that fitted what was actually in front of them. The nurse watched the treatment fail, went back and took the history properly, asked about the periods, and had the pregnancy test asked for while the patient was still talking. What followed was resuscitation, blood transfusion and emergency surgery for a ruptured ectopic pregnancy, and a recovery in which she was taught what to watch for and why the coil had not protected her. The lessons are that no woman of reproductive age with abdominal pain and vomiting has gastroenteritis until a pregnancy test is in the notes, that a coil does not prevent pregnancy, that the observations of a nurse who has taken a proper history are a diagnostic test in their own right, and that a treatment that fails without anybody being unkind is still a treatment that has failed. Every number spoken in this case is an illustration of reasoning and not a rule, and each one must be confirmed against the current guideline and the local protocol before it influences any real decision.

# Chapter 9 — The Swelling That Was Not Heart Failure

The first number Rhona Sinclair wrote on Derek Wharton's chart was his weight, and she wrote it on the same scale, in the same corner of the ward, at the same hour every morning, because a weight taken on two different scales on two different days is not a weight but an opinion. He was sixty-four, a retired electrician with thick forearms and a habit of standing very still while he waited for things to happen. His ankles had been swelling for three weeks, and climbing the stairs to his flat had left him breathless enough that he had started to take them one at a time. He came in on a Friday afternoon with a bag of his tablets and a letter from his general practitioner, and he apologised for the letter.

The case that follows is a constructed composite, assembled from patterns that wards see rather than from any one person's record, and no detail in it belongs to anyone living or dead. It is here because the swelling in it is the commonest kind there is, and because the commonest kind is precisely the kind that can hide a rarer and more serious illness inside it. The story is about a frame rather than about a pair of legs, a diagnosis that was reasonable when it was made and that everybody then read the world through. Every number spoken in it is an illustration of reasoning and not a fact to act on.

The letter from his general practitioner was two lines long and both of them were about the heart, and Wharton's own account to the registrar followed the letter more closely than he knew. His face was puffy in the way that a face is puffy in the morning and less puffy by the afternoon, though on this afternoon it was still puffy. The skin of his lower legs was stretched and shiny, and pressing his shin left a dent that filled in over several seconds, and the flesh at the base of his spine was dimpled in the same way. He had brought his walking shoes in a plastic bag because they would not close over his toes.

On the ward Dr Peter Naismith, the medical registrar, took the history and the observations himself: a blood pressure of 168 over 96 millimeters of mercury, a pulse of 84 beats per minute, a temperature of 36.8 degrees Celsius, and an oxygen saturation of 96 per cent in air. Those are the numbers your own service will read differently, and the line between a raised reading and an alarming one is drawn in different places and in different people, so treat every number spoken here as an illustration and check the current guideline and the local protocol before a decision is built on it. What mattered more was what the examination did not show, because the whole case turned on it. He arrived with a diagnosis attached to his name and did not leave the ward without it.

Wharton lay flat on the bed without asking for more pillows and without any difficulty breathing in that position, which is the first thing that argues against a heart that is failing him. There was no jugular venous pressure to see above his collarbone with him reclined at a low angle, the lungs were clear to the ear front and back, the heart sounds were regular and unremarkable, and there was no third sound and no crackling at the bases when he breathed deeply. His lips were pink rather than blue and his hands were warm, and he had no chest pain at all, which he mentioned twice and apologised for twice. None of this was written down as a finding against heart failure, because nobody was yet working against it.

Two other explanations were doing their quiet work in that room, and one of them is the reason this chapter exists. The first is kidney disease, and the dangerous form of it is nephrotic syndrome, in which the kidneys leak so much protein that the blood loses the substance that holds fluid inside the vessels, so the body fills with water while the vessels themselves run dry. The finding that would reveal it is urine that froths and will not settle, and a quantity of protein in the urine that no tired heart explains. The second is clot, because a patient losing protein in his urine is also losing the protein that stops blood clotting, and that would be revealed by a sudden painful swelling in one calf or by breathlessness arriving in minutes rather than weeks.

On the Saturday morning the team met in the small side room off the ward, and the consultant cardiologist, Dr Alison Trevelyan, did most of the talking. Her reasoning was careful and, at the time, entirely reasonable. The commonest cause of new swelling in both legs in a man of sixty-four is a heart that has stopped pumping effectively, and swelling of that kind is also what brings people to hospital breathless on stairs. He was not jaundiced and had no liver disease in his history, his legs carried no varicose veins or ulceration to suggest a purely local venous cause, and the letter named no medicine that would hold salt, so the differential she took the team through was heart failure ranked first, kidney disease second, liver disease third and venous disease fourth.

The dangerous end of that list, though, was not the same as the top of it, and Dr Trevelyan said so aloud. A patient who is breathless because his pump is failing can be managed within hours, and a patient who is breathless because his blood has turned thin as water and his kidneys are failing him needs a different answer within days. The difference between those two answers is worth several days of thought at the start of the week, and it was given. She asked for the urine to be sent for protein and for the albumin in the blood to be measured, the team agreed, and then the argument moved on to the more interesting question of what to do about the water in his legs.

Those results came back over the next two days. The full blood count was unremarkable apart from a haemoglobin a little below the lower limit of the laboratory's reference range, the urea and creatinine were within limits, and the urine dipstick was strongly positive for protein. The chest radiograph showed a heart of normal size, clear lung fields and no fluid in the pleural spaces, and the electrocardiogram, or ECG, showed no acute change. None of those results was alarming in isolation and each was reasonable to have asked for, and the registrar, who had a queue of other patients, read them and moved on to the next one.

The working diagnosis became congestive heart failure with fluid retention, and on the strength of it the team began the treatment that any ward in any country would begin, a diuretic, a salt restriction, a fluid restriction and a daily weight. Dr Trevelyan was explicit with the patient about why, because the reasoning was worth hearing out loud. His kidneys were holding on to water because the heart was not pushing it forward, and the way to shift fluid out of the tissues was to make the kidney let it go, and the way to know whether that had happened was to weigh him every morning before breakfast. Nobody disagreed with any part of that.

Rhona Sinclair was the registered nurse assigned to him, and she took the treatment seriously in the sense that she took the measurements seriously. She weighed him before breakfast in the vest and trousers he had arrived in, on the ward scale that was wheeled into the corner because the bathroom scale disagreed with it by more than a kilogram, and on the first morning he weighed ninety-four kilograms. She measured and recorded his urine, she kept the strict fluid balance chart that nobody had specifically asked her for, and she marked a point on each shin with a pen so that the circumference would be measured in the same place every morning, in the same way, by the same person. She also began a list of what he had been able to do before he came in, which she kept beside the chart and which turned out to matter later.

The rest of the nursing care was less numerical and more exacting. She taught Wharton to use a soft toothbrush and to report any blood in his mouth, which matters more than it sounds when the face is swollen and the gums are pushed up by the fluid. She sat with him at the first meal and went through what the kitchen would send and what it would not, and she noticed that he was a man who had spent his life repairing things and who found being told not to eat crisps an insult rather than an instruction. She wrote down what he said about his breathing in his own words, because she had learned that the words a patient uses carry more than the observations being charted.

By the third day he was no better. The leg circumference at the marked point had not fallen by even the small amount it usually falls, the pitting at the base of his spine was unchanged, his face was fuller than it had been on Friday, and the sacral pad had spread. He was passing less urine than he had been passing on the ward, his weight had drifted down by two kilograms where five were expected over the first few days of a diuretic, and at ninety-two kilograms he was still three kilograms above the weight he had carried in his trousers a month before he became ill. The expected weight loss moves with the patient, the agent and the laboratory, so take it as an illustration of reasoning and confirm what your own service uses against the current guideline and the local protocol.

The team was frustrated, and the frustration was reasonable, because a diuretic that does not shift fluid in a patient with fluid retention is either the wrong diuretic or the wrong diagnosis, and every person on the ward round had a view about which. The registrar wanted to increase the dose and add a second agent. The consultant wanted to establish that he was not quietly eating something salty in the day room, and she was right to want that checked. What nobody said aloud was the other possibility, which was that the fluid was not where the team thought it was, and that if it was in the blood vessels rather than in the tissues, then pushing it out of the vessels would empty them instead of filling them.

There is a particular kind of quietness that settles over a ward when a treatment is not working, and it deserves naming, because that is where blame usually grows. It is tempting to believe that the patient is not taking the treatment properly, that he has eaten a whole packet of crisps in the afternoon, that he has been hiding his fluid intake, and that the next step is a conversation about adherence with a man too tired to argue back. That explanation is attractive because it puts the fault in the bed, because it is easy to check, and because it is available before the alternative has been thought through at all. Nobody on the team was being unkind when they wanted it, and the process gave them a tidy place to put the difficulty.

What Rhona Sinclair noticed was not in the notes, or rather it was in the notes and had been read past. She had been emptying his urine for four days without thinking much about it, and on the third evening she looked at the bedpan after she had tipped it and saw a layer of white foam sitting on the surface that would not go away. She ran a little water into the empty pan and did the same thing with the water, and the water did not foam at all, and the difference was the answer. She wrote in the nursing record that the urine was frothy and that the froth had not settled on standing, and on the ward round the next morning she mentioned it in passing and was answered with the observation that his heart was not good and the water would follow.

She went back through her own notes for the week, and the picture they made was not the picture being described on the medical ward round. His neck veins had never been distended. His chest had been clear every time she had listened to it. His blood pressure had stayed high and had not fallen as the diuretic did its work, which is not what a heart under strain and being unloaded usually does. He had slept flat on two pillows from the first night and had never asked to be raised, and his lips had been pink every single time she had looked at them. And on the second day he had said, unprompted, that he had never been breathless in his life until this summer, in the tone of a man not yet sure whether the information mattered.

She took the problem to the ward round on the fifth morning, and she took it to Dr Naismith first, because she had a clear view of where her own job ended and his began. He listened with the particular expression of a clinician who has been awake since five in the morning and is being told that the answer he has been giving for four days is wrong. She said that she could not make the heart fit the picture, that his heart was not enlarged and had never sounded unwell, that he was not short of breath lying down, and that his urine had carried protein and froth since the day he arrived. He said that he wanted to believe her, and that he was going to go and look again.

Dr Trevelyan came back that afternoon and did the thing that turns a ward round into a diagnosis, which was to read the notes as a stranger. The protein in the urine was heavier than anything a tired heart explains, the albumin in the blood was low enough to account for the swelling by itself, and the radiograph had said in plain words that the heart was of normal size and the lungs were clear. The consultant asked for the urine protein to be quantified and the blood film to be reviewed, the registrar asked for a renal opinion, and both requests were made that afternoon rather than the following week. That was the shape of the correction, not a revelation but a re-reading.

The results arrived the next morning and they were not subtle. The urine protein was many times the amount used to define nephrotic syndrome in most laboratories, the albumin in the blood was low enough to explain the swelling on its own, and the estimate of kidney filtering function, though still recorded as adequate, was the lowest it had ever been for him. Every one of those numbers moves with the laboratory, the method and the moment at which it was taken, so confirm the values and the cut-offs in use against the current guideline and the local protocol rather than against a remembered number. The registrar wrote the diagnosis into the notes that afternoon, and then sat down to think about what to do with it.

Once the diagnosis was named, the shape of the previous five days suddenly made sense, and it made sense in the wrong direction. In nephrotic syndrome the volume inside the vessels can be very low while the body looks swollen, because the protein that holds fluid in the vessels has gone out in the urine and the fluid has left the vessels for the tissues. A diuretic given to such a patient takes salt and water out of the blood and can push the kidney into holding on even harder, so the swelling of the legs goes down a little while the dangerous emptiness inside goes further. The team's next instinct had been to push harder, and it would have been the most dangerous thing available to them.

The renal physician, Dr Tolu Adeyemi, came the same day and took the fluid problem away from the heart team, and the first thing she changed was the assumption that too much fluid was the only problem. The volume inside the vessels was expanded deliberately with albumin rather than taken away with more diuretic, the diuretic was kept but put under review, and the salt and fluid restrictions were relaxed rather than tightened, because a man who is thirsty and salt-starved is a man whose kidneys will cling harder to what he has. She asked about the anticoagulant protein as well, since a patient who loses it is a patient at risk of a clot in a leg vein or in the renal veins. And she asked who had read the chest radiograph, because that film had been in the file since Friday and had been read once.

A renal biopsy was arranged and carried out, and it showed the pattern of membranous nephropathy, in which the filters of the kidney are damaged in a way that lets protein escape. It is one of the causes of nephrotic syndrome that appears in a man of this age with no other illness to account for it, and it was therefore a genuine answer rather than a label. The immune process behind it was then treated under the renal team with immunosuppression, a decision made jointly and explained to Wharton carefully, because the word frightens people and because the person who takes the tablets is the one who lives with the consequences. The biopsy was not the end of the work but the beginning of it, because why his immune system had decided to damage his kidneys was still open and still needed an answer.

The swelling did not go quickly and nobody expected it to. Over the following weeks the weight came down by grams rather than by kilograms, the leg circumference at the marked point fell steadily, the pitting at the sacrum softened and then went, and his face flattened out at last. His urine output rose, the froth went from the surface of the urine, and the climb up the stairs at home became a walk he could manage without stopping. Nobody called it a cure, and the nursing team wrote in the notes that the daily weight was still the number that told them whether any of it was true.

Before he went home, Rhona Sinclair went through the things that would bring him back, and she went through them with him at the bedside with the curtains drawn and the television off. He was to watch his weight and report a rapid rise, and he was to report a swollen calf or a sudden pain in the chest, because the risk of a clot was real in this disease and was not something he had been carrying before. He was to have his blood tested at intervals to follow the kidney function and the albumin, and to telephone the ward rather than wait to be seen. The nursing team changed one thing about the ward itself, which was that the scale in the corner was never moved again.

The consultant wrote to the general practitioner and to the ward about what had happened and why, and the writing was unusually careful about blame. Nothing here was a mistake by a careless person at the bedside, but a reasonable diagnosis doing what reasonable diagnoses do, which is to explain everything that arrives afterwards. Swelling in a man of sixty-four is heart failure until the chest says otherwise, and the chest had said otherwise from the first hour. The correction was available to any team that had stopped to read the chart with the patient's chest in mind, and the fact that it was not taken at the time says something about ward rounds rather than about the people on them.

There is a second question here, and its answer is what made the correction right rather than merely different, namely that the treatment was changed to match the mechanism instead of the appearance. The appearance was swollen legs, and the mechanism was the loss of protein through the kidneys, with a fall in the albumin that holds fluid inside the vessels and a fall in the circulating volume that the body then tries to replace by holding on to salt. Treating the appearance means more diuretic, and in this situation more diuretic makes the circulation emptier and the kidney more stubborn. Treating the mechanism means replacing what was lost, protecting the kidney, and dealing with the immune process that is doing the damage.

The wider lesson is about frames. Once the team had decided that this was a heart, every later observation was read as a heart observation: the breathlessness was the heart, the water in his legs was the heart, the failure of the diuretic was the heart, and even the fact that his blood pressure was high was a fact about the heart. Nothing in that reading was dishonest and nothing in it was stupid, which is exactly what makes it dangerous. A frame is not a set of opinions a person holds but a set of expectations about what will be seen next, and a clinician who is not looking for the diagnosis is not looking for the evidence against it.

The daily weight deserves a sentence of its own, because it is the most useful single number on a ward and very few wards treat it that way. It is the only measurement that says something about the whole patient at once, it costs nothing, it takes a minute, and it is the number that says whether the treatment is working before anything else does. On this ward it was being used as a chart that got filled in, and it took the nurse asking for the daily change rather than the daily number, and asking why it had not come down to where it ought to be, to make it a measurement again. That line on the chart, with its small fall and its stubborn plateau, was the thing the team eventually read, and it was read after four days of arguing about the dose.

Wharton went home with kidneys that had been damaged by a process nobody had looked for, and with a set of tablets he did not enjoy, and understanding more about his own body than he had ever understood before. Rhona kept in touch with him for a while, as she did with the patients whose numbers she had followed closely, and she asked him about his weight and his ankles and his breathing on the telephone in a way that nobody had ever asked him before. It is easy to imagine a ward as a place where a machine manufactures a decision, and this was not that. It was a place where a group of people, including one who had noticed something odd about a bedpan, argued with a set of numbers until the numbers agreed with the patient.

To summarize, Derek Wharton's swelling was never a heart problem, and the heart was a reasonable answer that the whole team then read the world through. The failure was not in the diuretic and not in the ward round and not in any person; it was in a frame that turned every later observation into a confirmation of itself. A clear chest with a big heart is not the only chest, frothy urine is a bedside finding that costs nothing, and a weight that stays high while fluid is pulled out of the tissues is telling you that the fluid was never where you thought it was. The nurse who emptied the bedpan noticed, wrote it down in words, and said so at the wrong end of a busy week, and the diagnosis changed because somebody read the chart. Every number spoken here is an illustration of reasoning and must be checked against the current guideline and the local protocol, because the values differ between laboratories, between countries and between editions, and the patient in front of you is not one of the illustrations.

# Chapter 10 — The Bruises That Were Not Bruises

The bruise on Lisa Nowak's shin had no story attached to it, which was the first thing the nurse noticed and the last thing the team addressed. It sat on the front of her right shin, the size of a hand, and it carried the colours a bruise goes through when it is a week old, with yellow at the edges and purple and green in the middle, and no injury on her body that could account for it. Around it, and on her forearms and in the creases of her elbows, were the small purple spots, each the size of a pinhead, that she had found in the bath a fortnight earlier and decided were the sign of a tired teacher who needed a holiday. Three days ago the headache had started, and it has not let up since.

This case is a constructed composite, put together from patterns of illness rather than from any one person's record, and it describes no real patient and identifies nobody living or dead. It is here because the illness inside it is one of the great imitators in medicine, borrowing the appearance of a far simpler condition and paying it back with interest. It is also here because the mistake it describes was not a failure of attention but a failure of organisation, with four findings belonging to a single disease divided among three specialties, each attended to competently by the people who owned it. The chapter is about what happens when a team is right about one thing and wrong about what the whole picture means.

She was thirty-four and taught in a secondary school, and she came in on a Thursday evening with her mother driving because she had been too frightened to drive herself. She was a small, pale, watchful woman, and the bruises came in two sizes. The small ones were the purple spots, each the size of a pinhead, and they were on her forearms, her elbows, her ankles and the insides of her wrists, and they did not fade when she pressed them. There was also a blood blister on the inside of her lower lip and a tender patch on the hard palate, and the nurse found both by asking her to open wide and say ah, and neither had been mentioned.

Her observations on arrival were a temperature of 36.9 degrees Celsius, a pulse of 96 beats per minute, a blood pressure of 106 over 68 millimeters of mercury and a respiratory rate of 18 breaths a minute. She was afebrile and had no infection anywhere that anyone could find, which mattered, because every one of those numbers will be read differently by a different service and a different laboratory, and the thresholds that separate a normal observation from an alarming one move with age and with the population. Take the numbers as illustrations of reasoning and verify them against the current guideline and the local protocol before you build anything on them. What the nurse wrote in the record was that she was frightened, and more frightened than her words suggested.

The history was taken by the nurse before the registrar arrived, and it took twenty minutes because she asked about the shape of the bleeding rather than the amount of it. The bruising had begun two weeks before and had spread, and she had been unusually tired for a month before that, and she had a cold with a sore throat three weeks before any of it, which she had thought nothing of. Her periods had been heavier and longer for two cycles, and she had put that down to stress at work. She also said, without being asked, that her urine had looked dark and orange in the morning and that she had assumed she was not drinking enough water, and the nurse wrote it down because it was the only thing nobody had thought to ask about.

Two things had to be named at that point, and both were named. The first was the bleed inside the head, because if her platelets are low enough to bruise her into a hand-sized bruise on her shin, then the thing that will kill her is a haemorrhage in the brain, and the findings that would reveal it are a headache that changes its character, drowsiness, confusion, a weakness in an arm or a fit. The second was that a purpuric illness with a low platelet count is not always a platelet illness at all, and if hers were a disease of the small vessels in which platelets are consumed rather than destroyed, it would show up as anaemia with red cells torn apart, as fever, as confusion and as failing kidneys. Every one of those was written down that evening as something to watch for, and every one of them appeared.

The haematologist, Dr Samira Barzani, came and saw her within the hour, and the registrar, Dr James Thornbury, had already started a list. The most likely diagnosis in a well woman with this pattern was immune thrombocytopenia, in which the body makes antibodies against its own platelets and the count falls low enough to bleed, and the recent viral illness fitted the story of a trigger. The alternatives that had to be excluded were a marrow failure or a leukaemia, which would show a very different blood count, and a disorder of the clotting system, which would show in the clotting tests. The list was sensible, the reasoning was audible, and by the end of the first hour the working diagnosis was immune thrombocytopenia.

Dr Farouk asked for a full blood count, a blood film, a direct antiglobulin test, the clotting tests, kidney and liver function, and she asked for the film to be examined by a person rather than by an automated counter, and she said why. If the red cells were being broken up by something inside the patient, she said, the film would show it, and she wanted the film read by somebody who knew what she was asking about. The count came back with the platelets profoundly reduced and the haemoglobin lower than it should have been in a woman with bruises but not otherwise striking, and the film report arrived before midnight. She wrote her reasons into the notes beside each request, which is what a request is for.

The report read, red cell film shows marked variation in size and shape with numerous fragmented red cells, occasional polychromasia, no nucleated red cells seen, no blasts seen. The registrar read the middle of that sentence and was reassured by it, because no blasts means no leukaemia, and no leukaemia means a platelet problem, and a platelet problem has a treatment. Nobody on the team that night had any reason to think that a young woman with a viral illness could have a platelet problem and something else as well, and the report was signed and filed in the usual way. It was the document in the file that held the answer, and it held it in its second sentence, and nobody read it as an answer because nobody had asked the question.

The treatment was a corticosteroid with bleeding precautions and a plan to repeat the platelet count, and the reasoning was explained to the patient, who wanted to understand it. In most patients the count rises within a few days of a corticosteroid, so the response was expected to be both the treatment and the test of the diagnosis, and if the count did not move then the diagnosis was in doubt. The bleeding precautions were taught rather than issued: a soft toothbrush, no injections into the muscle, no rectal temperatures, no contact sports, nothing that might bump her, and a padded rail at the side of the bed. She was asked to keep a diary of any bleeding, however small.

The nurse who took her admission was Tricia Mahon, and she took the treatment seriously in the sense that she took the precautions seriously and wrote down what she saw in words rather than ticks. She began a neurological observation chart, because a headache of this kind with a very low count has to be watched for changes in consciousness, and she recorded alertness, orientation, the pupils, and the power in each arm and leg. She measured and charted the urine output, because a woman with a very low platelet count can bleed into herself and because a falling urine output is the first sign of many things that matter. She took her temperature four times a day and did not like the shape of that chart, though she said nothing about it at the time.

She also spent time on the things nobody charts. She asked what Nowak had been doing in the two weeks before she came in, and found a school trip with fourteen-year-olds, boxes carried up a corridor, and an ibuprofen taken more often than it should have been for the headache, which the nurse asked her to stop at once because it makes platelets less active and it can damage the stomach lining. She told her plainly that a headache that changed, blood in the urine or the bowel, or any new weakness was to be reported at once, and that she should not decide at two in the morning that it was nothing. She asked what she had been told by the other team, and Nowak mentioned that someone had asked her about a rash, and she had no rash.

The improvement everyone was waiting for did not arrive. On the second day the platelet count came back and it had barely moved, and Dr Farouk said that was not unusual this early and that she would look again. On the third day Nowak was nauseated in the morning and her headache had gone from severe to unbearable, and she said that bright lights bothered her and that she could not bear the television on. She was sick twice, and the nurse recorded all of it, and she recorded that she was oriented to person but not to place.

By the fourth day she was febrile at 38.7 degrees Celsius, her headache was worse than it had ever been, she was intermittently confused, and she had passed four hundred millilitres of urine in the whole of the previous day. A temperature of that height, a urine output of that size and a change in consciousness are judged against thresholds that differ from service to service and from patient to patient, and the cut-offs and the actions attached to them must be confirmed against the current guideline and the local protocol rather than carried in the memory. What the nurse wrote in the notes that afternoon was that Nowak was not simply unwell, she was different, and that the difference had a shape. The nurse also increased the frequency of the observations.

And then the case split into three, which is what it did in every ward that has ever seen this illness, and none of the three parts was wrong. The headache and the confusion went into the haematology pile, because a headache with a very low platelet count might be a bleed, and the haematologist had said so. The fever went into the pile of things that happen to people on corticosteroids, which is a real thing, and the confusion with it, because a corticosteroid can alter a person's mood and their sleep. The failing kidneys went to the renal team as a volume problem, because she had been vomiting and had not been drinking, and because a small urine output after three days of vomiting is what volume depletion looks like.

Each of those three explanations was defensible, and that was the problem, because a defensible explanation for each of three findings is not a defence of the patient. Nobody holding one piece of that picture could be blamed for the piece they were holding. From the inside, the ward round felt like an ordinary morning in which three sensible people were each doing their own job well. The temptation was to act on all three at once, to raise the corticosteroid because the count had not moved, to give an antibiotic for the fever, to increase the fluid for the kidneys, and to call the neurologist because of the confusion, and every one of those steps was reasonable and every one of them delayed the only step that mattered.

Tricia Mahon was the one who noticed the things that did not fit. The urine Nowak passed on the fourth morning was the colour of dark tea with a pink tinge in it, and when the nurse asked her to describe it she said it had been like that for a fortnight and that she had said so on the night she came in and had been told it was probably because she was not drinking enough. Nowak was paler than she had been on Friday and the colour had gone out of her lips, and she answered questions readily but lost the thread in the middle of them and then began again from the start without appearing to notice. The nurse wrote it all in words, and wrote that the patient was different from the patient of two days before.

The nurse went back to the file. The film report from the first night was a single sheet in the notes, and she read the whole of it rather than the part that had been quoted. The second half of the sentence about the red cells said that there were numerous fragmented red cells and no nucleated red cells, and the word fragments sat there in a report that nobody had read as a finding. She rang the haematologist and asked her to look at the film again, and she asked a question, which was the question the report had been written to answer and nobody had asked: were the red cells fragmented, and did anyone know why.

The answer to that question turned the case round inside an hour. A red cell that is fragmented has been pulled through a narrowing or a strand of fibrin, and a patient whose platelets are low, whose red cells are being torn, whose temperature is high, whose mind is going and whose kidneys are failing has one illness and not four, and the name of that illness is thrombotic thrombocytopenic purpura. Dr Farouk came to the ward and said the words out loud, and there was a silence on the round, and then Dr Thornbury said that the film had been in the notes since the first night. That sentence was not an accusation, it was the sound of a team discovering that the answer had been available in writing since the first night.

The treatment that changes everything is plasma exchange, and the reason to begin it on the day the diagnosis was made rather than after more tests is that this illness kills quickly and the treatment works quickly. Removing the plasma and replacing it removes the antibodies that are damaging the vessel walls and replaces the missing enzyme that keeps those walls intact, and the decision was made on the combination rather than on any single number, because no single number identifies this illness and the combination identifies it well. The corticosteroid was continued, further treatment aimed at the antibodies was arranged, and the cause of the vessel damage was to be looked for rather than assumed. The decision was made at the bedside with the family present.

The work that followed was mostly nursing work, and the nurse ran it. She arranged the central line and cared for it, she kept the balance of volume going in and coming out, and she recorded both with the same attention she had given the fluid balance chart on the first night. She managed the reactions that donor plasma produces, which include shivering, a rise in temperature and a rash during the exchange, and she managed the fear, which was not irrational, since the patient could feel her blood running through a machine and had no way of knowing that this was the treatment working. She also did the small things that keep the night staff from being frightened, which was to explain before every change and to write down what she had explained.

The response was not a miracle and it was not gradual. She needed the intensive care unit, or ICU, for two nights while the exchanges were established and running, and then she went back to the ward with a plan and a schedule. The count rose over the following days, the haemoglobin rose with it, the temperature came down to normal and stayed down, the confusion lifted, and the urine output came back to something she could measure. Each of those was watched for separately, because in this illness the count can rise while the patient is still dying, and the neurological state and the urine output are the two things that say whether the patient is actually better or only looks better in a blood test.

What caused the damage to the small vessels was investigated later, because it has to be looked for, and in many people it is a medicine, an autoimmune process or an infection, and in some it cannot be found at all. The medicine history was taken again in full, because a drug is the commonest cause that can be found. The follow-up was built around the fact that this illness comes back, and the intervals and the definitions of remission differ between services and between editions of the guidance, so confirm them against the current guideline and the local protocol rather than trusting a remembered number. Nowak was given a card stating what she had had and what to do if the headache, the fever and the bruising came back together, because those three together are the warning, and she was told to telephone rather than to wait.

The teaching in this case is simple to state and hard to remember under pressure. Purpura with fever, confusion and failing kidneys is one disease and not four, and the moment those three arrive in the same patient the list of diagnoses should get shorter rather than longer. A low platelet count explains a bruise, and it does not explain a fever, and it does not explain confusion, and it does not explain a rising creatinine, and the moment a count is used to explain a finding that it cannot explain, the count has stopped being a finding and become a frame. None of the parts of this illness is dramatic on its own, and all of them together are unmistakable to anybody willing to hold them together.

The process failure was structural, and it is worth being exact about it, because it will happen again tomorrow on some ward. The platelet count belonged to the haematology pile, the fever belonged to the steroid, the kidneys belonged to the renal team, and the headache belonged to everybody and therefore to nobody in particular, and a patient with one illness is safe only while one person is holding the whole of it. Nothing here required anybody to be cleverer or more thorough than they already were. It required somebody to say out loud that these four findings had arrived together in the same person in the same week, and the structure of the ward did not invite anybody to say it.

The second lesson is about the blood film, and it is a lesson about reports rather than about laboratories. That report contained the answer in its second sentence and it also contained the sentence that made everybody relax, and the two sat a few words apart on the same line. A report is written to answer the question that was asked of it, and this one had been asked, in effect, whether there were blasts, because the patient had bruising and leukaemia was the thought that came to mind. Nobody had asked whether the red cells were fragmented, so the answer to that question was written down in a report and never read as an answer by anybody.

What the nurse did was not clever, and that is the point of including it. She read the whole of a report instead of the reassuring line inside it, she asked a colleague a specific question rather than a general one, and she said the sentence that joined the four findings together when she brought them back to the round. Each of those is available to any nurse on any ward, and none of them requires a grade or a title or a permission, and together they turned a case round in the time it takes to read one sheet of paper twice. She did not do it because she was clever, she did it because the patient was different and she had said so in writing and nobody had taken her up on it for three days.

So what should have happened on the second morning, when the count had not moved and the headache had begun? Not a bigger corticosteroid and not a conversation about adherence, but a return to the beginning: what else could produce bruising, a very low count, a fever, confusion and a falling urine output in a woman of thirty-four, and what single test would separate the answers. That question takes two minutes to ask, and it is the question the ward round did not ask, because asking it meant admitting that the diagnosis might be wrong, and admitting that in front of a patient is one of the harder things in this job. Nobody on that round had asked a wrong question, and nobody had asked the right one.

Nowak spent the best month of her year on a hospital ward, frightened and itchy and frustrated, with a rash of corticosteroid across her face and a headache she could not get rid of. She had been a teacher three weeks before and she was a patient for most of a month, and the part she remembers is not the plasma exchange but the first night, when a nurse she had only just met sat down on the end of the bed and asked her to describe the colour of her urine, and then asked what the doctors had said about it. Somebody had taken her seriously very early, and in the end that was the thing that turned the case. Most of that month was spent waiting for somebody to put four findings together.

The service changed two things afterwards. The first was that any patient admitted with an unexplained low platelet count and any other unexplained symptom now has a single senior clinician named, so that the findings have one owner rather than three. The second was that a blood film in this situation is reviewed by a person for the features the history suggests, and the report must state what was looked for, so that a report can no longer answer a question nobody asked. Neither change is dramatic and both were cheap, and both came out of a case in which the answer had been in the file the whole time.

To summarize, Lisa Nowak had one disease and the team had four, and the four were each attended to by competent people reasoning correctly inside a frame that was wrong. The failure was not a missed examination or a careless drug chart; it was the splitting of a syndrome across three specialties, so that nobody held the whole patient at once, and it was a blood film that answered a question nobody had thought to ask. A low platelet count explains a bruise and nothing else, and purpura with fever, confusion and failing kidneys is one diagnosis rather than four, and a team that reads those findings together will reach the right answer days earlier. The nurse who noticed the urine, then read the whole of a report and asked one precise question, is the reason this case turned at all, and she did it with no more equipment than a bedpan and a sheet of paper. Every number quoted here is an illustration of reasoning only and must be checked against the current guideline and the local protocol of the service where you work, because the thresholds and the timings are not the same in every country, every laboratory and every edition, and this is educational material rather than a protocol.

# Chapter 11 — The Acidosis That Was Called Sepsis

The emergency department at half past four in the afternoon had a drip stand, a hard chair and a man in it, and the first thing Miriam Pemberton noticed was not the chart on his knee but his breathing. He breathed in through his nose, down and slow, and out through his mouth, longer than the way in, and he did it with a concentration that made her stop and look twice. Gordon Mellor was sixty-eight, a retired printer who had come in by car with a plastic bag of medicines in his lap because he had been told to bring them. He had been sick for three days and could not keep anything down, had been short of breath since that morning, and said it in the flat voice of a man who has decided not to make a fuss. Whether three days of vomiting still counts as an acute illness is a question for the current guideline and the local protocol rather than for a story, and on that afternoon both readings of him were still open. The reason this chapter is worth telling is not the man but the word that formed around him within the hour, because sepsis was the right word for the ward and the wrong word for the man.

What follows is a constructed composite and it does not describe a real patient. No detail in it identifies anyone living or dead, and the events have been assembled to make one piece of reasoning visible. The piece is the moment when a reasonable diagnosis stops being a hypothesis and becomes a frame, and everything the patient says after that moment is read through it. Mellor arrived with a frame already attached to him, because the frame was in his own history and in his own medicines, and neither of them was wrong, and both of them were being read in a hurry.

His record was a record of a chronic illness that nobody had looked at properly for a long time. He had had type 2 diabetes for twelve years and a chronic kidney condition of the kind that is written down as stable and reviewed in the way that stable things are reviewed. He had been taking metformin since the year he was diagnosed, and the drug had never once been the subject of a conversation that anybody could find in the notes. Those years carry no meaning of their own, they are simply the length of an unreviewed medicine in a man whose kidneys are no longer the ones he had when it was started, and the intervals your own service uses for reviewing a long-standing medicine are written in the current guideline and the local protocol.

At triage his temperature was 36.8 degrees Celsius, his pulse was 96 beats per minute and regular, his blood pressure was 128 over 74 millimeters of mercury, and his respiratory rate was 24 breaths a minute. He was alert and mildly uncomfortable rather than frightened, his mouth was dry, his tongue was coated, and his eyes were a little sunken. There was no rigor, no rash, and no confusion beyond the mild muddiness of a person who has been vomiting. Each of those figures is a reason rather than a fact, and the values that count as abnormal vary with the person and the place, so check them against the current guideline and the local protocol before anybody builds a decision on them.

The dipstick on his urine was positive for protein and for blood, and the sample was cloudy to the eye, which in a man with a chronic kidney condition and three days of vomiting is the sort of result a busy clinician reads as confirmation of whatever the clinician has already decided. His abdomen was soft and not tender, his chest was clear, and there was no obvious source of infection anywhere that anybody could find. He had no rash, no tenderness over either kidney, and nothing about him pointed at a source. Pemberton did that examination with him and wrote all of it down, and she wrote down the breathing as well, because she had been told on her first day that when the breathing does not fit the story you write down the breathing and not the story.

Two possibilities were missing from the list at that moment and should not have been. The first was a surgical abdomen, because three days of vomiting in a man of sixty-eight can hide an obstruction or a perforation, and the findings that would reveal it are a distended tender abdomen, a rigid quiet belly, and vomit that looks like old coffee, and none of those were present. The second was a metabolic cause of the deep breathing, and the finding that would have revealed it was sitting in front of her in the waiting area: a deep, slow, regular and deliberate pattern of breathing in a man whose temperature was normal. In sepsis that pattern belongs to a body compensating for a metabolic upset, and it is also the pattern of a body that is acidotic for a reason nobody has looked for.

The registrar on that shift was Samir Haldane, who had worked in the emergency department for three years and had the habit the nursing staff valued of saying out loud what he thought was most likely to be the reason the person in front of him might die. That habit would have saved this patient, and it did not, because what he said out loud was sepsis, and sepsis was in fact the most likely reason a man like this dies in an emergency department. When the shift changed and Julia Renwick arrived as the intensivist, she was handed a story that had already been written, and she read it, and it fitted the evidence she had been given. The frame arrived with the patient and nobody noticed it arrive.

Haldane built the differential aloud at the bedside, because that is how he worked and because the nursing staff had learned to listen. A urinary infection with an obstructed outflow came first, on the reasonable grounds that an older man with diabetes and cloudy urine is a common patient and the commonest thing is usually the right thing. Acute gastroenteritis was second, since three days of vomiting and nothing else fits it better, and then came a bowel obstruction, a chest infection, and a flare of his kidney condition. What none of them said aloud was that the vomiting, the dehydration and the tablets on his lap might together be producing a chemical problem rather than reflecting one, and the reason none of them said it is that the story had a name and the name was doing the work.

The working diagnosis was sepsis of urinary origin, and the reason for it was audible and reasonable. He was sixty-eight with diabetes and a raised white cell count, his urine was cloudy and contained blood, and a normal temperature in sepsis in an older person is common rather than reassuring, because many who die of it never have a fever at all. His lactate was raised at 3.9 millimoles per liter, and a lactate raised in a very unwell patient is read by almost everybody as evidence of the very illness being treated. The figures here are illustrations of how the reasoning went rather than facts to be acted on, and the thresholds your own laboratory uses should be read from the current guideline and the local protocol.

So the treatment began, and it began properly. He was given intravenous fluid, because a man who has vomited for three days and is passing little urine needs volume, and antibiotics were started, because if the diagnosis is right then delay is what kills people, and blood and urine were sent for culture, and a chest radiograph was requested, and a venous blood gas was taken. The nurse began a fluid balance chart, observations were set at four times an hour, a glucose was checked because he had diabetes, and the hourly urine output was measured into a jug that was emptied and recorded. Nothing about any of that was wrong, and it was what a well-trained team does when it believes it has sepsis, and the belief was reasonable and the treatment was appropriate to it.

The fluid balance chart was the other document that was telling the truth and being read as something else. Pemberton kept it because it was hers to keep, the way a nurse keeps a chart when nobody has told her to, with the columns in order and the totals at the foot of the day. What it showed on the first evening was a large volume going in and very little coming out, and beside it she wrote that his mouth was still dry after all that fluid and that the breathing had not changed. A chart showing input far exceeding output is often read as proof that a patient is still dehydrated, and here it was proof that his kidneys were not responding to fluid in the way healthy kidneys do.

What Pemberton could not put down was the breathing. She had seen it in the waiting area, and she saw it at eleven, and again at one, and again at four, and each time it was the same deep slow purposeful rhythm.
