There is a particular kind of tiredness that comes with a patient who has a diagnosis and a chart and a plan, because once the plan exists the observations become a matter of confirming it, and a nurse on a busy bay will fill in a chart that agrees with the plan without asking whether the plan agrees with the patient. She was not sure what she wanted to say at four in the morning, and she said nothing, and that was the whole of her part in it.

She can still describe the breathing precisely, because she described it that night in the nursing notes, and that is the only reason anybody reading this case can see what she saw. He breathed in through his nose with a slow count and out through pursed lips, and the out-breath was longer than the in-breath, and the movement of his chest was deep, deliberate and regular. It did not speed up when she came to the bedside and it did not stop when she left. There was no noise in it and no wheeze, and he was not using his shoulders or his neck to breathe, and he was not fighting for air the way people with sepsis fight for air, he was doing something closer to blowing out a candle, slowly and with control, over and over. She had seen that pattern before, in renal failure, and she had been taught that it is a body correcting its own chemistry.

He was no better in the morning, which is not in itself a failure, because nobody expects a man with sepsis to be well by morning, and his blood pressure had held all night and his temperature had not moved. What had changed was the second set of bloods, and the venous blood gas that was repeated because his breathing rate had not come down. The lactate had risen from 3.9 to 7.8 millimoles per liter, the pH had fallen to 7.14, the white cell count had barely moved from the value it had held overnight, and the urine culture was growing nothing so far. The first of those results was read as proof of uncontrolled sepsis, and it was the one result in the file that should have been read as a change of diagnosis, and the line between a raised lactate and a dangerous one is drawn in the current guideline and the local protocol rather than in this account.

The intensivist came in that morning and did what intensivists do, which is to look for the thing that will kill the patient soonest, and she found the same answers the registrar had found, because the answers were inside the frame. His blood pressure was 128 over 74 millimeters of mercury and his urine was still being made, and the lactate was high, and she began the calculation everybody begins, which is which antibiotic to change to and how many hours are left, and the pressures and rates she was weighing are the ones set out in the current guideline and the local protocol. There was a moment that afternoon when the registrar said the words that would have changed everything, and he said them as a joke, which was that this man was not going to die of a urinary infection, he was going to die of something nobody had thought of. Nobody asked him what he meant by that.

And it was Pemberton who asked the question, on the afternoon of the second day, when she went back through his plastic bag of medicines because she had run out of things to write on the chart. She did it as a routine, the way she would for any man of sixty-eight with diabetes, and read the labels out loud to him to check them, and then asked the question nobody had asked him in twelve years, which was when his metformin had last been reviewed. He told her it had never been reviewed, that it had been given to him when he was first found to have diabetes, and that he had seen the same doctor every year and had never once had blood taken. She wrote that down exactly as he said it, because a sentence in a patient's own words is worth more than a paragraph in a summary.

That afternoon she rang the practice and asked for his most recent results, and the ones they sent back were eleven months old, and they were already drifting in the wrong direction, and the note said the kidney function was stable and the medicine should continue unchanged. That eleven months is an illustration of how long a result can sit in a file unread, which is a gap in the system and not in anybody's care, and the intervals your own service sets for reviewing a long-standing medicine are written in the current guideline and the local protocol. She sat down with the chart on her knees and read the drug list again, and this time as a list of things the body had to clear. Metformin is cleared by the kidneys, and his kidneys were no longer the ones he had had when it was started.

The other thing she found was in her own handwriting on the nursing chart, and it was the reason she had not worried. His glucose had been normal every time it was checked, at 5.4 and 5.8 millimoles per liter, and she had read that as reassuring, because a normal blood sugar in a man with diabetes is what a well-controlled diabetic looks like. It was not reassuring at all, it was the drug doing what it does, and a blood sugar that stays normal in a man who has been vomiting for three days and passing little urine is being kept normal by something that is no longer being cleared. The values here are illustrations of how a number can be read backwards, and the ranges your laboratory uses should be confirmed against the current guideline and the local protocol.

The reason it was missed is worth saying plainly, because this case has no villain in it. The medication reconciliation on the second day listed metformin as one current drug among eleven, entered by a system that carried it forward from an earlier admission, and nobody read it, because a person reading it would have had to ask why a drug for diabetes was being continued in a man who was vomiting. Nobody asked why the metformin was still going, because the frame said the problem was an infection, and the frame said a drug for diabetes had nothing to do with an infection. The clue was not hidden, it was printed on the front of the chart in the drug list, in the same handwriting as every other drug, at the same size, on the same page.

The change began with a telephone call to the ward registrar, and then it began with a sentence written into the notes, which was that the metformin was being stopped. Everything that followed was a consequence of that sentence and nothing else. Pemberton had made the case in four sentences and she did not overstate it: a man whose kidneys have deteriorated, who has been vomiting for three days, who has a normal temperature and a normal blood sugar and a deep steady breathing pattern, and whose lactate has risen while his blood pressure has held. She was right about all of it and right for reasons she could not yet explain, and she said so, because a nurse who claims more than she knows has her next observation discounted.

Then the treatment changed, and it changed in the way treatment changes when a name changes. The metformin stopped, the acidosis was treated with intravenous fluid and with insulin, and the question of whether his blood should be filtered was raised early, which is a decision that has to be taken early and that many services cannot offer at any hour. The intensivist went looking for the reason his kidneys had been failing silently, and when the urine culture grew nothing the antibiotics were stopped, because continuing them would have kept the frame in place and done nothing for the man. The vomiting was treated and his fluid replaced carefully rather than pushed, and his diabetes was reviewed with a plan for the future that did not depend on a medicine his kidneys could not clear.

From the moment the drug stopped, the nursing notes changed character, and the ward noticed before the laboratory did. The respiratory rate came down over the afternoon, and Pemberton documented the change with the times, and she wrote that the out-breath was shorter and that he was able to speak in full sentences, which he had not been able to do since triage. She changed the frequency of the observations because the patient was improving rather than because a protocol told her to, and kept the fluid balance chart running because the fluid plan was now a delicate thing. Nothing about that appears on a chart as an intervention, and that is the point, because what changed was that the nurse was reading the patient as a question and not as a set of results.

He improved over the two days that followed in a way nobody had expected, because the treatment that changed him was not an antibiotic. His lactate came back into the normal range, his blood gas came right, his blood pressure never needed support, and by the second morning he was eating breakfast and complaining that the food was bland, which every ward receives as good news. The intensivist was clear that this was not luck: the pattern had a metabolic cause with a precipitant, the drug had been the fuel, the vomiting and the dehydration had been the spark, and once the drug stopped the body had been able to clear itself. The intervals over which such a recovery is expected are set out in the current guideline and the local protocol rather than in the length of this account.

What Pemberton watched for in the days that followed was not the lactate, which was somebody else's number, but the breathing and the record of it. She wrote a short note for the next nurse, and it said that the rate and depth of his breathing were the first things to change and the last to change back, and that a patient breathing deeply and steadily with a normal temperature has a story that has not been read yet. She also wrote that if his glucose was normal she should not be reassured, and why, because she had learned it on a Thursday and there was no reason to learn it twice. That is the whole of her contribution, and without it the treatment would have gone on in exactly the same shape for another two days.

The discharge plan was not a list of medicines, although the medicines were on it, and the list was shorter than it had been on any day in hospital. He went home with a plan for what to do when he became ill, which is a piece of paper saying what to do when you are vomiting and cannot keep your tablets down, and who to telephone, and when, and he was told to stop the metformin permanently and to have his diabetes managed by a different plan. He was also told, in plain words, that his illness had been caused by a medicine his body could no longer clear, and that the fault was not his. He was told it twice, once to him and once to his wife, because people frightened by a diagnosis need to hear that they are not the cause of it.

Two things changed outside the hospital as well. The general practice agreed to review the long-standing medicines of everyone over sixty-five and to check kidney function before renewing any medicine the kidneys clear, and the emergency department agreed that a medicine reconciliation on admission would be a conversation rather than a copy, which means somebody would ask the patient what he actually takes. Neither change would have saved him if the nurse had not asked about the metformin, and either would probably have caught the same case months earlier, and that is the honest way to describe what a system change is. It is not a wall against a mistake, it is a set of hands that happen to be holding the same piece of paper.

The teaching of the case is about the label, because sepsis is a label that stops people asking questions. It is a good label and a dangerous diagnosis to miss, and it is the correct first thought for a man with a raised white cell count and cloudy urine, and thinking of sepsis first is not the error in this case. The error is that once the label was attached it became the explanation for everything, including the breathing, the normal temperature, the normal blood sugar and the tablets in the bag, and each finding was folded into the sepsis rather than being asked about. A label is a tool for remembering, and a tool that has been picked up should be put down and looked at once in a while, and the moment to do that is the moment the patient's story stops fitting it.

There is a habit here that goes beyond the case, and it is the habit this book is built on. A treatment that fails is not a failed patient, it is information, and the person who does not improve has not disappointed anybody and has corrected them instead. Mellor was given fluid, antibiotics and observations, all of them were appropriate, all of them failed, and the failure was not in the treatment but in the frame that had chosen the treatment. The nurse who watched that failure did nothing dramatic: she read the drug chart and asked a question, and the question was the whole of the treatment.

There was no moment in this story when anybody was unkind, inattentive in the way that makes a story easy to tell, or wrong in a way that could be shown. Haldane thought sepsis and he was right to think it. Renwick looked for the thing that would kill him soonest and she looked in the places the frame told her to look. The nurse saw something she could not name and wrote it down and said nothing, and then, on the second afternoon, said something, and medicine is not a series of clever people being careless and it is not a series of careful people being lucky, it is a series of people reasoning correctly inside frames.

To summarize, Gordon Mellor was treated for sepsis when what he had was lactic acidosis caused by a medicine his kidneys could no longer clear, and the sepsis was a reasonable diagnosis made competently and then allowed to explain everything that followed. The evidence that gave the game away was in front of the team from the first hour: a normal temperature, a normal blood sugar, a blood pressure that held, and a white cell count that barely moved. The deep steady breathing pattern was there too, and nobody read it as what it was until a nurse wrote it down and then went back to the drug chart. Every number in this account is an illustration of reasoning and must be checked against the current guideline and the local protocol before it is used to act, and the teaching is that sepsis is a label that stops people asking questions, and that a deep steady breathing pattern with a normal temperature deserves the drug chart read again.

# Chapter 12 — The Woman Who Would Not Sit Still

Dr Anwen Pryce has seen a great many women of forty-five in her room, and most of them arrive with a word they have brought from home or from the internet, and the word is usually one it is a relief to be wrong about. Ruth Kelleher came with two. She was forty-five, she administered a school office, and she sat down with a folded page on which she had written the things she wanted to be asked about, the first line being the word anxiety. She had six weeks of palpitations, a tremor in her hands, intolerance of heat, and a weight loss of seven kilograms that she had been explaining to herself as the effect of being busy. She said the word anxiety and Dr Pryce agreed with it, because the woman looked anxious and everything she described could be described by that word. The trouble with a word that fits everything is that it will also fit everything that comes later.

The case that follows is a composite assembled for teaching, and it does not describe anybody who has ever been a patient. No detail in it identifies anyone living or dead, and the case has been assembled to make one piece of reasoning visible. That piece is the way a diagnosis made in one place becomes a frame in another, and the way a frame protects itself from the evidence that would break it. The failure in this case was not a missed sign so much as a sign that was seen, written down, and explained away, and the person who put the case back together was the nurse who had been keeping the record.

The consultation lasted twenty minutes and most of it was useful. Ruth had palpitations that came in waves and lasted minutes at a time, usually in the evening, and they woke her from sleep. Her hands shook when she held a cup, and she had spilled two coffees that week. She was sleeping badly, she was irritable with her staff, she was sweating in a sweater in a cool room, and she could not tolerate the radiator in the office. She had lost seven kilograms in six weeks without trying, and she said she assumed it was stress. She had been buying the same brand of hand cream for three weeks because her hands were hot, and she mentioned this as a small strange thing rather than as a symptom. How much weight loss over how long should start a search for a cause is set by the current guideline and the local protocol rather than by the impression of a consultation.

Dr Pryce examined her and found the things you would expect to find in a woman who was frightened of her own heart. Her pulse was 104 beats per minute and regular, her blood pressure was 118 over 64 millimeters of mercury, and her temperature was 37.4 degrees Celsius, which is not a fever and is also not quite normal. Her hands were warm and dry, she had a fine tremor of the outstretched fingers, and her eyes were wide and staring, and she was sweating in a sweater in a cool room. The figures that decide whether any of that matters are the ones your own service uses, so check the current guideline and the local protocol rather than carrying somebody else's numbers around in your head.

Here is where the case turns, and it turns on one sentence in a consulting room. The findings in front of Dr Pryce had a second explanation, and the second explanation was an overactive thyroid, because weight loss with a fast pulse, tremor, heat intolerance, sweating and staring eyes belong to that diagnosis as much as they belong to anxiety. What would have settled it were a temperature that stayed up, a pulse that stayed fast at rest, and a neck that moved when she swallowed, and none of those had been looked for. Thyroid function blood tests had been done at the start of the year and had been normal, and a normal result is read as the end of a question as often as it is read as the beginning of one, and so the note said anxiety and the note said a review in six weeks.

Dr Pryce started an antidepressant and wrote to the emergency department when the palpitations did not settle. The referral letter was helpful in every particular except the one that mattered. It said a forty-five-year-old woman with six weeks of palpitations and tremor, it said anxiety, it said she had been started on an antidepressant four weeks ago, and it did not say weight loss of seven kilograms, because that had been explained to herself as stress and never written down where the hospital could see it. She had also said, as she was leaving, that her mother had had her thyroid removed years ago for a lump, and the note did not record that either.

Three weeks before she came to the emergency department she had a viral illness of the kind that does not deserve a name, and it knocked her flat for a week. She was tired, she had a sore throat and a cough, she had a temperature for two days, and she stayed in bed. She felt better afterwards in the sense that a person feels better after a bad week, but she never went back to what she had been: her palpitations did not stop, the heat intolerance did not stop, and she went on losing weight. She told the emergency department about the virus and it was written in the notes, because a viral illness is the sort of thing everybody mentions, and it was never joined up to the weight loss or to the pulse. Whether two days of a raised temperature counts as anything is the question the current guideline and the local protocol in your own service will answer, rather than the impression of a story.

On the evening she came in, she had been sitting in the car in the school car park for twenty minutes because she did not think she could make it from the gate to the door. She was the administrator of a secondary school and the mother of a boy of twelve, and she had come with her husband Martin, who had driven her and then sat in the corridor unable to answer a single question about the previous four weeks, because she had been the one talking. She told the triage nurse that she had been in a state for six weeks. She told her that she could not sit still. She told her that she was hot, and that she was not sure whether that was the menopause or something else.

Delia Farrow was the nurse who took her in, and the first thing she noticed, before she took a single observation, was that the woman was hot and damp and did not stop moving. The heat came off her in a way that made the triage room feel warm. Her hands and wrists were warm and red, she had a fine tremor of the outstretched fingers, her eyes were wide and staring, and the sweat stood on her upper lip. She said she had not been able to sit still in the car, and she said it as a complaint, and she said it again in the bay, and again to the nurse who brought her a blanket, and every time as though it were the explanation for everything else.

The triage observations were recorded and they did not agree with each other. Her pulse was 128 beats per minute and irregularly irregular, her blood pressure was 118 over 64 millimeters of mercury, her temperature was 38.1 degrees Celsius, her respiratory rate was 22 breaths a minute, and her oxygen saturation in air was 97 per cent. An irregular pulse at that rate, with a temperature above 38 degrees Celsius, is a different animal from the palpitations of an anxious woman. The limits that separate them are the ones written in the current guideline and the local protocol rather than in the memory of a triage nurse.

Toby Farrant was the registrar that evening, and he did what good registrars do, which is to read the referral letter before he reads the patient. The referral letter said anxiety. He read Ruth Kelleher through it, and everything she told him was filed under the word in the letter: the palpitations were anxiety, the tremor was anxiety, the sleeplessness was anxiety, the flushed face was anxiety, and the weight loss was anxiety, because the letter did not mention that she had lost any. A referral letter is a summary written in a hurry by somebody who met the patient once, and such a summary is often more certain than the patient standing in front of you.

He said his reasoning out loud, as registrars are taught to, and he ranked it. First, anxiety with an arrhythmia, because she had been anxious for six weeks, the antidepressant had not helped, she had a rapid irregular pulse, and she was now frightening herself. Second, an overactive thyroid, because the weight loss, the heat, the tremor and the irregular pulse all belonged to that, and the findings that would have confirmed it were a temperature that stayed up, a fast pulse that did not settle with rest, a neck that moved on swallowing, and staring eyes. Third, an infection, because she had had a virus three weeks ago and had been febrile since the day before she came in.

The second was set aside, and this is where the case turns, because the reason was not a clinical judgement. It was the letter. A diagnosis that arrives in writing from a colleague who has met the patient and has a good reason for their opinion is given more weight than the same diagnosis made at the bedside of a stranger, and the result was that a nurse's observation about the warmth of a hand carried less weight than a general practitioner's note written after twenty minutes. The team accepted the frame because the frame had been made by somebody they respected, and that is the most ordinary and least blameable way for a case to go wrong.

The treatment began, and by the lights of the ward it was correct. She was given a beta blocker, because a fast irregular pulse in a frightened woman is treated with a beta blocker, and because the rate is part of what is making her frightened, and it is a good drug for that. She was given a sedative for the nights, because she had not slept and the one she was already taking had not worked. An electrocardiogram, or ECG, was done and showed an irregular rhythm, and a cardiology referral was written for the outpatient clinic, because an arrhythmia in a woman with anxiety needs a rhythmologist and not another bottle.

The nursing that night was the nursing the ward does for a woman with palpitations, and it was done well. Observations were taken hourly and the cardiac monitor was applied, and the nurse documented what she saw rather than only what the monitor said. She wrote the temperature at every set of observations. She noticed that the woman was hot rather than cold, that her hands were warm and dry rather than cold and clammy, and she wrote that down as a sentence, because she had been taught to write down what she saw and not only what the numbers said. She noticed that the woman was talking continuously and that her sentences were getting shorter and shorter as the night went on.

The failure was not obvious, and it was the kind that looks like success from the foot of the bed. The beta blocker did what beta blockers do, and by midnight the rate had come down from 128 to 96 beats per minute and the monitor showed a slower, more organised-looking rhythm, and the registrar came to see her and was satisfied. The nurse was not, because the rate was the only thing that had improved, and her temperature had gone up rather than down. The sedative had made her sleepy and unsteady, and she was found on the floor of the bathroom at three in the morning, and nobody was sure why, and it was put down to the sedative and to a long and anxious day. The rates and pressures a team treats as adequate are the ones written in the current guideline and the local protocol, and a number that moves in the right direction is not the same as a patient who is improving.

The team did what teams do with a patient who is not improving but is not obviously worse either, which is to adjust the plan rather than to abandon it. The registrar considered changing the antibiotic that had been started for the fever, on the grounds that she had had a virus three weeks ago and might have a chest infection. He considered whether the sedative should be increased, and whether she should be moved to a bed where she could be watched more closely. None of those thoughts was unreasonable, and every one of them was a way of not asking a harder question, and the harder question was not about the pulse at all.

Delia Farrow is the reason this case has a different ending, and what she did was unglamorous and completely decisive. She had written the temperature at every set of observations since eight in the evening, and before the end of her shift she laid the figures out on a sheet of paper in the order they had been taken. The temperature had not fluctuated, had not settled with the beta blocker, and had risen in step with the rate all night, and the highest readings were at the times when she was hottest and least able to settle. She had done the same thing many times with fever charts on children, and it had never occurred to her that a chart of a woman's temperature would be the thing that reopened the diagnosis.

She went back to the bay at half past seven in the morning, at the end of a long shift, and did two things that nobody had done in the previous six weeks. The first was to ask Ruth Kelleher about her neck, and when she put her fingers on it she felt the gland sitting low and moving when the patient swallowed, and she asked her to swallow again, watched it move, and wrote it down. The second was to ask about the family history, and Ruth said, without being asked, that her mother had had her thyroid removed years ago for a lump, and then said that she had mentioned it to the doctor four weeks earlier and had been told it was nothing. She said she had thought it was nothing too.

Toby Farrant came in at eight and was told the two things, and he did not dismiss either of them, because he had held the diagnosis of anxiety for less than a day and had not yet begun to defend it. He put his own hand on her neck, and he wrote the words thyroid storm in the notes and then crossed them out, because it was not his to write yet. He rang the endocrine team and asked for the endocrinologist to come in that morning. He stopped the antidepressant and the sedative as well, because both had been given for a diagnosis that was now in doubt, and he told Ruth Kelleher that he was sorry she had been given them and that he wanted to go over everything again from the beginning.

The bloods that came back put the whole picture on a single page. The thyroid stimulating hormone was suppressed, the free thyroxine was far above the range the laboratory reports, and the potassium was low, which is what happens when a body is driven at that rate for that long. Her ECG showed the irregular rhythm of atrial fibrillation, and the story that had been built on anxiety had a name now that everybody in the room could say. The criteria that define a storm differ between sources and between patients, and the rates and thresholds that decide whether a heart is coping are written in the current guideline and the local protocol in your own service, so check them there rather than against a story.

Thyroid storm, which is the name for an overactive thyroid running away with the body, precipitated by the viral illness three weeks earlier and unmasked by everything that had been done for the anxiety. The antidepressant had not caused it, but it had treated a symptom of it and had kept her inside a diagnosis. The sedative had made her slow and vague, so that the confusion of a storm was read as the effect of a sleeping tablet. The beta blocker had brought the rate down, so that the storm looked as though it was settling, when what it did was slow the heart while the temperature kept climbing, and it is the temperature and the confusion and the heart together that tell you which of those two things is happening.

She was moved to the intensive care unit, or ICU, that afternoon, and the care was taken over by the endocrinologist, who set the plan. The treatment for the storm was given: drugs to block the gland and stop it making more hormone, and the beta blocker continued because the rate is part of what is killing her, and corticosteroids because the body has run out of its own way of coping. Supportive care with fluids and cooling went with it. The precipitant was looked for as well, because a storm that is not given its cause will come back, and the chest infection that followed the virus was treated with antibiotics.

Farrow went with her, and the nursing in the intensive care unit was the nursing a person in a storm needs, which is continuous observation of the heart, the temperature and the level of consciousness, and a chart that everybody reads. The nurse brought the sheet of paper with the temperature figures written on it, and it was taped to the end of the bed, and it stayed there for three days. She also did the thing that patients in this state need most, which is to be talked to, and she told Ruth Kelleher what was happening and why. She asked her about her mother and her mother's operation, and the answer was a lump and a thyroid and a scar, and the answer was in the room with them.

The recovery took days, and the first sign of it was not the heart rate but the temperature, which came down over the afternoon of the second day and stayed down, and the confusion lifted with it. By the fourth day she was sitting up, and she remembered the three in the morning on the bathroom floor, and she was mortified, and the nurse told her that the medicine had done that, and not her illness and not her character. She was moved back to the ward, the beta blocker was continued and reviewed, the sedative and the antidepressant were not restarted, and the discharge letter went to her general practice with the thyroid results in it and the medicine list changed, because the letter is how one part of a system tells another what it has learned. How long a recovery of this kind should take is set out in the current guideline and the local protocol rather than in the length of this account.

Once the storm was over, the underlying condition was dealt with properly rather than as an afterthought, because the storm is the emergency and the thyroid is the illness. The team discussed what would stop the gland making hormone for good, and whether that meant an operation or a course of treatment that blocks the gland, and she was referred on for that decision once she was well. Her heart was re-examined and the rhythm was watched, because a heart that has been driven at that speed for days is not automatically a heart that has recovered. She was told plainly what had happened to it, which is that the rhythm had gone out of her and would need watching, and that many people recover it completely.

The general practice changed its own practice as well, and the change was small and specific. A woman under fifty with palpitations, tremor and unexplained weight loss is to have thyroid function tested before she is given a drug for anxiety, and a result that is normal once is not a result that is normal for ever, and if the symptoms continue then the test is done again. The practice also began recording the family history that patients mention in passing, on the understanding that a mother with a thyroid operation is not small talk. None of it would have saved Ruth Kelleher on the night she came in, though all of it would have caught her three weeks before she arrived.

Before she went home, and again in the clinic, Farrow taught Ruth Kelleher the things that would have brought her back sooner, and she taught them in the language of a woman who administers a school office rather than the language of a ward. She told her that a heart beating at this rate, a body that is hot, and a mind that is confused are three separate events that belong together. She had been given one name for all three, and the name was the problem. She told her to keep a diary of her pulse and her temperature for a month, and Ruth Kelleher, who is an organised woman, kept it, and the diary is why the next episode was caught in a day rather than a week.

The teaching of this case is about a word and about what a word does. Anxiety is a real diagnosis and a useful one, and it explains a fast pulse, a tremor, sleeplessness, a flush and a frightened woman, and it is the right diagnosis very often. It does not explain a temperature that climbs through the night while the rate is being treated, and a single symptom that does not fit is not a detail to be tidied away; it is the point at which the diagnosis has to be reopened. And the second half of the teaching is about the menopause, because hot flushes at forty-five are not a diagnosis, they are a description, and a description cannot be allowed to stand in the place of an examination.

There was no careless person in this story and there was no unkind one. Dr Pryce saw an anxious woman whose thyroid tests were normal and wrote an honest letter about her. The registrar believed a letter from a colleague and treated a real arrhythmia with a real drug. The nurse wrote down what she saw and was not believed for a few hours, which is what happens to the person who says something the team has not been thinking. The failure was in the sequence of the words, in a referral that arrived with a diagnosis already in it, and in a system where one word was used for a feeling, a mechanism and a disease at the same time, and any team could have caught it.

What Farrow takes from it is not that nurses should overrule doctors, because that is not what happened and it would not be safe. She has thought about the moment she looked at her sheet of paper and thought that she might be wrong, and about how long that thought took. The quietness of that doubt is the thing that nearly cost this patient another three days. She takes the smaller lesson that a temperature chart is not a formality, and that the woman on the monitor was not the woman who had come in, and that anybody who watches a person for a whole night can see that.

To summarize, Ruth Kelleher was treated for anxiety with an arrhythmia when what she had was a thyroid storm, and the anxiety was a reasonable diagnosis made competently and then allowed to explain a temperature, a pulse and a confusion that it could not explain. The evidence was in front of the team from the first hour: a woman who was hot rather than cold, with warm dry hands, a fast irregular pulse, a fine tremor, staring eyes, and seven kilograms of weight that nobody had written down. Every number in this account is an illustration of reasoning and must be checked against the current guideline and the local protocol before it is used to act. The teaching is that anxiety does not explain a fever with a rapid irregular pulse, and that a woman of forty-five with palpitations and tremor is not a woman having hot flushes.

# Chapter 13 — The Woman Who Woke Up Shaking

Three in the morning is the hour when medicine is least well equipped to be subtle. Nadia Haddad was fifty-two years old and she cleaned wards on the early shift. She woke beside her husband shaking so hard that she could not get her hands under the blanket. She was sweating in a cool room, did not know where she was, and her heart was beating fast and hard. Karim, her husband, brought her in because he had been awake with her for an hour and was frightened by what he saw.

What follows is a constructed composite, assembled for teaching, and it does not describe a real patient; no detail identifies anyone living or dead. The case turns on a question asked at three in the morning that nobody had thought to ask, and on a medication more complicated than any note said. It is a case in which the first treatment worked, which is normally taken to be the end of a story, and in which the second half began only when that treatment stopped working. Every number in it illustrates reasoning.

The presenting problem was a woman with diabetes who was shaking, sweating and confused in the small hours, and the team had to hold two readings of it at once. The first was the obvious one, that her blood glucose had fallen too low, which fits those symptoms exactly and which the bedside meter confirmed within minutes. The second was the dangerous alternative in the other direction, a hyperglycaemic emergency, in which the sugar is high rather than low; the findings that would reveal it are a high reading on the meter, deep and deliberate breathing, a dry tongue and concentrated urine, and not one of them was present. The third possibility was the one the case turned on, which is that the pancreas itself makes too much insulin, and the finding that would reveal it is hypoglycaemia that returns after the tablet causing it has been stopped.

Between the meter reading and the treatment there were ten minutes in which the history had to be taken properly, and that is where the case begins. Nadia had been diagnosed with type 2 diabetes years earlier and had attended a clinic for it since then. She worked the early shift, so she slept through the afternoons and woke through the night, and she had eaten nothing since a sandwich at eleven o'clock the previous morning. She had not taken her usual tablets that day because she had slept through the alarm she set for them. When Aisha Farouk, the nurse, asked what she had eaten, Nadia said that she had been frightened of eating in the small hours ever since she started this job, because of what she had been told about her sugar.

Dr Jonas Petrie was the emergency department registrar who took her, and the nurse working with him was Aisha Farouk, who had nursed in that department for years and had a way of asking questions people wanted to answer. The working diagnosis was a hypoglycaemic episode, and the reasoning was audible and entirely reasonable: a person with diabetes, a textbook pattern of tremor, sweating and confusion, and a meter that confirmed the low. They ranked the possibilities twice, first by likelihood and then by how much harm would follow if one were true and missed. The only entry high on the second list was the hyperglycaemic emergency the meter had excluded, and the possibility that her pancreas was making too much insulin went into the notes without anybody owning it.

The examination in the resuscitation area was quick, and reassuring in the way examinations are once a number has answered the question. Her pulse was 124 beats per minute, which is fast and not by itself alarming, and the rate at which a fast pulse should worry a clinician is taken from the current guideline and the local protocol in your own service rather than from recollection. Her blood pressure was 104 over 68 millimeters of mercury, and a pressure that low means something different in a person whose pressure is normally high, so the reading that should concern you has to come from the same documents. Her temperature was 35.9 degrees Celsius, and the degree at which a temperature is called low is another matter for the same documents. She had no weakness in either arm or leg and no sign of dehydration, which argues against the hyperglycaemic emergency.

The treatment started at once, and it was correct for what they had found. The meter had read 2.8 millimoles per liter, and she was given glucose, which is the right answer to a low blood glucose whatever the cause, and when she could swallow safely she was given food and kept warm, and the plan for the night was written as observation rather than discharge. The registrar was explicit about his reasoning, and the explicit part was this: the meter had answered the question asked of it, and a person whose sugar is low in the small hours after a long shift has an ordinary explanation in a missed meal and a tablet. Aisha wrote that reasoning down and added the two things that did not fit it: the episodes always came in the small hours, and they always came after she had eaten almost nothing.

The nursing that night was not a backdrop to the medicine, because the medicine had one job and it did it. Aisha checked the glucose at the bedside through the night rather than trusting the value that had brought her in, because a reading taken at four in the morning tells a different story from a reading taken on arrival. The level at which a person is called hypoglycaemic differs between laboratories and is written into the current guideline and the local protocol rather than into anybody's memory. She started a food and symptom chart, and on it she recorded the time of every episode, what had been eaten and when, and the time of every check. At the bottom she left a note for the next nurse: this patient eats almost nothing between eleven at night and six in the morning, and her husband said she had begun eating at the bedside because she was frightened.

She went home the following morning with ordinary advice, and most of it was good: eat regularly, do not skip meals, carry something sweet, and check the sugar if she felt unwell. She was given a written plan for what to do at three in the morning if the shaking started again, and a number to telephone. The registrar thought the case was closed. He was not being careless; he was reasoning correctly inside a frame that was already the wrong shape. What he did not know was what was written in the clinic record, and neither did anyone else in the room, because that record had been copied rather than asked for.

The recurrence came three weeks later at four in the morning, was managed exactly as the first one had been, and was followed by two more in the month after that. Every one of them was in the early hours, every one followed a meal she had not eaten, and every one was over within the hour once she had taken glucose. She was admitted each time and discharged with a smaller variation on the same advice, until it amounted to eating a biscuit before bed, which she did for a fortnight and which changed nothing. How many episodes in what period should make a service call a pattern rather than a coincidence, and how long an episode should be allowed to run, are questions for the current guideline and the local protocol, and nobody here had asked either, so the pattern was never named. The third time she came back she sat on the edge of the trolley and said she was frightened of going to sleep, and Aisha sat down beside her and asked what she had eaten the day before.

Between those admissions the team's responses became steadily more elaborate and steadily less directed. The food before bed was increased, a night-time snack was arranged, she was asked to set an alarm for two in the morning, and the timing of the tablet was moved. She was offered a repeat clinic review and asked to bring her tablets, and she brought them, and the nurse counted them and wrote the number down, the number that had always been in the box. Every one of these interventions was reasonable and every one treated a missed meal.

The failure in this case was quiet, and quiet failures are the hardest to see. The team had not been careless; they had been consistent, and consistency is what a wrong frame buys you. The registrar's first feeling was that she was not taking her tablets properly, his second was that she was eating irregularly because the work was hard and the clinic had not got through to her, and his third, which he was honest with himself about afterwards, was that some people have brittle diabetes and that brittle diabetes is managed rather than cured. Aisha's feeling was harder to defend, because she had kept the charts, and she had a chart that said the patient ate nothing for hours and another that said the sugar was low every time. What she did not have was a chart that said what tablet the sugar had been low on.

So on the third admission Aisha did the thing that turned the case, which was to stop asking what medication the patient was on and start asking about every tablet, one at a time, wherever it had come from. Nadia's answer took ten minutes, and it was the answer that the notes had not been asking for. She took a tablet from the diabetes clinic, and she had been given a new box at her last review with instructions to take a different number of them, and she had never attended that review because she was on shift. She had asked a neighbour to read the new instructions and had not understood them, and the leaflet had two numbers on it with the newer one written on in pen. It was a sulfonylurea, a tablet that works by making the pancreas release more insulin, and it is exactly the class of drug that causes the blood sugar to fall in the early hours after a short fast.

That last point is the hinge of the whole case. A sulfonylurea taken by a woman who eats almost nothing during the day produces a blood sugar that is unremarkable at four in the afternoon and dangerously low at four in the morning, a well known behaviour of the drug rather than a rare thing. The tablet had been doing exactly what it is designed to do, the shift pattern was exactly the situation in which it does the most damage, and the two had been working against each other for months while everybody assumed the two were unrelated. Nobody had looked at the two together, because the shift pattern was discussed on the ward round and the tablet was in the notes, and people read the notes. The decision to raise her dose had been made at a review she had not attended, a process most systems have and never examine.

The moment the team understood what had happened is worth describing, because it was not dramatic and nobody enjoyed it. The registrar read the leaflet in Aisha's hand, looked at the number in pen, and said that the dose had been increased for a woman who eats nothing for eleven hours a day, and then he sat down, which he had not intended to do. Aisha's feeling was that she had held the answer in her chart for a month and never asked the question that would release it. Nothing had gone wrong that anybody could name: a count of tablets had been taken, a number had been written down, and the number had been the wrong number.

The first change was the smallest one available, and it was made because of the history. The sulfonylurea was stopped, and Dr Helena Bramwell, the diabetes specialist who had reviewed her in the clinic, rebuilt her treatment around a regimen that did not carry the risk of a low blood sugar in the small hours. Nadia was told plainly why, in language she could act on: the tablet was making her sugar low at night because she eats so little during the day. The episodes stopped, completely, and stayed stopped for six weeks. Six weeks is a description of what happened rather than a threshold, and how long a response should be observed before a case is closed is a question for the current guideline and the local protocol. The team wrote the six weeks down as a good outcome, and nobody asked what would happen when the tablet was no longer there to do part of the work.

They came back in the seventh week, and this time the episode was worse rather than better. Karim found her on the floor of the bathroom at four in the morning, calling her name for a while before she woke. There was a bruise over the side of her head and no recollection of how it had come, the glucose at the bedside was very low, and she did not rouse as quickly to the treatment that had worked so well the first three times. Aisha stayed with her while the registrar was called, and said out loud the thing nobody else had said, which was that this was the first episode that had left a mark. A fall in a person whose blood sugar falls without warning is not a minor addition to the picture, because the injury that brings her in is also one a low blood sugar will not let her feel.

The team now had to look further, and the first thing they found was a decision made months earlier that nobody had ever read again. During the very first admission, because she had already had more than one episode and the cause was not obvious, a scan of the abdomen had been requested to look at the pancreas, and it was reported as showing nothing abnormal, and it was filed. It was not a bad scan and not misreported: a small tumour that secretes insulin is much easier to find when the insulin is high and the sugar is low, and that scan had been done when neither was. A scan done at the wrong moment is not a negative result but an uninformative one.

The scan was therefore repeated, and this time prepared for the question. That meant reproducing the low blood sugar under supervision with the glucose measured at the same time as the scan, agreeing the fast with the current guideline and the local protocol rather than improvising it, and doing it once, by a specialist team, with the ability to treat the low at the bedside when it happened. It is an uncomfortable thing to do to a woman whose blood sugar has already fallen dangerously low, and that discomfort is why it belongs to a specialist team. The patient and her husband were told what would be done and why, and Aisha was there, because a nurse who has watched a patient's sugar fall knows about the fear a scan does not.

The scan found a small tumour in the pancreas, and the blood taken at the same moment supported it by showing the hormones that control sugar moving in the pattern such a tumour produces. The diagnosis was an insulinoma, a tumour made of the cells that make insulin, and every part of the case now fitted it. A tumour of this kind keeps the blood sugar low at the hours when the body should be releasing glucose to fuel the night, which are the small hours, exactly when her episodes had always come. It also explains the six weeks of freedom, because the tablet and the tumour had been producing the same effect together and stopping the tablet removed only half the cause: she had been low because of the tablet and because of the tumour, and for six weeks the tablet had been doing the tumour's work.

The surgeon was Mr Simon Harcourt, and the reason for operating was that an insulinoma is one of the causes of hypoglycaemia in which the tumour can be removed and the patient cured, which is not true of most causes and which is why the effort of finding it was worth making. The approach was discussed with Nadia and Karim, and the decision was shaped by what she wanted: to be free of the episodes and to sleep through the night. The nursing around the operation was planned rather than improvised, with the glucose watched closely before and after, because the sudden withdrawal of all that extra insulin makes a low blood sugar in the first days after surgery a real risk. Aisha worked some of those nights, and she could say to the patient what nobody else in the building could say: she knew what it was like to watch this happen.

The first night back on the ward was the first night in months without an episode, and it was strange for everybody. Nadia slept, which she had not done properly for a long time, and Karim slept in a chair beside her, and nobody woke her to check anything at three in the morning because there was nothing to check. Aisha came in at the usual hour anyway, found them both asleep, and wrote the night down, because a nurse who has watched a patient fail for weeks does not trust a good night until it has happened twice. The blood sugar she had learned to fear became something she measured rather than something that happened to her, and by the end of the first week the shaking and the confusion had gone, and the chart Aisha had kept for months still hung on the wall with nothing new written on it.

She went back to work and did her shifts, and slept in the afternoon with a glucose meter beside the bed for the first few weeks, not because she needed it every time but because she wanted to see it behave. She told people at work that she had had a tumour of the pancreas that made her sugar fall, which was more than she had told anyone about the episodes themselves, and found that saying it plainly took an enormous amount of energy at first and none at all after a month. The diabetes was still there and still needed treating. The treatment for it was rebuilt around the fact that she works nights and eats in shifts, which is a thing a clinic can do if it thinks about it and which had never been thought about.

What the nursing team did with her afterwards was, in the end, the most durable part of the treatment. Aisha taught her to recognise the early signs, which are tremor, sweating, hunger and confusion, and taught her that the treatment of a low blood sugar is fast carbohydrate first and something with protein in it afterwards. She was given a written plan specific to her hours rather than a generic sheet, and shown how to time her diabetes medication around the times she eats, and told what would happen if she did not. She was also told what to say in an emergency, which is that she has had a tumour of the pancreas that made her blood sugar fall, because a woman who says only that she is diabetic will be treated as one whose diabetes is uncontrolled, and that is a different problem with a different answer.

The follow-up was arranged with the diabetes clinic, and the change that mattered was small and entirely within the gift of any team. A person who does not attend a review does not have her treatment quietly left as it was on the assumption that she is taking it, and a person who misses a review because of a shift pattern needs it offered again at a time she can attend. The clinic began to ask about the interval since the last review and about the medicines actually in the house, and began to treat the clinic list as the beginning of a conversation rather than the record of one. And the team wrote down what the case had taught them, which was that the response to a treatment is evidence about the treatment and not a verdict on the diagnosis.

Three things came out of this case, and the first is not the one a listener expects. The first is that a treatment which works and then stops working has still taught you something, and the something is usually not that the diagnosis was wrong, since a treatment that worked is often the reason a diagnosis is not questioned for longer. The second is that the most valuable document in a case is often the one nobody asked for, and here it was a medication list assembled at three in the morning, one question at a time, from the patient herself, worth more than one assembled from the notes. The third is that a failure of correct treatment is a finding in its own right, and that the proper response to it is to go back to the beginning and look again rather than to add something else.

Aisha was clear afterwards that she had missed nothing a reasonable colleague would have missed, and she was right. She had measured the glucose accurately, kept the charts, written accurate notes, and done everything the job of a nurse on a night shift consists of. What she had not done was treat the medication as a question rather than a formality, and the reason was that she had asked it in the wrong form, which was whether the patient was taking her tablets, an answer to which is yes for almost every patient who has not skipped a morning. The question that worked differed in every particular: it was not more medical and not more expert than the one she had been asking, but simply more specific. It is now the question she asks of anybody who comes back a second time with the same story, and she asks it at the first visit, because on the first visit it changes nothing and on the second it changes everything. Noticing is partly luck and asking is a habit, and the habit is the part that transfers.

What Nadia took away from the whole business was more practical than any of the teaching. She sleeps through the afternoon and eats at odd hours and now has a plan that matches her life rather than one that asks her to change it, and she has written down what a low blood sugar looks like for her specifically, which is not what it looks like in a textbook. She has written down what she will say to any doctor or nurse who meets her, and she has told Karim what to watch for and what to do about it. She also says that the part she minds most is being asked whether she takes her tablets, because that is the question she could never answer honestly and never has, and it is the one question she now answers without any difficulty at all.

To summarize, Nadia Haddad had a low blood sugar because of a tablet and because of a tumour in her pancreas at the same time, and the tablet hid the tumour for six weeks while everybody congratulated themselves on the good result. The decisive act was a nurse asking about the medicines one at a time at three in the morning rather than accepting a medication list assembled from notes, and the decisive finding was that the tablet had been increased at a review the patient did not attend, with the new instructions read aloud by a neighbour and misread by everyone including the patient. A treatment that works and then stops working is not the end of a case; it is the first piece of evidence that the working diagnosis was incomplete, and the right response to it is to reopen the case and look again. The case was solved by surgery, by a medication history taken question by question, and by a nurse who watched the treatment fail and asked what the tablets were actually called, and no individual in it failed through carelessness, since the failure belonged to a frame that closed around a reasonable answer and to a system that prescribed for a woman who was not in the room. This chapter is a constructed composite and describes no real patient, and it is educational material rather than medical advice, a protocol, or a substitute for the judgment of the clinician with the patient. Every number quoted here, including the blood glucose values, the pulse, the blood pressure and the intervals at which a response is judged, is an illustration of reasoning and must be confirmed against the current guideline and the local protocol before it informs any real decision.

# Chapter 14 — The Migraine That Was Treated as a Stroke

The account of a stroke is usually taken once, quickly, and in the words of the person who was frightened. The account of Claire Dubois was taken twice, and the second time it took an hour and a half, and the difference between those two conversations is the whole of this case. She was forty-four years old, she taught secondary school, and in three weeks she was admitted twice with the same complaint, which was numbness down one side of her body and disturbed vision, and both episodes had resolved before the end of the day. On the second admission a nurse sat down with Claire and with her husband, Marc, and with a notebook, and took the story again from the beginning, slowly, and asked about the words.

What follows is a constructed composite, assembled for teaching rather than lifted from anyone's record, and it does not describe a real patient; the names are ordinary names and no detail here identifies anyone living or dead. The reasoning that organizes it is a single question asked twice: what did this episode actually consist of, and what should have been done with the answer. The answer changed nothing about the treatment for the first three weeks, and then it changed everything, and the thing that changed was a set of words. A stroke unit is a place where people are treated for a disease they may not have, and that is not a rare situation, and the harm of it is not always visible from inside.

Here is what happened the first time. Claire was at home on a Tuesday evening in the autumn, marking work at the kitchen table, when she noticed that the page in front of her had gone strange. She said the words she used were that a light was moving in the corner of her vision, like a zig-zag, and that she could not look at it directly. It passed after a while, and then her right hand went numb from the fingertips to the elbow, and the numbness spread up the right side of her face, and she felt sick and vomited twice. Marc found her in the bathroom, frightened and disoriented but able to walk, and by midnight the numbness had gone and the headache that came with it had settled. She did not go to hospital that night, and she went the next morning, and by then she was well, and the referral was written on the strength of an account she gave in six sentences.

Dr Miriam Carmody was the stroke physician who saw her, with Dr Owen Radcliffe the registrar, and Nadine Osei the stroke unit nurse who took the admission. The working diagnosis was a transient ischemic attack, and the reasoning was audible and reasonable: a woman of forty-four, a focal neurological deficit that came and went within a day, a scan that showed no bleeding, and no cardiac cause found on an electrocardiogram, later an ECG. Two readings were kept in front of them. The dangerous one was a stroke that had declared itself, and the findings that would reveal it are a deficit that does not resolve, a change on the imaging, or a deterioration over hours. The other was bleeding around the brain, and the findings that would reveal that are a sudden severe headache, vomiting, neck stiffness and an abnormal scan. Neither was present, and the team said so, and they were right to say so.

The treatment was the correct treatment for what they believed, and it was begun for the reasons any stroke unit would begin it. A computed tomography scan of the head, a CT scan, was done to exclude bleeding before any drug was given, and it was normal. An antiplatelet drug was started, a statin was started, and a risk factor assessment was begun with her blood pressure, her weight, her smoking history and her contraceptive. The blood pressure at the clinic had been 146 over 92 millimeters of mercury, and where a pressure counts as raised differs between populations and between documents, so the number worth working from is the one your own current guidance uses. She was admitted to the stroke unit rather than sent home, and that decision was also reasonable, because a deficit that has resolved once can resolve again and be worse the second time.

The nursing on the unit was the same nursing that every patient there receives, and it was done well. Neurological observations were kept to a schedule, and the schedule is set by how unwell the patient is rather than by habit, and the frequency your own service works to is written into the current guideline and the local protocol. A mobility assessment was done, and Claire walked unaided with a frame she did not need and would not use. The nurse took a detailed account of what had happened, which is a nursing task that sounds like administration and is not, and Nadine Osei took twenty minutes over it on the first afternoon and wrote it out in full. She wrote that the patient reported visual disturbance before the numbness, that the numbness was on the right side, that there had been vomiting, and that the episode had lasted most of a day. She wrote what the patient had said, not what the patient had been admitted with.

Nadine's own account of that afternoon has the quality that good nursing notes have and bad ones do not, which is that a stranger could read it and know what had happened. She wrote that the light had been in one half of the visual field and had moved, that Claire had turned away from the light and had asked for the blinds to be closed, and that she had been sick twice. What she wrote, and what everybody else read, was the phrase visual disturbance, and she wrote it because that was the phrase Claire used. The word disturbance is neutral. It says something was wrong with the sight and nothing about what, and a word that says nothing cannot be questioned later, and that is how a detail dies.

Claire went home after three days with a plan for what to bring her back, a supply of the antiplatelet drug, and a date for the risk factor clinic. She was told that the episode had been a warning rather than a stroke, and that the tablet was there to reduce the risk of the warning becoming the thing itself, and she believed that, and she was entirely willing to be a person who had had a warning. Her pulse was 76 beats per minute and her heart was regular, and the rate that counts as a normal resting pulse is worth confirming against the current guideline and the local protocol if you are going to reason from it. She went back to teaching. She had had, the team believed, a small stroke that left no trace, and the treatment was aimed at the next one, and the whole arrangement was coherent and wrong.

Three weeks passed, and in that time she went to the risk factor clinic, which is where a stroke unit sends the people whose diagnosis it believes. The clinic took her weight, which had been taken before, and her blood pressure, and asked about smoking, and she had never smoked, and asked about alcohol, and asked about her contraceptive, which she had taken for years without any trouble. The nurse who took the history wrote that she was a non-smoker with a raised blood pressure and no other risk factors, and then wrote in the free text box that the family history was negative, because when the specialist asked, Claire had said that there was nothing in her family. Nobody asked what her mother had, or what her mother's episodes had been called, and so the one piece of information that would have changed everything was excluded by the word nothing. The clinic adjusted her blood pressure treatment and asked her to come back, and she came, and she was a good patient.

Three weeks later she came back. This time the visual disturbance came first and lasted longer, and the numbness started in the same right hand and spread in the same direction, and she was sick three times and could not keep fluids down. The episode lasted most of the working day and she went to the emergency department in the afternoon rather than the next morning, and the registrar on duty was Dr Radcliffe, who had seen her before. Her blood pressure was 138 over 86 millimeters of mercury, and the threshold that should concern a team looking at a blood pressure is one your own protocol states, not one carried between units. A CT scan was done and it was normal, and magnetic resonance imaging, an MRI, was considered and arranged, and the working diagnosis was again a transient ischemic attack. How soon a second episode should be allowed to change a working diagnosis is a question for the current guideline and the local protocol, and nobody asked it here. She was admitted to the stroke unit again, and she was reviewed by the same physician, and nobody anywhere said the word migraine.

The further episode happened on the ward, on the second night. Claire asked for the lights off and asked the staff to turn the television down, and she was sick twice, and at some point in the early hours she was found by her husband sitting up in bed with her right arm held across her chest because she said it felt wrong, though it was not weak and it was not painful. She was not slurred, and she was not confused, and she was oriented to person, place and time, and she answered questions accurately. The neurological observations were unchanged, the pulse was 84 beats per minute and regular, and a rate that should concern a team looking at a ward patient is set out in the current guideline and the local protocol rather than in a habit. The scan on the ward was normal. The team found themselves where stroke teams often find themselves, which is with a patient they are certain about who is having another episode of the thing they are certain about.

The feeling in the room was not anxiety so much as a kind of professional despair. Dr Carmody's thought, which she did not say aloud at first, was that this was what a transient ischemic attack looked like when it was going on to be something bigger, and that the second admission should have been the last warning before a stroke. The registrars' feeling was that the right response to an incomplete picture was to make the picture more complete, which in practice meant more imaging, more tests and a longer stay. Nobody thought about the antiplatelet drug in a way that anyone would want written down, and then Nadine thought about it, and she thought about the fact that a woman who was bleeding from the bowel had been given a drug that makes bleeding more likely.

What happened next was that Claire developed a gastrointestinal bleed on the ward, which is to say she became anaemic, passed black stool, and became dizzy on standing, and she had to be taken out of the unit. The bleed was treated by the team that manages it, and the decision about the antiplatelet drug was taken by the specialist team together with that team, and the drug was stopped. The registrars' feeling about the bleed was that it was bad luck, and possibly an ulcer, and possibly the stress of the admission, and the stroke physician's feeling was that this was the price of doing the right thing for a woman with a real vascular risk. All of those feelings were reasonable and all of them were wrong, because there was no vascular risk to protect her from. The harm had been done by a treatment for a disease she did not have.

That is the sentence the case turns on, and it is worth slowing down on, because the idea that a treatment given for a wrong reason can do harm is obvious in other settings and is very often invisible in this one. An antiplatelet drug given to somebody who has had a stroke is a drug with a real purpose and a real cost, and the cost is paid in the blood. Given to somebody whose episodes were a migraine with an aura, the same drug buys nothing, because there is no clot to be prevented, and the cost is still paid in the blood. The person who pays it does not know she has been given the wrong diagnosis, and she is not in a position to argue with it, because she has just been told that she had a warning sign of a stroke.

The clue that had been in front of them since the first afternoon was not hidden and it was not subtle, and the chapter has to say plainly how it was missed. The nurse reconstructed it, and the reconstruction is the most important piece of nursing in this book. It was done by someone who had been keeping a record of her own for years and who had assumed that everybody else kept one too. On the second admission Nadine Osei went back to the notes and to the nursing diary she had kept during the first admission, and she sat with Claire and with Marc separately and then together, and she asked the same question of each of them in the same words: what happened first, and what did it look like, and how long did it last.

The answer came out in a different order from the one in the notes. The visual symptom came first, and it was not a blurring or a loss of sight but a zig-zag of light in one half of the visual field, which spread and then faded, and it lasted about twenty minutes. Then came the numbness, in the right hand and then the right side of the face, and then the sickness and the vomiting, and the whole thing lasted over an hour. The time course of an aura is one of the numbers a service should have a view about, and the guide you use should be the current guideline and the local protocol rather than a remembered number. Claire wanted the lights off and the curtains shut for the whole of it, and she could not bear the television, and she was quiet and would not be drawn into conversation. Marc added the last piece, because he had been there, and he said that her mother had the same thing and that it had been called migraine all her life.

That is the diagnosis, and it is a common one, and the reason it was missed is not that the symptoms were exotic. It is that the symptom arrived through a door that was already labelled. The team had been told that Claire had had a transient ischemic attack, and every finding was read through that label, so the zig-zag of light became a visual disturbance, the vomiting became nausea, the wish for darkness became a preference recorded by the night staff, and the mother's history became a family history of stroke because nobody had asked what the family called it. A label does not merely describe a patient; it selects what will be noticed, and a diagnosis that has already been made selects against the very observations that would overturn it.

The nurse was not outside this and it would be dishonest to place her outside it. Nadine had written the words visual disturbance into the notes herself, and she had written them because they were the words the patient used, and using the patient's words is usually the right thing to do. She had also been the one who wrote that Claire had asked for the blinds closed, and she had filed that as a nursing note about comfort rather than as a symptom, because it was a note about comfort and because nobody had asked her to think of it as anything else. What she did not do was go back and ask what the light looked like, and what she had not done was ask the family what they called it, and the correction was available to her on the first afternoon.

The team now had to reconsider what they had done and why, and the reconsideration was uncomfortable in a specific way. The scan had been normal and that had been reassuring, and a normal scan had been allowed to stand for more than it was worth, because in a woman of forty-four with no risk factors beyond a mildly raised pressure and a contraceptive, the scan being normal was less informative than it would have been in an older patient. The absence of a cause on the scan had been treated as the absence of a cause in general. The team also had to face the plainest fact in the case, which is that a drug they had given her without question had put her in a hospital with a bleed, and that a drug given for a stroke that never happened is a drug given for a stroke that never happened, with all of its risks and none of its benefits.

So the treatment was changed, and it was changed in two halves. The antiplatelet drug was stopped and stayed stopped, and the specialist team documented why, because the default after a bleed is to restart a drug that has obvious benefits and to think hard about one that has not. The statin was also reconsidered, and if she has no vascular disease then there is no argument for it, and a drug that is continued for a condition she does not have is another treatment with a cost and no purpose. Then the correct treatment for migraine with aura was begun: a migraine-specific acute treatment for the attacks themselves, and a preventive treatment for the attacks, which is the part that matters, because it is what reduces how often they come, how severe they are, and how often she ends up on a ward.

The contraceptive was the other thing that had to be reviewed, and it was reviewed with the same principle applied to it. She had been on a combined oral contraceptive, and in a woman who has migraine with aura, an oestrogen-containing contraceptive is not a neutral choice, because the two together raise the concern that brought her to the unit in the first place. The decision was taken by the specialist team together with the family planning service, and the advice about what to use instead was given in writing, and the follow-up was arranged. That was the point at which the case stopped being about a misdiagnosis and became about a woman with a real diagnosis and a real future, which is a better use of everybody's attention.

Claire improved, and the improvement was not only that the attacks became less frequent. She stopped being a woman with a warning sign in her history and became a woman with a condition she could treat and explain. The headache specialist, Dr Ingrid Solberg, sat with her and went through the whole sequence again in a calm room, and the description was familiar to her, and the familiarity was itself a finding, because a specialist who has heard this story before recognises the shape of it instantly and a general team looking at the same words does not. Claire went back to teaching in the following term. She stopped describing herself as someone who had had a stroke, and she started describing herself as someone who had migraine, and she told her colleagues what the warning signs were, which is a form of treatment that the ward cannot provide.

The stroke unit changed what it did, and the changes are worth naming because they are small and general and any unit could make them. The detailed account of an episode is now taken by a nurse with a set of specific questions, and the questions are about the shape of the symptom rather than about the diagnosis, so that the first half, the kind of symptom, its side, whether the person can see something or has lost something, how long it lasted, what came before it and what came after it. A nurse is told to write what the patient said rather than the label the patient arrived with, and to write the words for the light and the words for the numbness, because the words are the evidence. The unit also now asks the family what they call it, which takes four seconds and has changed more diagnoses than any scan.

There are two things in this case that are easy to confuse, and the chapter would be worth nothing without separating them. The first is that an aura is not a transient ischemic attack, and that the difference is made by the description of the event rather than by any test, because a scan of a normal brain does not distinguish the two and neither does a normal neurological examination.
