The second is that a treatment which is not indicated has harms of its own, which is why the question about a diagnosis is never only about what is best for the patient but also about what will be done to her while the team is still deciding. The second point is the one that a stroke unit is least equipped to hear, because in a stroke unit the cost of not treating is so much higher than the cost of treating that the instinct to treat is almost automatic.

Nadine Osei kept the diary after the case, as she keeps it for everybody now, and it is the reason she caught what nobody else caught. She had written it for herself and for the next shift, and on the second admission she read it back and found her own words from the first afternoon, and the words were not the words she had meant to write. She had written that the patient reported visual disturbance and requested low lighting, and she had meant both of those as description and both of them were diagnosis, and the one that did the damage was the first. What she took from it was a rule she now uses with every patient on the unit, which is to ask the family what the episode looked like at home and to write that down in their words, because the word a family uses for an episode is usually the word that knows what it is.

To summarize, Claire Dubois was not having transient ischemic attacks, she was having migraine with aura, and the treatment given for the wrong diagnosis put her in a hospital with a gastrointestinal bleed. The decisive observation was made by a nurse who reconstructed the sequence of the episodes from the patient, from her husband and from her own nursing diary, and the decisive findings were ordinary ones that were in the file from the first afternoon: a zig-zag of light in one half of the visual field lasting about twenty minutes, then numbness on one side, then vomiting, then a wish for darkness, and a mother whose identical episodes had been called migraine all her life. An aura and a transient ischemic attack can produce the same complaint, and only the description of the event separates them, so the language used to report an episode decides what the team investigates. A treatment that is not indicated has harms of its own, and no individual in this case failed through carelessness: the failure belonged to a label that selected the observations the team was willing to see, and to a service that treated a woman it had already labelled. This chapter is a constructed composite and describes no real patient, and it is educational material rather than medical advice, a protocol, or a substitute for the judgment of the clinician who is with the patient. Every number quoted here, including the blood pressures, the pulse rates, the duration of the aura and the intervals at which neurological observations are kept, is an illustration of reasoning and must be confirmed against the current guideline and the local protocol before it informs any real decision.

# Chapter 15 — The Fits That Stopped When She Was Distracted

Everything about this case turned on a single word, and the word was seizure. The family used it, the referral letter used it, and by the time Zoe Brennan arrived in the emergency department on a wet Tuesday evening the diagnosis had effectively been made before anybody in the building had laid a hand on her. This is a constructed composite assembled for teaching, and no part of it describes a real patient, a real family or a real service; the details are invented so that a piece of reasoning can be examined. What follows is a young woman whose episodes were described accurately enough for a specialist to change his mind, and whose description was written down by a nurse who was told, politely, that she was writing the wrong thing in the wrong place.

Zoe Brennan is 22, a second-year student, brought in by her flatmate after what the flatmate called her third collapse in a month. She is on her feet, embarrassed, and more angry about the visit than frightened by it, because the previous two visits ended with a leaflet and a letter to her general practitioner and no change in anything. She tells the registrar that she has never lost consciousness, though she cannot recall much of what happened, and she asks, before anything else is done to her, whether this is going to be on her record forever. The nurse who takes the history is a senior sister called Nuala Mackay, and she begins by asking what the episodes look like rather than what they are called, because the two are not the same question.

The family account, given by the flatmate, is that Zoe goes stiff, shakes, and then comes round after a minute or so with no memory of any of it. On that description epilepsy is close to unavoidable, and the registrar writes it down as the provisional diagnosis before he has finished the history. What the flatmate has not said, because nobody asked in a way that would draw it out, is a great deal about what actually happened. Her eyes stayed closed for the whole event. The shaking was not the same movement repeating; it changed shape, sometimes faster and sometimes slower, sometimes pausing in one arm while the other carried on. She did not bite her tongue, she did not wet herself, and she was wide awake immediately afterwards, asking what had happened and how long she had been gone.

Zoe herself can describe something of the inside of the events. She was aware throughout, she says, of a sensation of going somewhere else, of seeing the room without being part of it, and of being unable to move for a short time. She has never been incontinent, has never bruised her tongue, and has never woken confused or drowsy or with a headache afterwards. The three events so far have happened in the waiting room of a library, in a queue at a shop, and once outside a lecture theatre, and never in her own flat, where somebody is always present. The nurse writes each of these as a sentence, in words, in the space on the chart reserved for describing an event, and then asks the flatmate to say the whole thing again from the beginning.

The dangerous alternative had to be named before anything else was decided, because it is the one that changes everything. A genuine seizure can be followed by a period of continuous activity without recovery, and that condition, called status epilepticus, can injure the brain and kill; the finding that would reveal it is a convulsion that does not settle, with no return of consciousness between episodes. The other dangerous possibility is a structural cause behind the events, a scar or a tumour, which would be revealed by an onset beginning in one limb, by an abnormality on an electroencephalogram that localizes, or by something visible on a scan. Neither is what the family describes, but neither can be excluded on the word seizure alone, and the length of an event is part of the distinction, so the cut-off your service works to should be confirmed against the current guideline and the local protocol.

On the ward her observations are unremarkable in the way that matters. Her temperature is 37.2 degrees Celsius, her pulse is 88 beats per minute, her blood pressure is 118 over 76 millimeters of mercury, and her oxygen saturation is 99 per cent in air; these figures illustrate a patient who is well between events, and the limits your service uses for each of them should be checked against the current guideline and the local protocol rather than taken from this account. The neurological observations recorded at the bedside are of a young woman who is oriented, speaks in full sentences, moves all four limbs equally, and has no focal deficit. Nothing on the chart supports a brain that is currently injured, and nothing on it supports one that is not.

The case is discussed on the ward round the following morning, with the neurologist Dr Neil Corrigan, who has come to see the notes rather than the patient, and with the registrar who admitted her. They rank the possibilities first by how likely they are to be true and then by how dangerous they would be if missed. The list is long: epilepsy of generalized onset, focal seizures from a structural lesion, seizures secondary to a metabolic or toxic disturbance, fainting with abnormal movements, and a functional neurological disorder, a disturbance of the nervous system that is not caused by a structural or electrical abnormality and is not the same as pretending. The registrar is uncomfortable with that last entry, because the word seizure has been in the notes since the triage desk and it is difficult to take a word out of a chart.

The argument for epilepsy is a good one and deserves to be stated fairly. Three episodes of shaking with a period of unresponsiveness in a young adult is a classic pattern, and the team knows that a normal examination between events and a normal electroencephalogram do not exclude epilepsy, because the test only catches the abnormality while it is happening. The neurologist asks what else would account for a change in consciousness, and the answer, on the evidence available, is not much. There is no fever, no alcohol or drug history, no head injury, no family history of anything ever called a fit, and no abnormality of the heart to suggest fainting. The computed tomography scan and the magnetic resonance imaging scan arranged alongside the electroencephalogram are normal, and the blood tests show no metabolic cause, but none of those results was going to move the argument.

The working diagnosis is therefore epilepsy, most likely generalized tonic-clonic seizures, and the neurologist says it out loud and writes it down with the reason attached. He proposes the obvious next step, which is also correct on the evidence of that hour: an antiseizure medication, because an unprovoked seizure of this description carries a real risk of recurrence and of injury during a further event. Driving advice is given in the same breath, because the patient has to be told and because the law has something to say about it, and a neurology appointment is arranged with an electroencephalogram and a brain scan to follow. Nothing in the plan is wrong, and every step in it is defensible; the frame is wrong, and the frame is what the plan was built from.

The medication begins that afternoon, and the ward nurse, Nuala Mackay, stays with her through it and records what she sees. Zoe is warned that she will be drowsy at first and that she will need to keep taking the medicine, and she asks how long, and the honest answer, that it will be reviewed, does not reassure her. She asks again whether the word epilepsy will appear on her discharge letter, and she is told that it will, and she says, in a flat voice, that this is the part she minds most. The neurological observations continue, and a seizure chart is started, with columns for the date, the time, the duration, the description and the witness, because somebody has decided the shape of an event matters as much as its name.

What the nurse does next looks like nothing, and it is the thing the whole case turns on. She sits down with the flatmate and asks her to describe the last episode from the first second, and she asks Zoe separately, without letting either hear the other's answer, because two people who have talked an event through together will begin to tell the same story. She asks what the eyes did, what the mouth did, whether the tongue was injured, whether there was any loss of bladder or bowel control, and how long it was before Zoe was herself again. She writes what she is told in words: the eyes remained closed throughout, the movements varied in shape and rate, there was no tongue injury, no incontinence and no confusion afterwards, and the patient reports awareness throughout.

At the first clinic review, the news is bad in two directions at once. The events have continued, two of them since the medicine was started, and one happened in the supermarket queue and was witnessed by a stranger who called an ambulance; how often events like this are treated as epilepsy, and after how many, is a matter of local practice, so the threshold your service applies should be checked against the current guideline and the local protocol. The medication is making her sleepy in the mornings, dull in the evenings and low in mood, and she has stopped going to the lectures she used to attend because getting through a day feels like swimming. Her flatmate says she has become someone who checks her pockets for the medicine she is sure she has taken. The neurologist changes nothing on that visit, because the events on the medicine are as frequent as the events before it.

The failure is not dramatic; it is the failure that goes on. The neurologist is troubled and slightly defensive, because the diagnosis was reasonable and the medicine is not working, and there is a version of the next few months in which he increases the dose, adds a second agent, requests a video electroencephalogram, and eventually works through the list of epilepsies resistant to treatment. He thinks about it in the corridor and thinks about it again the next morning. The registrar wants to add a psychological assessment, and the flatmate wants to know whether this is something she has brought on herself, and each of these is a reasonable thing to want. What none of them has done is go back to the beginning and ask what the events looked like, because every question the team now wants to ask is a question about the disease, and not one of them is a question about the description.

Nuala Mackay does not take the case to the ward round. She takes it back to the flatmate and asks for the account of the supermarket episode to be played back in the order it happened, in the flatmate's own words, without using the word seizure at all. She writes down what she is told, and it takes four lines, with no neurological disease in it. The eyes were closed the whole time and did not open until a hand on her shoulder made them open. The shaking started hard in the right arm and then went to the left, then stopped and started again in a different pattern. Nothing was bitten and nothing was spilt, and Zoe said at the time that she could see the shelf and could not reach her own arm. And then it was over, in under a minute, and Zoe was asking what she had been doing.

The last piece arrives by accident, and the nurse was there when it happened. The third event had been in the shop two weeks before the clinic, and the flatmate had described it more than once without ever mentioning how it ended. On this visit the flatmate says, almost as an afterthought, that the thing that actually ended it was her taking Zoe's phone out of her hand and asking her to read the message that had come up on it. The shaking stopped within seconds, the way a person stops shaking when they have been given something to do. Zoe confirms it and looks, for the first time, uncomfortable in a way that is not embarrassment. A seizure does not stop because somebody has handed you a telephone.

The neurologist reads Nuala Mackay's notes on the ward, and then reads them again, and what happens in his face is not a conversion but a rearrangement. He says that he has been treating a description he did not take, and that the description does not support epilepsy: the eyes stay closed and never open, the movements are not rhythmic and change shape, there is no tongue injury, no incontinence and no confusion afterwards, and the patient is aware and can describe the event while it is happening. He notes the distribution too, because the absence of any event at home, in a young woman who is never alone there, and the presence of events in waiting rooms and in shops, is itself a pattern. He asks for the electroencephalogram to be repeated with an event captured on video, and then changes his mind, because a request that assumes an event will not produce the evidence he needs.

Testing follows, and it is reassuring in the way he wanted it to be: the electroencephalogram is normal, the CT and the MRI are normal, and the blood tests show no metabolic cause. The dangerous alternatives are now properly excluded rather than informally assumed away. There is no status epilepticus to fear, because every event has ended spontaneously and none has been followed by the long confusion a genuine convulsion leaves behind. And there is no structural cause, because the imaging is normal and the events do not begin in one place and spread. He is willing to say that the events are not epileptic, while keeping the door open, because a functional neurological disorder can exist alongside genuine epilepsy, and a future event with the features of a true seizure will be treated as one.

The change of approach begins with the medicine, and it is done carefully rather than quickly, because stopping it abruptly carries its own risk and because the confidence with which it is done will matter more than the speed. The antiseizure medication is withdrawn under supervision, with the neurology team involved and with the nurse explaining what is happening and why as it happens. The explanation to Zoe is not that she was not having seizures, which would be a denial of what she has lived through, but that the events do not have the features of epilepsy, that the medicine was treating a description that did not fit, and that she has a real and treatable disturbance of the nervous system. The drowsiness and the low mood are what the medicine was doing to her, and they will lift as it leaves.

The family is brought into the same conversation, because how the family responds is part of the treatment and not a matter of good manners. The mother has spent a fortnight searching, arrives with a folder of possible causes, and is asked to close it once the neurologist has read it. She is asked to stop describing the events as fits, because the word has been doing the work of a diagnosis all along, and she is asked instead to write down what she sees: the eyes, the mouth, the movements, the breathing, how long it took, and how Zoe was afterwards. If an event does not stop, if Zoe injures herself, if she does not come back to herself, if she cannot breathe, or if the pattern changes, then the family calls for help at once, because rushing to an emergency department with an event that resolves is one of the ways this pattern is kept alive.

The rehabilitation is the part of the story with the least dramatic moments and the most consequence. A physiotherapist works with Zoe on movement, on the return of ordinary physical confidence after months of watching herself for signs of danger, and on the physical consequences of the drowsiness and the low mood the medicine produced. Psychological therapy is offered and taken, and it is not a form of talking that asks her to stop the events by an effort of will, because that is not how it works and it is a cruel thing to suggest to somebody who has already been told, implicitly, that she is the problem. She is taught that the events have a pattern of their own, that distraction and engagement are part of it, and that the attention she cannot help paying to them tends to make them more likely.

And then the fits stop, and it is important to be precise about what that means, because this is the place where a case like this most easily goes wrong in the telling. What stopped was the count of events. The patient did not get better in the sense that anything was cured or explained away; for a while after the medicine was withdrawn she was tired, apprehensive, ashamed of the label attached to her, and slow to trust her own body again. She had spent months learning to watch herself for the onset of a fit, and she had reorganized her flat, her lectures and her friendships around that watching. The disappearance of the events was the removal of a drug and a change in what she and her family were looking for, and it was not by itself a recovery.

What the recovery actually looks like is boring and slow. Zoe returns to her lectures in the second term and finds, at first, that she cannot concentrate, and then that she can. The flatmate goes back to being a flatmate rather than a nurse, and the mother's folder goes into a drawer. Zoe takes up a sport she gave up the year before, and the first time she falls over she laughs, and the sound of that laugh is the moment her flatmate dates as the real recovery. The drowsiness lifts as the medicine leaves and the low mood lifts with it, and she says that the worst thing about the whole business was not the events at all but the tiredness and the flatness, which nobody had thought to ask about because everybody was too busy watching for the next fit.

The service changes what it does with events, and the change is a piece of writing rather than a piece of equipment. A structured description card is added to the seizure chart, and every event, whatever it is called by whoever reports it, is documented in words: what the eyes did, what the mouth did, whether the tongue was injured, whether there was incontinence, whether the movements kept the same pattern, how long it lasted, and what the person was like afterwards. The card exists because the word seizure was, in this case, doing the work of a description. No test can be ordered sensibly until somebody has said what the thing actually looked like, and the order of the questions in the chart decides which test gets ordered.

Some listeners will hear this story and take it to be a story about a young woman who was not ill, and it is important to say why that reading is wrong. A functional neurological disorder is not a manufactured symptom and it is not a deception; the symptoms are real to the person who has them, they are not produced by choice, and the person is usually not aware of what they are doing. Its cause lies in how the nervous system is running rather than in a structural injury, and it commonly appears in people who have been frightened, or under strain, or managing something difficult, often with none of that visible on the surface. It can coexist with genuine epilepsy, and it does; it is not an alternative to being told the truth.

The question a fair-minded listener will ask is how competent people got this wrong, and the answer is not that they were careless. The frame was set before the first doctor saw her. The family used the word seizure because they had watched the episodes and believed them to be seizures, and they were not being unreasonable. The triage nurse wrote seizure because that was what the referral said and what the family said, and the neurologist read a coherent story with a classic pattern and acted on it, which the evidence at that hour invited him to do. The error was not in any individual's reasoning; it was in the reasoning of a system that could not see a description, because it had been handed a word.

It is worth saying something about the nurse, because the temptation to treat her as an instrument rather than a colleague is exactly the mistake. For a while after Zoe's first visit Nuala Mackay believed she had misunderstood what she was looking at, and she carried the knowledge that she had written something different from what everybody else had written, without being able to explain why it mattered. She was not confident, because the medicine was on board and the neurologist was the neurologist and the family believed what they had been told. She did the thing she had been trained to do, which was to keep writing, and she kept writing the description of the events and not the label, and when the flatmate mentioned the telephone by the way, she took those four lines to the ward round and read them out.

When the plan changed, the handover was written carefully, because the failure had been a failure of communication as much as of medicine. The new summary states what the events are, what they are not, why the medicine stopped, and what would change the plan, and it says in plain words that a future event with the features of an epileptic seizure will be treated as one, immediately, without argument. The neurology team has kept her under follow-up, and the follow-up is not a formality. The nurse continues to see her in the clinic, and the description card goes with her, and the first question at every visit is whether there has been an event, and the second is what it looked like. By the following spring Zoe is in her third year and has had no events, and she accepts the arrangement without resentment, which surprises the team more than anything else.

The overlap between these two conditions is not a technicality but a practical warning. Zoe may yet have a genuine seizure; the door is open, and she knows that it is open. The nursing team has taught her, her flatmate and her mother, in writing, the features that would make an event a seizure rather than a functional episode: a gradual onset of stiffness, movements that keep the same pattern throughout, an injured tongue, an eye that opens during the event, confusion and drowsiness afterwards, and an event that does not stop when a person is distracted. A person who knows those things has a way of taking her own history that no clinic can offer her, and it is the most durable thing the service gave her.

To summarize, this case failed not because the diagnosis was unreasonable but because nobody described what they were looking at. The dangerous possibilities, a status epilepticus and a structural cause behind the events, were named early and excluded properly once the description was available. The antiseizure medication was started for good reasons, made the patient worse in ways mistaken for the disease, and was withdrawn under supervision once the events were recognized as something other than seizures. The decisive observation was written in words by a nurse who asked what the event looked like instead of what it was called, and it consisted of eyes that stayed shut, movements that changed shape, an event that ended when somebody gave the patient something to do, and no event at home. A functional neurological disorder is real, it can coexist with epilepsy, and the rehabilitation and the family's response are part of its treatment rather than a softer option. Every figure quoted here is an illustration of the reasoning and not a standard, and each one must be confirmed against the current guideline and the local protocol before it is used in the care of a real person. This chapter is educational material and not medical advice, and the clinician with the patient is the one who decides what to do.

# Chapter 16 — The Fit That Was Not a Fit

This chapter is told in the order the minutes happened, because in this case the order was the diagnosis. The case is a constructed composite built for teaching, and no part of it describes a real patient, a real theatre or a real service; the sequence of events has been invented so that the reasoning inside it can be examined. What follows is a young man on a table in a day-case unit, a nerve block and a recovery room. It is also a set of observations taken by a nurse in the minutes before a fit, each of which was written down in words and each of which was read by nobody in time.

Callum Reid is 28, an electrician, admitted as a day case for a repair of the rotator cuff of his right shoulder, an operation he has been putting off for some time because of the sling and the weeks of ladder work that follow it. The block is an interscalene nerve block of the shoulder, done under ultrasound guidance with local anaesthetic, and it works well: his arm is numb and immobile when he comes out of the operating room. The general anaesthetic used for the operation itself includes an inhaled agent and a depolarizing muscle relaxant. The surgeon's part of the story ends with a wound that closed easily, a sling, a set of discharge instructions, and a young man who is awake, oriented and comfortable in the recovery room.

He comes back into the recovery room complaining that the room is noisy and that the lights are too bright. The recovery nurse on that shift is a senior sister called Fenella Marlowe, and she notes the time and writes down what he said, because what a patient says in the first few minutes after an anaesthetic is worth more than anything on the observation chart. He is anxious and cannot settle, which is unremarkable after a general anaesthetic and in a young man, and the observations are taken and recorded as they always are. He is talking, which is why the oxygen mask has not yet been put on him, and he says that the room is ringing, and that his lips and his tongue feel numb, and that there is a metallic taste in his mouth.

His arms begin to twitch, and then he says that his whole body feels strange. Not long after the block, and a few minutes after the first of his complaints, he has a fit in the recovery room: his body stiffens, his jaw tightens, and the movements are rhythmic and generalized, and it lasts rather longer than most people expect a fit to last. The recovery nurse calls for help and stays with him. The two minutes that follow are the ones that everybody present would remember differently, and the difference in the memories is the subject of this chapter.

The dangerous alternative had to be named first, and in this setting it has a name, a treatment and a clock. Malignant hyperthermia is a reaction of the muscle to certain anaesthetic agents, and it begins in the operating room or the recovery room with a rising carbon dioxide, a rising heart rate, muscle rigidity and a rising temperature; the findings that would reveal it are those four things in that order, rigidity often appearing before the temperature does. A second danger sits behind it, and it is the one that actually threatens this patient: a fit that is not treated can injure the brain, and an anaesthetic complication mistaken for a different one is treated with the wrong medicine while the clock runs. Both possibilities were on the table from the first minute, and the team had to work on them at the same time.

The anaesthetist, Dr Martin Ravenscroft, and the surgeon, Mr Hugh Tansley, are called, and the recovery nurse stays at the bedside because the observations are hers and she has been with him since he came back. The two doctors rank the possibilities in the way that ranking is done in a recovery room: first what is most likely, then what is most dangerous if missed. Malignant hyperthermia is at the top of both lists, because the anaesthetic included an inhaled agent and a depolarizing muscle relaxant, because the reaction is known to follow both of those, and because the alternatives are rare. The rest of the list is longer and more ordinary: an emergence phenomenon after a general anaesthetic, a reaction to the local anaesthetic, a low blood pressure, a low blood sugar, a seizure of another cause, and the possibility that the shoulder is simply sore in a way he cannot interpret through a numb arm.

The reason for that ranking is sound. A young man in the recovery room, agitated, tachycardic and twitching after an anaesthetic that included a muscle relaxant, with a rising carbon dioxide on the capnograph, is a patient with malignant hyperthermia until somebody proves otherwise. The capnograph is what tips the balance, because a rising carbon dioxide in the recovery room is the earliest and most reliable sign of the reaction, and it separates the reaction from an emergence phenomenon, in which the breathing is shallow but the carbon dioxide is falling as the anaesthetic wears off. The working diagnosis is malignant hyperthermia, the drug used to treat it is given, active cooling is begun, and the theatre team is called back in to bring the anesthetic machine and to help.

Meanwhile the recovery nurse does the thing that later turns out to have mattered most, and she does it without any idea that it matters. She writes the words he used, in his own words and in quotation marks, with the time beside each line. The room is ringing, he said, at four minutes past the hour. His lips and tongue feel numb, he said, at five minutes past. There is a metallic taste, he said, and the lights are too bright. He cannot settle, he said, and his arms are twitching, he said, at nine minutes past, and then he said that his whole body feels strange, and at twelve minutes past he said nothing at all, because by then he was fitting.

The treatment fails because the diagnosis is wrong, and the failure is slow, which is what makes it instructive. Over the following half hour the observations refuse to move in the way the diagnosis requires them to move. His temperature stays at 37.4 degrees Celsius and never climbs; these values illustrate a patient whose temperature is not rising, and the pattern that should send you back to the diagnosis is a temperature that climbs with the muscle activity, so the values and the trends should be confirmed against the current guideline and the local protocol rather than taken from this account. His muscles are not rigid; he is floppy rather than stiff, and the arm that was blocked is the arm that is twitching. The end-tidal carbon dioxide, which had been rising in the first minutes, falls instead of continuing to climb, because as the anaesthetic wears off his ventilation has increased and he is breathing more deeply.

The other observations refuse to fall into place as well. The electrocardiogram, or ECG, shows a fast rhythm without the changes in the shape of the complexes that the team were expecting, and the blood pressure, at 92 over 58 millimeters of mercury, is low rather than collapsing, which is a familiar recovery room finding after a general anaesthetic and a shoulder operation. That pressure is an illustration rather than a rule, and the point at which your own service would start vasopressors is a local decision to be checked against the current guideline and the local protocol. The arterial blood gas shows a carbon dioxide that has come down and a bicarbonate that has not moved much, and nothing in it points to a metabolic cause of the agitation.

And the situation now is the one every team finds itself in when a treatment is not working, which is uncomfortable, because the working diagnosis is the reason the drug was given and giving more of it feels like the only available action. What they are tempted to do is what they would have done in the theatre if the diagnosis were right. More cooling, more of the drug, a repeat blood gas, an urgent blood test for the muscle breakdown that malignant hyperthermia causes, and a plan to take him back to the operating room so that a different anesthetic machine can be used and a different circuit can be flushed. The anaesthetist is thinking about the fact that the drug does nothing for a fit and everything for a reaction of muscle, and he is aware, in the way experienced clinicians are aware, that if the working diagnosis is wrong the treatment is not merely useless but is consuming time that the patient does not have.

There is a further reason for the strength of the conviction, and it is worth naming because it is the kind of reason that makes a team slow. The anaesthetist saw a case of this long ago as a junior doctor, and he has never forgotten it, and everybody in the room knows the story. That history is not a mistake and it is not bias; it is what a dangerous event does to the people who witness it, and it makes the correct response here harder to reach rather than easier. Without that memory, the same man would have looked at the observations that refused to fit and been uncomfortable in a different way. With it, he is uncomfortable and certain at the same time, and certainty is what keeps the frame in place.

And then somebody reads the notes. The recovery nurse asks the anaesthetist to look at the lines she wrote before the fit, and she reads them out loud in a recovery room into which the theatre team is now walking. The room was ringing. The lips and the tongue felt numb. There was a metallic taste. The patient was anxious and could not settle, and his arms were twitching before the fit began. The anaesthetist stops. He asks for the notes and reads them twice, and then he says that numb lips and a metallic taste and a ringing in the ears and twitching are not features of malignant hyperthermia at all, but features of something else entirely, and that this patient has been awake for every moment since he came back and has been telling them so.

The surgeon supplies the second half of the reasoning from a different direction, in the way that a person who has been concentrating on the operation rather than on the anaesthetic does. He asks whether the block is still working, and then, when the answer is that the arm is entirely numb, he asks how long the local anaesthetic will still be active and whether any of it might still be circulating. It is not that the anaesthetist did not know any of this. It is that the question was asked from the point of view of the shoulder, by a man who had spent the last hour thinking about tendons and sutures, and a question asked from a different angle lands where the same question asked from the obvious angle does not.

The name for that something else is systemic toxicity of the local anaesthetic, and it is a complication of the block performed in the operating room rather than of anything that has happened since. The local anaesthetic was meant for the tissues around the shoulder, and in a proportion of blocks the needle enters a vessel and the drug is delivered straight into the circulation, so that the heart and the brain meet a concentration the tissues around the shoulder would have tolerated perfectly well. The recognized early picture is the one that was written down: numbness of the lips and of the tongue, a metallic taste, ringing in the ears, a sensation of going away, anxiety, restlessness, and twitching of the muscles. It comes before the collapse, it is not specific, and it is easy to attribute to something else, which is exactly what happened here.

Why was it missed? For the same reason that governs every other failure described in this book. The frame had been set by the block and the anaesthetic, both of which are known causes of catastrophe in a recovery room, and once malignant hyperthermia had been named the observations were read as evidence for it rather than as evidence against it. The normal temperature was read as an early sign of the reaction rather than as a serious argument against it. The twitching was read as the muscle activity of the syndrome rather than as the twitching that precedes this other toxicity. The prodrome was in the notes and in front of people who were looking for a different disease, and the same words would have been invisible to them in a different frame.

The treatment changes inside a minute. Everything is stopped: the anaesthetic agent, the muscle relaxant, the cooling, and the drug that was given for malignant hyperthermia, which is now understood to have been given for a condition he does not have. The airway is supported, with oxygen into the airway and the jaw lifted, because the fit itself is not the main threat but what happens to the chest during one is. The fit is controlled with an anticonvulsant, and the intravenous lipid emulsion is brought and given, because it sequesters the drug in a lipid compartment, which lowers the free fraction reaching the heart and the brain. From that moment the room changes from managing one catastrophe to managing another, and everybody in it can feel the difference.

The nurse is the one who calls for it, and the way she calls for it matters. She does not announce that the diagnosis has changed, because that is not her role and because an announcement invites an argument. She says that the symptoms of local anaesthetic toxicity are written in the notes, and that she would like the intravenous lipid emulsion used now, and that she knows where it is, because she checked the emergency trolley when she brought the patient back from theatre. The anaesthetist agrees and the drug is given, and the order of those two sentences is the order in which the case was saved. Nothing in this was the work of the most senior person in the room; it was the work of the person who had written down what the patient said.

What follows is an hour of work that would have been easier to describe if the diagnosis had been right. His blood pressure comes up. His heart rate falls from 148 beats per minute towards 80 as the drug is prevented from reaching the heart, and the number that matters is the trend rather than the number, so compare the rate and the rhythm against the current guideline and the local protocol for your own service rather than with any figure given here. The fits stop, or shorten and stop. He does not arrest, and the arrest that follows this toxicity is the one that kills, so the absence of an arrest is the single most important outcome of the hour. The nurse keeps the observations, the times, the record and the intravenous infusion running, and she is the one who notices that his consciousness comes back.

The resuscitation team arrives in the middle of this and finds the room already working, which is the best possible thing that can happen to them. They bring the trolley and the monitoring the recovery room does not have, take over the observations for a while, and the anaesthetist briefs them in a sentence and a half. Nothing about the handover is dramatic; the value of it is that the people who arrive do not have to work out what is happening from a confused room, and in this case the whole treatment of the last hour has been a case of somebody knowing what was happening. He wakes, and he is confused and frightened and in pain from the shoulder, and the confusion is the expected kind rather than the kind that suggests brain injury.

The numbness of his lips goes. The metallic taste goes. The ringing goes. He asks several times why he cannot remember the last hour, and the nurse answers him the same way each time, in plain words, because the explanation of what happened is part of the treatment of what happened. He is admitted to the intensive care unit, or ICU, for observation, and he goes home within a few days. The operation and the block are reviewed afterwards, and the review is not about blame. The block was done with ultrasound guidance, the best available method, which does not remove the possibility of an intravascular injection, and the technique already involved dividing the local anaesthetic and checking for blood by aspiration between injections.

What the review changes is the setup rather than the man. The intravenous lipid emulsion moves from a locked cupboard in the emergency department to the recovery room itself, and the local protocol is rewritten so that the trolley holding it is checked at the start of every list and every patient who has had a local anaesthetic block has a documented plan for who is called if they become unwell. Callum Reid comes back to the clinic a few weeks later. The shoulder is healing, the block site is not, and he has no neurological deficit and no memory of the fit, which he finds harder to live with than the shoulder does. He says that the worst part was the fit and the second worst part was the room before it, and he has thought about the metallic taste often since, because the taste is the thing he can still bring back and the fit is the thing he cannot.

He is told, carefully, what happened and why, and that the overwhelming majority of blocks do not cause this, and that the signs had been written down before anything happened. He is asked to write his own account of the recovery room, and he does, and it is his to keep, and it is not filed in the notes because it is too blunt to be filed. That last detail matters, because a service that cannot accept a patient's blunt account of its own failures will hear the next account only in its sanitized form. He also leaves with written advice about the warning signs of the same thing happening again, in words he can carry in his wallet, and he agrees to be the reason the protocol changes, because the alternative is that the next patient on a table writes the same four lines and has them read by nobody.

The teaching of this case is about timing rather than about drugs. Between the first symptom and the fit there were only a few minutes in which a nurse wrote down what the patient said, and each of those things pointed away from the diagnosis the team was holding and towards one they had not considered. Any one of them would have been enough, and the combination was decisive, and it existed only because somebody had asked the patient what he was feeling and had written the answer in his own words. Between two catastrophes on a table, one of which kills within minutes and the other of which is survivable, the difference is often a set of observations taken before the worst moment rather than after it; the size of that window is set by the local protocol for recovery room observations, which is worth checking against the current guideline rather than assumed.

It is worth saying that the recovery room is the least well-documented place in most hospitals, and this is not a criticism of any individual but a property of the work. Observations in a recovery room are taken to a routine, a standard and a time limit, and the person taking them is often the only one in the room with the patient, which makes the record both more and less valuable. It is more valuable because it is contemporaneous and unprompted by anybody with a theory. It is less valuable because it is written to satisfy a list rather than a question, so that the ringing in the ears and the metallic taste become free text that nobody reads, or do not get written at all.

Every observation in this case was correct, and that is the part people find hardest to believe. The temperature was normal, and it was recorded accurately. The carbon dioxide was falling, and it was measured accurately. The blood pressure was low, and it was written down accurately. Nobody made an error of measurement and nobody made an error of technique, and the case went wrong anyway, because the errors were all of attention rather than of observation, and because in a system organized around the observation chart an observation that is not on the chart is one that nobody will find. The practice change that follows is small and unglamorous. The recovery room chart is altered so that it contains a space for what the patient says, in his own words, in the first hour after an anaesthetic, and the anaesthetist who reviews the chart is told to read that space first.

A standing instruction goes into the local protocol that any patient who has received a local anaesthetic block is asked about the symptoms of that block's toxicity while the block's effect is still on them and they can still answer, and it is put to the trainees that this question is worth asking every time, not only when something has already gone wrong. The juniors are told that the answer will usually be that everything is fine, and that this is a good enough reason to have asked. There is a general rule here for recovery rooms anywhere, and it is simple enough to be posted up. The information that decides the outcome of a perioperative collapse is usually in the patient's own words, usually available before the collapse rather than after it, and usually dismissed as a symptom of the very condition the team is treating. The nurse who wrote it down had no way of knowing that she was writing the diagnosis; she was doing the ordinary work of a nurse on a routine afternoon, which is the only way decisive observations get made at all.

The aftermath reaches further than the service that contained it. A man who has had a bad experience in a recovery room will tell people, and some of them will read the story as a story about incompetent hospitals rather than as a story about a few minutes of writing, and the honest answer to that is that it was mostly about those few minutes. The protocol that comes out of this case will be read by anaesthetists and nurses for years, and most of them will never know the name of the man whose shoulder operation it was. The improvement is built out of exactly those unremarkable minutes, which is how improvements in safety are built, one unremarkable minute at a time, all the time.

To summarize, this case went wrong because the observations were read through the diagnosis rather than against it, and because the words that would have changed the diagnosis were written down and not read. The dangerous possibility was named early, and malignant hyperthermia was a reasonable working diagnosis for a young man who was agitated, tachycardic and twitching after an anaesthetic that included a muscle relaxant. What it was not was supported by a temperature that stayed at 37.4 degrees Celsius, by a patient who was floppy rather than rigid, and by a carbon dioxide that came down instead of going up, and each of those numbers is an illustration of the reasoning rather than a standard, to be confirmed against the current guideline and the local protocol for the service concerned. The prodrome of local anaesthetic toxicity comes before the collapse, and it was written in the patient's own words by a nurse trained to record what was said rather than what it was called, and she is the one who asked for the intravenous lipid emulsion. He recovers without brain injury, and the change that outlasts him is a piece of writing: a space on the recovery room chart for the patient's words, a trolley in the recovery room rather than in a cupboard, and a protocol question asked of everybody who has had a block. The difference between two catastrophes on a table is a set of observations taken before the worst moment rather than after it. This account is educational material and carries no clinical authority, and the decision about what to do belongs to the clinician who is with the patient.

# Chapter 17 — The Cough That Outlasted the Antibiotics

Grace Mwangi is thirty-seven and she teaches thirty primary school children in one classroom, and she has had a cough for eight weeks. She has had a fever that comes and goes, weight she has lost without trying to lose, and night sweats she mentions once and then never again. Two courses of antibiotics from a community clinic have made no difference, and that is the fact she puts first, because in her view it ought to have sorted her out by now. She was born and raised in a country where tuberculosis is common, and her mother, a day's journey away, has coughed for two years and lost weight. She has come in because a chest radiograph has mentioned something at the top of her right lung that nobody explained to her.

This case is a constructed composite, assembled for teaching, and it describes no real patient and identifies nobody living or dead. It is here because the failure in it is not a failure of care but a failure of reading, in which competent people reasoned correctly inside a frame that was wrong. The frame was a bacterium, and it was held for three days by people trying hard to make the evidence fit it. The things that would have broken it were written down, in the notes, by the nurse looking after her. Everything turns on a specimen, a temperature chart, a conversation about a mother, and a radiograph read twice.

Sister Angela Brannigan is the nurse assigned to her, and she took her on the day of admission and stays with her for the whole of it. The observations on arrival are ordinary, which is to say unremarkable, with a temperature of 37.9 degrees Celsius, a pulse of 104 beats per minute, and a blood pressure of 104 over 66 millimeters of mercury. Those are the numbers a ward admits every day, and the values your own service treats as abnormal and the rate at which a pulse like that should prompt a second look are set by the current guideline and the local protocol rather than by this account. What is not ordinary is the chest, where the film shows patchy shadowing in the upper part of the right lung and the report describes consolidation and says nothing about a cavity.

Two possibilities have to be named before anything else is decided, because the one that is missed is the one that matters. The first is a resistant bacterial pneumonia, likely for reasons the team can hear in the history, and the finding that would support it is a consolidation that settles when an antibiotic covering the organism is given. The second is pulmonary tuberculosis, revealed by a cavity in the upper zone of the affected lung and by acid-fast bacilli in the sputum on microscopy, and it is dangerous precisely because it is curable and because the delay after a missed diagnosis is measured in months. A third is a lung tumour, which the same film has to be able to exclude and which would show as a mass.

The team meets on the morning after admission. Dr Yvonne Fenwick is the respiratory consultant, Dr Daniel Okafor is the registrar, and Sister Brannigan is there because she has been on the ward since the previous afternoon and because what she has written is part of the evidence. They build the differential together and rank it twice, once by likelihood and once by danger. Resistant bacterial pneumonia comes first on both lists, because she has had a febrile illness for eight weeks with consolidation on a film and because two courses of oral antibiotics have failed, which is the classic reason to change the antibiotic rather than the diagnosis. She is not diabetic, not immunosuppressed and not an inpatient, and the working diagnosis is a resistant bacterial pneumonia including an atypical organism.

Tuberculosis is named and ranked third, and the reasoning that puts it there is worth recording, because it is the kind of reasonable-sounding judgement that is hard to unpick later. She is young and well-nourished, nobody has confirmed a contact, the mother has coughed for two years without anyone testing her, and the film has been reported as consolidation rather than as the upper zone disease it actually shows. There is also a version of the reasoning that is frankly wrong, which is that two courses of antibiotics ought to have worked if this were a bacterium. The team is not careless; the frame was handed to them by the referral, by the word pneumonia, and by a report that used the word consolidation.

What is started is intravenous antibiotics chosen to cover the resistant hospital organisms and the atypical ones, a repeat chest radiograph for comparison with the film from the clinic, sputum for routine culture, blood cultures, and the ordinary supportive care of a chest infection. The written plan includes a review the next morning and a change of antibiotic if she is no better. It is the right plan for the diagnosis they believe, and the review on which it depends will not happen in the form they expect, because what she has neither improves nor deteriorates. The most anyone can do at that hour is to be right about the evidence available, and at that hour the evidence did point to a bacterium.

What happens next belongs to the nurse, and it is not background. The exposure history is taken properly, which means not a tick on a form but a conversation held in words and written down in words. Grace's mother coughs and has lost weight and has never had a chest radiograph. The mother lives with Grace's older brother, his wife and two children, and the brother's own chest illness two years ago was treated with something and tested for nothing. And Grace has been coughing in a classroom of thirty children since the beginning of term, which nobody regards as anybody's business but hers. How long a cough should count as chronic is a question for the current guideline and the local protocol rather than a matter of opinion.

The sputum is the other thing, and the nurse treats it as a procedure rather than a box to be ticked, which is the whole lesson of this chapter in one behaviour. The first request comes up on the ward round at eleven in the morning, and the specimen that comes back is clear and frothy and is, on inspection, saliva. She carries it back to the laboratory, is told it will be rejected, and says she will get a proper one at six in the morning, because that is when sputum comes up after a night lying still. She writes on the request the time the specimen was produced and what it looked like. It is a small act and it changes what the laboratory is able to do with what it receives.

The nursing continues with a temperature chart kept and read properly, with observations, and with a daily weight on the same scale, which turns a loss of weight that everybody had been explaining into a number that moves. On the second night the nurse finds the bed damp and the nightdress folded over the chair, and Grace says, without being asked, that she has changed it twice. The nurse asks what the sweating is like, and Grace says it comes on in the small hours and leaves her soaked and shivering. The nurse writes it down in words with the time beside it, and nobody on the medical team reads it, because it reads as a symptom of fever and they believe she has fever from a bacterium.

There is an upstream failure before the hospital, and it deserves naming because it is the same failure at a cheaper price. Two courses of antibiotics were given over eight weeks by a community clinic without a chest radiograph and without anyone asking what else the cough might be. A cough of eight weeks is not a cough that a course of antibiotics should end, and the interval that should trigger a radiograph is written in the current guideline and the local protocol rather than in a clinician's memory. In the weeks before the film was taken the illness was in a household with a mother, three children and a school class.

The therapy fails in the quietest way there is, which is that nothing happens. The temperature chart shows the fever returning each evening with the highest readings in the night, and she sleeps badly and eats almost nothing, and the cough is no better. The sputum culture reports no growth of any organism after the full reporting period. The repeat film is reported as no significant change, and the antibiotic is changed in the hope of a better fit, because that is what one does when a chest infection has not responded. Nothing on the ward is going wrong. Everything on the ward is going exactly as the working diagnosis says it should.

The registrar feels it first. He does not want to be the man who stopped a treatment a colleague had started and who had it right, so he finds himself looking for reasons to carry on rather than reasons to stop. There is also a version of this that would have been easy, and it deserves naming because it is what many of these cases were: discharge her with oral antibiotics to complete at home, a review at the community clinic, and a letter asking the general practitioner to reconsider if she does not improve. It would have been defensible, it would have been kind to her, and it would have moved the entire problem out of the building.

The consultant is slower. She is inclined to read a fever that has not settled as a sign that the antibiotic has not had long enough rather than as a sign that it never will, and she cannot switch that off. What she does instead, on the second morning, is go back to the beginning, starting with the first film, and ask for the report from the clinic to be sent over. She also asks, in passing, whether anyone has taken a proper exposure history, and Sister Brannigan says that she has, and that it is in the nursing notes, and the consultant says she will read it, and does not. The interval after which a patient who has not improved should have the diagnosis reopened is set out in the current guideline and the local protocol.

The nurse, who has no power to stop anything and no vocabulary for a diagnosis, notices something simpler than either doctor. She has kept the chart on the wall for three nights and the shape of it has not changed since the day before Grace came in, and the same fever at the same hours is not a fever that is being beaten. She has asked twice at handover whether anybody was minded to look at the sputum culture, and has been told it is not back yet, and it has been back for a day. She is not dramatic about it, and what she feels is mostly the particular frustration of holding information she has no route to deliver.

The clue that turns the case had been in the notes since the second morning and nobody read it, and it is the sputum. The culture reported no growth, and the team read that as a laboratory that had not caught up with an unusual organism. Sister Brannigan says something different on the ward round, and what she says is a question rather than a conclusion. She collected that specimen herself, produced at six forty in the morning after the patient had been sitting up and coughing, and she wrote on the request that it was thick and mucopurulent and streaked with blood. A specimen like that ought to grow something, and if it does not then either it was not what it was labelled as, or the organism is not one the laboratory was asked to find.

There are three others, and each was present from the first hour and each was read as something ordinary. The weight is in the admission note as eight kilograms lost over the summer, attributed by Grace to the school term and by the doctor to stress, and the amount of weight loss that should start a search for a cause is set out in the current guideline and the local protocol rather than in the impression of a consultation. The mother is in the nursing notes too, because the medical team asked the exposure question about hospitals and about countries, and the nurse asked it about the people in the house. And the film was there from the first afternoon, showing shadowing at the top of the right lung.

The radiograph is looked at again on the third morning, and here the team recovers. The shadowing at the top of the right lung, read as consolidation on a single projection, has a rounded lucency within it, and there is a fluid level in the lower part, and the wall of it is thin. The consultant says the sentence that reorganises the case, and says it to the registrar rather than to the team, because that is how these things are said in a corridor: nobody has been treating a resistant bacterium, because there is no bacterium in the top of that lung. She says it quietly and stops, and the registrar stands there for a moment, because the sentence is also an accusation and because it is not one, since everyone in that room has been reasoning as they would have reasoned.

What happens in the next hour would be reassuring if it were not so instructive. Sputum is sent for microscopy and for a polymerase chain reaction, or PCR, test, and the earlier specimens are recalled and re-examined rather than thrown away. The registrar cannot write the referral note the way he would have, because the writing changes when the answer is already known, and the consultant asks him to write what is known and what is still suspected. The mother is telephoned, and Grace is told first and is told with the nurse in the room, and the hardest part is not the diagnosis but the sentence about the children at home and the children in her class. Grace moves into a single room, and the ward manager does the arithmetic about who has been near her.

The treatment that changes the course of the illness is combination antituberculous therapy, chosen on the basis of the confirmed organism and given under the specialist team rather than by guesswork on the ward, and what was given and for how long is a matter for the current guideline and the local protocol and not for this account. The reasoning is not hard, and it is worth saying aloud, because the organism grows slowly, so a single drug allows resistant strains to appear and the illness to return, and the combination is chosen to make that unlikely. Alongside it come notification, which is both a duty and a practical protection, and the isolation of the patient while the diagnosis is confirmed. The examination of everybody close to her is part of the same plan.

The mother is the part of the story the team would rather had not happened, and it is the part that changes what the service does afterwards. She travels a day's journey to the nearest clinic, because the nurse telephoned her and explained what was being looked for. The clinic sends her for a chest radiograph the same morning, and the film shows the same picture in the other lung, and she has had two years of coughing and three years of weight loss and nobody had ever looked. The household is examined, the brother whose chest illness nobody diagnosed included. Grace's own household are examined and are well. Her classroom is a separate matter, handled with the local protocol: a letter to every parent, an evening for questions, and a plan for the children who sat closest to her.

She improves slowly, which is what everybody afterwards says they should have expected. The fever settles within a fortnight and the cough thins without ever disappearing quickly, and the weight comes back in the right order. The nurse spends several days with her on the education, because the treatment only works if it is taken, and she teaches it to Grace, to the mother and to Grace's partner, in the words each of them uses. What she teaches them to watch for is the point at which the treatment has to be protected rather than abandoned, which is the cough getting worse, the fever returning, the weight falling, the sputum changing, any of which means a telephone call to the team rather than a decision made at home. She also teaches them what to say to the neighbours, because the fear of the word is one of the reasons people quietly stop taking their tablets.

The follow-up is described in the same spirit, because this is an illness that can come back and one that can damage the lung even after the sputum has cleared. The nurse is at the clinic appointments, weighs the patient on the same scale, and asks where and when the sputum she was given was produced. The radiograph is repeated at intervals set by the specialist team, and the interval that matters is the one written in the current guideline and the local protocol rather than in this account. She watches for the two things that undo a treatment like this one, which are a patient who feels well and stops taking the tablets, and a patient who feels worse and tells nobody because she has been told that feeling worse is part of getting better.

What the service changes afterwards is small and unglamorous and is the point of the chapter. A chest radiograph and a sputum request become a paired action for any patient whose cough has lasted beyond a set interval, and the interval itself is set in the current guideline and the local protocol rather than here. The sputum request form gains a box for the time of production and for what the specimen looked like, because a specimen without those two facts cannot be interpreted afterwards. The exposure history on the nursing admission form is rebuilt so that it asks about people rather than places, and specifically about anyone in the household with a long cough, because those are the answers that carry the disease. And a retrospective review finds two other patients in the previous year whose sputum was sent for routine culture only, and one of them is written to.

Why was it missed? For the reason that governs every other failure described in this book. The frame had been set by the referral, by the word pneumonia, and by a report that used the word consolidation, and once that frame was in place the observations were read as evidence for it rather than against it. The fever that did not settle was a sign that the antibiotic needed changing. The sputum that did not grow was a laboratory that had not caught up. The weight loss was a busy term and a bad autumn. The night sweats were the sweats of fever, and the mother was a mother, and a cough that runs in a family is the most comfortable evidence in medicine because it explains a great deal and predicts nothing.

None of those readings was stupid, and each was made by a person working carefully inside a frame that had been handed to them. The nurse in this story is not the person who noticed something dramatic, and she would not describe what she did as making a diagnosis. She did three ordinary things: she asked the question nobody had asked and wrote down the answer, she insisted on a specimen of the right quality and said so on the request, and she wrote a description of a wet bed in words that could be read aloud on a ward round. It is not that they were ignored by careless people. It is that the notes that carry a history are not the notes that carry a nurse's observations, and the words of a patient at the bedside are filed where the ward round does not go.

The fix is not to ask nurses to be more heroic. The fix is to put the words where the decisions are made, so that a sputum request carries its time and its quality, so that an exposure history has somewhere to live that the ward round will actually read, and so that a nurse who has been asked a question can see the answer acted upon. The nurse who asks twice is not being stubborn; she is offering the team a second chance at a decision. And the offer is worth something only if somebody on the team can take it, which on that ward, for those three days, nobody could.

Grace is asked, near the end, what she thought was happening to her, and she gives an answer the team had not expected. She says the worst part was the afternoon she was told the diagnosis, and the second worst was the afternoon before, when three different people had told her that she was getting better. The consultant goes back and apologises, not for a mistake that belonged to anybody alone but for the days she spent inside a frame that had been handed to her, and the apology is accepted by a woman too tired to be angry, who asks instead whether the children in her class are all right. That question is the reason the letter to the school goes out that week, with a nurse's name at the bottom of it.

The last thing this case teaches is the plainest one, and it is the one the family makes. A patient is not a body that has failed a treatment. A patient is a body with a mother who coughs, a classroom of thirty children, a house with four people in it, and a network of contacts along which the illness travels exactly as it travels along the air. The community clinic that gave two courses of antibiotics without a radiograph was not at fault, and neither was the general practitioner or the registrar. Each was doing reasonable work inside a frame that had been handed to them, and the frame was the thing that failed.

What the frame was, in the end, is a sentence nobody said aloud: that a cough should respond to an antibiotic, and everything afterwards was read in order to keep that sentence true. The defences against it are procedural and they are cheap: ask the exposure question in a form that expects an answer, treat a specimen as a procedure with a time and a quality, put a radiograph next to a sputum request, and re-read the film when the patient does not improve rather than when the film changes. None of these requires a different kind of person. They require a different arrangement of the ordinary ones, and an arrangement is something a service can change on a Tuesday morning.

To summarize, this case went wrong because a diagnosis that was reasonable on the evidence available was allowed to become a frame, and because every finding that did not fit the frame was read as something else rather than as something that mattered. The dangerous possibility was named early, and a resistant bacterial pneumonia was a reasonable working diagnosis for a woman with eight weeks of fever, weight loss, night sweats and consolidation on a film. What it was not supported by was a sputum culture that grew nothing from a thick, purulent, blood-streaked specimen, a fever that returned at the same hours night after night, a bed found damp in the small hours, and a film whose upper zone shadowing was read as consolidation. Those observations are illustrations of reasoning and not thresholds, and each must be confirmed against the current guideline and the local protocol before it informs any real decision. The nurse collected a proper specimen at the right hour and wrote on the request what it was, took the exposure history nobody had taken, and wrote down in words what the night sweats looked like, and the change came from her. The truth was pulmonary tuberculosis with cavitation, treated with combination antituberculous therapy, with notification, isolation, contact tracing of the household and the classroom. The teaching is that a sputum specimen is a procedure and not a box to be ticked, that a cough which has outlasted two courses of antibiotics deserves a radiograph and a conversation about exposure, and that the family is part of the patient.

# Chapter 18 — Five Days of Fever

Three in the morning is the hour when a children's ward is at its most honest. The lights are off and the doors are open and the temperature charts hang from the end of the beds like small flags, and Mei Lin Chow is awake and crying for the fifth night running. She is four years old and she has been admitted for two of those five nights, and the line of ink down the edge of her chart has the same shape on every one of them. Sister Bethan Llewellyn is the nurse on the night shift, and she is the person who has been putting the thermometer into the axilla every four hours and writing down the answer.

The case below is a composite, built for teaching, and it does not describe a real patient or identify anyone living or dead. It is here because the failure in it is a failure of the ordinary examination, which is the kind paediatric teams know well and rarely admit to. Nothing exotic goes wrong in this story. A child has a fever, and the fever does not break, and a rash is called viral, and the eyes are called a cold, and five findings that belong together are written on five different charts by five different people and never read in the same breath. The illness is not rare, the treatment is not complicated, and the window in which it does the most harm is measured in days rather than in years.

Mei Lin Chow came to the emergency department on the second day of her illness with a temperature and a rash and a mother who was frightened. Her mother has said from the first morning that the red eyes are from a cold. The rash began on the third day, on the trunk and then the arms, and the notes describe it as maculopapular and blanching, and it has faded and come back several times since. Her temperature was 39.4 degrees Celsius on admission, her pulse was 132 beats per minute, and she weighed 15.2 kilograms. Those figures are the working context for everything that follows, and the values your own service treats as concerning in a child of this age and weight come from the current guideline and the local protocol rather than from this account.

She has not had the combined vaccine against measles, mumps and rubella, and the reason is ordinary and common. The family moved from one part of the country to another a year ago, the records did not transfer, the community clinic had a backlog, and the appointment was made and then the mother was working and the appointment was made again and then not. Two of the children in her street have the same story. There has also been a cluster of measles in the district, which is why this ward has an isolation room, and why the infection control team has been asking about the child's immunisation status since she arrived. None of this is unusual, and the mother has apologised twice for being a difficult patient.

The team on the ward is small. Dr Felix Underwood is the paediatric registrar, Dr Susan Hargreaves is the consultant paediatrician, and Sister Llewellyn is the nurse who has been taking the temperature and doing the four-hourly examination of a child who has been crying for five days. Two possibilities have to be named at the outset, at the bedside in the first hour. The first is meningococcal infection, and the findings that would reveal it are a rash that does not blanch under pressure, purple spots, a child who becomes floppy and cold, and a breathing rate out of proportion to the fever. The second is Kawasaki disease, and the findings that would reveal it are a fever of five days or more with red eyes without discharge, red cracked lips, a red tongue with a bumpy surface, swollen hands and feet with red palms, a large node in the neck, and a rash that fades and returns. The number of days of fever that should prompt this thinking is set in the current guideline and the local protocol rather than in this account.

The working diagnosis is a viral illness with a viral rash, and measles is what the registrar says aloud, because the child is unvaccinated, the rash fits, the eyes fit, and there is a cluster in the district. The absence of a cough and a runny nose is noticed and set aside, because measles without them is said to be less common. Kawasaki disease is named and set aside, and the reasons are given out loud. The rash is called viral, and a viral rash does not do that. The eyes have a discharge, because the mother says the eyes are weeping, and conjunctivitis with a discharge is not the eyes of this illness. The hands and feet are not swollen, because nobody has looked at them properly, and because a four year old in a hospital bed does not describe the swelling of her own fingers.

What is started is what is started for a febrile child with a rash in this situation. Fluids, an antipyretic for the discomfort, and antibiotics in case there is a bacterial superinfection on top of the viral illness. Measles is infectious for several days before the rash appears and for a few days after it, so the single room, the gowns, the advice to the parents, and the letter to the community clinic about the other children in the street are all part of the plan on the first day. The plan is written down, and at the bottom of it is a review in the morning with the temperature chart in front of them.

The nursing is the part of this story that everything else turns on. The temperature chart is kept properly, with the time of every reading and the method used. The fluid balance chart records what she drinks and what comes back out, because a child who stops drinking is worse before she looks it. The four-hourly examination is done at every set of observations: the mouth, the eyes, the lips, the hands, the feet, the neck and the rash, in that order, every time. The interval at which that examination is repeated, and the observations that go with it, are set out in the current guideline and the local protocol rather than in this account. And every time, the answers are written down in words, because a chart of ticks cannot be read back as a pattern.

What Sister Llewellyn wrote across five days is a set of findings that nobody has put together. The eyes are injected on both sides, with no discharge and no crusting at the edges. The tongue is red, and the surface of it is bumpy, like the surface of a strawberry. The lips are cracked and red and dry. The hands and the feet are swollen, and the skin of the palms and of the soles is red. There is a large node in the side of the neck, bigger than a child's own head is wide.
