The rash fades and returns, and at one point it is gone altogether for most of a day and comes back in the evening. And the nurse has asked about the cough and the runny nose twice, because the mother has blamed a cold for a week.

On the sixth day the child is worse rather than better, and the reason she is worse is not obvious from the numbers. She is irritable in the morning and floppy in the afternoon, and that order is the wrong way round for a child with a viral illness. The rash is more intense and more red than it has been, and the skin of the hands looks tight and shiny. The fever has not broken once in six days, and the chart shows it, the same peaks and the same troughs and not one reading back to normal, and the interval after which a fever of this kind should send a team back to the diagnosis is set in the current guideline and the local protocol.

The registrar is where the thinking goes wrong, and it is worth saying how, because he is not being stupid and he is not being careless. He has a febrile child with a rash and a probable measles and a child who is not drinking, and the pressure on him is to keep looking for a complication of the measles rather than to start again. The tempting moves are all reasonable. Add something to the fluid plan and ask the infectious diseases team to review. Send the laboratory tests again and add a blood test for a bacterium. Consider that the antibiotics have not worked, which is odd, and think about a reaction to them. Consider a complication such as pneumonia or an encephalitis, and send a chest radiograph and arrange a lumbar puncture.

Two of those thoughts are checked and two of them are not. The drug reaction is checked, because the rash began on the third day and the antibiotic was started on the fourth, and the sequence is written in the chart. The other checks do not happen, and the reason is a sentence in the notes on the first evening that everybody has read since: viral exanthem, probable measles. Once that sentence is in the chart it becomes the frame, and the swollen hands are a rash, and the red lips are a viral rash, and the eyes are a cold, and the neck is a normal node in a thin child, and the fever is the fever of the measles. Everything that does not fit is folded into it, and nothing that fits is looked for.

The phrase itself deserves a moment, because it is worth knowing how sentences like that get into charts. It was written by the registrar at ten in the evening, on a busy ward, in the shorthand the emergency department had used that day, and it was written to be helpful, because it told the next person that this looked like the other two children seen that week. It was never a formal diagnosis and was never meant to be one, and no one would have defended it as one. And yet it was the sentence the night shift read, and the sentence the ward round quoted, and the sentence the discharge summary would have carried out of the hospital if the child had gone home that night.

The observation that opens the case is made by the nurse, and it is made in the most ordinary way there is. At the handover on the sixth evening Sister Llewellyn says that she has been writing the same things in the same box on the same chart for five days, and that nobody has ever put them together, and that the fever has never come down once. She lists what she has written: the eyes with no discharge, the bumpy red tongue, the cracked lips, the swollen hands and feet with the red palms, the large node, and the rash that fades and comes back. She asks the registrar to look at the five charts together rather than one at a time.

The registrar looks, and the findings are there, because she has been writing them down for five days while everybody else was looking at the child. He checks the eyes with the light and finds the injection without discharge, and looks at the tongue and at the palms and the soles, which are red and swollen. Dr Hargreaves comes in, and the consultant repeats the examination herself in front of the nurse, and the diagnosis is named out loud on the ward round: Kawasaki disease, an inflammation of the medium-sized blood vessels of a young child, which in the untreated case damages the coronary arteries of the heart. The name is said quietly and then it is said again, because a team needs to hear itself say it before it can believe it.

What follows is the treatment under the paediatric team, and it is two things rather than one. The child is given intravenous immunoglobulin, and aspirin, and what is given and how much and for how long is written in the current guideline and the local protocol and not in this account. The reasoning is straightforward. The illness is an inflammation of the vessel wall rather than an infection that an antibiotic can reach, and the treatment that works is an antibody mixture that calms the immune response together with an anti-inflammatory drug that takes down the inflammation in the vessel. The echocardiogram is done to look at the coronary arteries, because the damage this illness does is to those arteries.

The fever is gone the next day. That single fact is both the treatment working and the diagnosis confirmed, and the team says so to the nurse first, because she was the one who asked the question. She says that she is glad, and that she is not finished, because the damage to the coronary arteries can develop for a week or more after the fever has settled. The consultant writes that into the plan in front of the family, and the mother writes it down in her own hand on the back of the discharge sheet, and the nurse underlines it. The interval that applies to that watch is set in the current guideline and the local protocol, and the family is told what it means in words they can repeat.

The follow-up is arranged in the specialist clinic, with echocardiograms repeated at intervals, and the nurse is at those appointments. She does what she did on the ward, which is to check the things that will go wrong first. The first of those is the fever, and then the rash, the eyes, the cracked lips, the hands and the feet, and any joint pain, because the joints of a young child can swell again after the fever has settled, and that recurrence is the point at which the coronary arteries are most likely to be involved. She also asks how the child is eating and how the family are coping, because the parents are watching every symptom now and need to be told which ones matter.

The mother needs more than the discharge letter, and the nurse gives it to her over several days. She is carrying the belief that she did this to her child by not giving her the vaccine, a belief other people have given her and that nobody on the ward thinks to take apart. The nurse explains what the illness is in plain words, that it happens to children who have been perfectly well, and that what the child needs now is ordinary care and a series of appointments. She also explains what she cannot promise, which is that everything will be fine, because that would be a promise she cannot keep, and because a family that has been promised certainty cannot forgive a recurrence.

What the ward changes afterwards is written into the local protocol, and it is a form and a habit rather than a new drug. A fever of five days or more in a child now triggers a structured examination with a printed list of the things to look for and where to look for them, and the list is short and is printed on the observation chart itself, so that it is in the nurse's hand rather than in a policy document. The number of days that should trigger that examination is set in the current guideline and the local protocol rather than in this account. The instruction to write findings in words rather than in ticks is repeated, and the ward round is instructed to read what the nurses have written before it reads what the laboratory has reported.

A review of the ward's febrile children over the previous year is done as well, and it finds several children whose fever lasted for days with a rash and a normal white cell count, and one child in particular, whose rash had been called viral and whose eyes had been called a cold by two different people who were both busy and both trying to help. Nothing was done wrong in any of those cases. The same sentence was written in the same way, and the sentence did the work, and the sentence was not challenged by anybody who had not been told that it needed challenging. The change in the protocol is aimed at the sentence, and not at the people.

Why was it missed? For the same reason as every other miss in this book, and this case is the clearest example in the book of a diagnosis that was named and then dismissed on the evidence rather than on the disease. Kawasaki disease was on the list from the first morning, and the reasons for putting it at the bottom of that list were given out loud and were reasonable-sounding in exactly the way that reasonable-sounding reasons are. A viral rash does not do that. The eyes have a discharge. The hands are not swollen. Each of those statements was true, and none of them had been checked, because the nurse who had checked them had written them in the notes and the notes had not been read. The frame was not a failure of attention but a failure of reading, and the reading was one person, at four in the morning, writing down what she saw.

The nurse in this story did not make the diagnosis and did not claim to. She did what nurses do when a child has been crying for five days and the charts say the same thing every time: she examined the child properly, in the same order, and wrote down what she found in words that could be read aloud. The system then read the chart as a set of separate observations belonging to a viral illness, because five findings spread across five days and five sheets do not look like a diagnosis to the person reading them one at a time. The registrar says afterwards that what he would take from it is not a list of features but a habit: write the whole set down, in one place, in one column, and read it as a set.

The consultant says something slightly different, which is that a child with a fever and a rash is not a child with a fever and a rash, and that the difference between the two is a systematic examination and the willingness to be told by it. There is one more detail that she goes back to, and it is about the child rather than the disease. On the third and fourth days she stopped complaining and the tiredness was read as improvement, and on the fifth day she was floppy and it was read as deterioration. Both readings were half right and both were wrong, because a child with this illness becomes quiet when the inflammation is high, and a quiet child is easy to mistake for a better child and then for a worse one.

The nurse had been recording the child's mood at every set of observations, and the record of it shows the change that nobody noticed, and it is the same lesson as the rest of this chapter in a smaller key. Three things went wrong here, and none of them was a mistake of technique. Nobody measured the temperature wrongly, nobody wrote down a finding that was not there, and nobody withheld anything. The findings were stored in a way that hid them, and a measurement stored apart from the finding it belongs to stops being evidence and becomes a series of facts about a sick child.

The mother is asked at the end what she thought was happening, and she says that she thought it was the measles and that she was frightened, because she had heard that measles can make a child blind and can kill. The father says that he had been the one telling everybody that the eyes were a cold. The nurse tells him that he was not, and that a parent who brings a child in and believes the wrong thing is doing exactly what the ward should want. Before the child goes home, the nurse speaks to both of them about what to say and what not to say, because the fear of the word is one of the reasons families stop bringing children back.

There is a general point at the end of this case, and it is the thing the book is for. A fever that has lasted five days in a child is not a fever to be waited out and not a fever to be explained away. It is a diagnosis that has to be looked for. The set for this illness is small. It is the eyes, the tongue, the lips, the hands, the feet, the neck and the rash, and a nurse who examines them every four hours in a child who has been crying for five days is doing the examination that makes the diagnosis. That is the ordinary heroism, and from the inside it does not look like heroism. It looks like a list on a chart and a nurse working down it.

The chart is the instrument and the written word is the instrument, and the institution that arranges for the words to be read together is doing something more valuable than any individual can do alone. The other children in her street who are unvaccinated are offered the same review that she has had, which is a consequence nobody planned and which turns out to be the best thing about the whole case. The parents are told what the echocardiogram showed and what it did not show, in words they can repeat, and they are told what the next appointment is for. And the diagnosis was never hidden from the team. It was distributed among them: the eyes in one chart, the tongue in another, the hands in a third, the fever in all of them, and nobody given the job of putting the five together at the same moment.

That job belongs to the ward round, and it belongs to the nurse who has been with the child for five days, and it can only be done if the notes are written in a form that can be read together. The change that would have made this case shorter is a list of seven findings on the front of the chart. The change that would have made it shorter still is somebody asking, at handover, what the nurses have been writing. Which is the whole of the lesson, and it is worth saying plainly that it costs nothing at all.

To summarize, this case went wrong because a diagnosis named on the first morning was dismissed on assumptions that nobody checked, and because five findings belonging to a single disease were recorded separately and read separately. The dangerous possibilities were named early, and a viral illness with a viral rash, and then measles, was a reasonable working diagnosis for an unvaccinated child with a fever, a rash and injected eyes. What it was not supported by was an examination nobody did properly: the eyes injected on both sides with no discharge and no crusting, a red tongue with a bumpy surface, cracked and red lips, swollen hands and feet with red palms and soles, a large node in the neck, a rash that faded and returned, and no cough and no runny nose. Those findings are illustrations of reasoning and not criteria to be applied from memory, and the days of fever and the observations that should trigger this thinking belong in the current guideline and the local protocol. The nurse examined a crying child in the same order every four hours, wrote down what she saw in words, and was the one who said at handover that nobody had put the five findings together. The truth was Kawasaki disease, and the treatment that changed everything was intravenous immunoglobulin and aspirin under the paediatric team, with echocardiography of the coronary arteries, and the fever was gone the next day, which was the diagnosis confirmed and the treatment working. The teaching is that a fever lasting five days in a child is a diagnosis that has to be looked for, and that the nurse who examines the hands, the eyes and the tongue of a febrile child is doing the examination that makes it.

# Chapter 19 — Agitation in a Lady of Eighty-Four

A woman of eighty-four who has dementia is not agitated because she has dementia, and the distance between those two statements is the whole of this case. Evelyn Hart was eighty-four, had lived for six years in a nursing home, and was admitted to an acute medical ward after a fall in the bathroom of the home. She was agitated from the moment she arrived, and the agitation had a shape that the first day of her admission was already beginning to hide. This is a constructed composite and it does not describe a real patient, and nothing in it identifies anyone living or dead. The case is built to make one failure visible: a frame in which a description of behaviour was accepted as though it were a diagnosis. Every figure quoted here is an illustration of reasoning rather than a threshold to act on, and the figures your own service uses should be taken from the current guideline and the local protocol.

Evelyn Hart had taught English for thirty-one years and had lived in the same small room at the home for six years, with a photograph of her husband on the dresser. She had vascular dementia, diagnosed some years earlier, and needed help with washing but still had long stretches when she was entirely herself. She had been continent until the previous year, and the staff spoke of that with pride, because incontinence at eighty-four is a failure in the home's eyes. Her daughter, Gillian Rooke, visited twice a week. Two objects in that room had no business being there, and the first was a dog, because Evelyn Hart had asked for the dog every day since it died six years before and the staff had learned to say that she was out in the garden. The second was the habit of picking, for she would work the edge of the sheets with her fingers, tearing a thread from the seam, and she did it when she was anxious and not when she was content.

The fall happened in the small bathroom between her bedroom and the day room, in the early hours, and Marion Attwood, the care assistant who found her, had heard a noise and found Evelyn Hart on the tiled floor with the door open. She had been unable to get up, and she was cold and wet through. She was brought in with a bruise over the right hip and a complaint of agitation. Three days before the fall, and this is the detail the case turns on, the home's medical visit had started a new blood pressure medicine for her. The interval between a new medicine and a fall in a person of her age is short enough to matter, and the interval that matters in your service should be confirmed against the current guideline and the local protocol.

She was examined in the first hour on the ward. Her temperature was 36.8 degrees Celsius, her pulse was 78 beats per minute, and her blood pressure was 168 over 88 millimeters of mercury. The observations are given as illustration only, and the values that should worry you in a patient of this age come from the current guideline and the local protocol. She knew her own name and the name of the home, and she did not know the day of the week. She moved constantly, called out and pulled at her nightdress, and she was not aggressive towards anybody. The dangerous reading of that picture was not a behavioural illness but an acute illness with an unfamiliar name, a delirium, and the finding that would have revealed it was that her state changed within the day, that she was clear in the morning and lost by the evening.

The other dangerous reading was the hip, because a fracture in a woman who cannot report pain does not announce itself, and what would have revealed it was pain when the hip was moved and a leg that would not take weight. Three further facts were on the list, recorded without weighing. Evelyn Hart had not eaten properly for several days, and the home had been pleased about it, because she had refused food for much of the previous year. She had been unsteady on her feet for most of the week and had stopped walking to the day room, and the home had put that down to the new medicine. And she had been getting up in the night more often than usual, which the home attributed to the new medicine, and nobody followed that with the question of whether she was passing urine.

The team that gathered for her was a geriatrician, Imogen Clarke, a registrar, Eli Sanderson, and the ward nurse who would look after her, Maeve Donnelly. They met at the bedside in the first hour with the intention of ranking explanations twice, once by how likely they were and once by how dangerous they were. The history from the records at the home was of a woman whose agitation had been building for weeks and whose daughter had described her mother as not being herself since the summer. Imogen Clarke asked what had changed and in what order, and the answer was that the walking had stopped a week ago, the eating five days ago, and a new tablet three days ago.

The differential they built had dementia with behavioural disturbance at the top, and it was there for reasonable reasons, because it was the label the home had been using for two years and the label the referral letter carried. Underneath it they put a delirium on a background of dementia, then pain from a fracture of the hip, then urinary retention with constipation, then an effect of the new drug, then an infection, and finally a stroke and a tumour. The dangerous end of that list was the fracture and the acute physical illness, because both were treatable within hours and both would be made more dangerous by the plan they were about to adopt. Imogen Clarke said what she thought out loud, that a person who is agitated and cannot tell you what is wrong is a person in whom something is wrong, and the agitation is the only language available to her.

What they decided was to treat the behaviour and to look for the cause in parallel. Evelyn Hart was given an antipsychotic medicine, chosen because it is the drug used for agitation of this kind in older people, and because it was what the referral expected. Her regular medicines were reviewed, which meant a list was requested from the home and a call made to her daughter, and the new blood pressure medicine was noted and left in place for the moment, because her pressure was high and stopping it in a patient who had fallen seemed the more dangerous mistake. She was given a bed in a corner of the ward, near the toilet and away from the noise of the main corridor, and the plan was to observe her and keep her out of harm's way at night.

The nursing was where the case was actually held, and Maeve Donnelly treated it that way from the first shift. She set up an observation chart that recorded more than the routine numbers, because she had a rule that anything unusual went in as words rather than as a tick, and she wrote down the call for the dog and the picking at the sheets and the fact that Evelyn Hart could not find her way to the door. She gave the medication round herself at the end of every shift, because she wanted to see what went in and how the room looked afterwards. She organised the toileting routine so that she was taken to the bathroom before she became distressed, and she put a chair by the bed so that the night staff could sit with her. The nightly fight about washing was the worst of it, and it was a fight about dignity, and the staff won it and the woman was left clean and humiliated. And she took the medication history herself, question by question, from the daughter and from the home, and wrote down what they did not know as well as what they did.

The first day went well, and the team was pleased in a way that made them careless. Evelyn Hart slept, she took fluids, she accepted the medication, and she was calm enough to be moved into a chair by the window. Her daughter came in, found her mother sitting up and holding a cup, and cried in the corridor. Eli Sanderson wrote that the plan was working and the behavioural disturbance was controlled. Nobody asked what had been treated. The question, which sounds obvious and was not asked, was why a woman with dementia had suddenly become agitated at all.

The night went wrong. Evelyn Hart was up and about after midnight, and she was looking for her dog, and she could not be settled, and at some point in the small hours she tried to reach the toilet on her own and fell. The sound she made when she hit the floor woke the whole bay, and Maeve Donnelly, who had been with her twenty minutes earlier, was there in a moment. She found her on the floor between the bed and the wall, and she could not move the left leg without the woman screaming at her, and the scream was not that of a frightened old woman but the short, sharp sound of a person in real pain. She wrote down what she had seen and heard, in words, and that the leg was not moved by the patient at all. And then she did the thing that took her the rest of the night, which was to read the notes of the day before.

The ward round the next morning found Evelyn Hart harder to read than the day before. She was asleep at nine in the morning and could not be woken for breakfast, and she was mumbling at eleven, and the impression was of a patient more confused than she had been on admission, which is a sentence that changes the direction of a case. Eli Sanderson felt the pull towards a second dose of the antipsychotic and towards a sedative for the nights, and equally the pull towards writing that she was deteriorating and telephoning the family in. Imogen Clarke asked for the whole of the nursing record rather than the summary on the front of it, and Maeve Donnelly handed her the chart, and it was in the chart that the shape of the disturbance became visible.

Because the observations had been written in words as well as numbers, the chart could be read backwards. Evelyn Hart had been clear and orientated and able to hold a cup at seven in the morning, and in the day room by ten. Then, after the lunch round, the entries changed and the language changed with them, and the nurse on the afternoon shift had written about shouting and about the sheets. The last entry of each day was always the worst and the first entry of each day was always the best. There was a pattern, and the pattern had a clock in it. Delirium is an acute disturbance of attention that develops over hours and fluctuates through the day, whereas dementia is a chronic decline measured in years. Maeve Donnelly had written the word for it once as sundowning, and that word had been the reason nobody looked further, because sundowning is a word that ends a sentence.

The reason the pattern had not been read is worth stating plainly, because it was not stupidity and it is the way the case would have gone in any ward. The referral letter said dementia with behavioural disturbance, and every entry written after it was written in the light of that phrase. A confused old woman calling for a dog is a familiar sight on any ward, and the dog was six years dead, so it had become a detail of the personality rather than a detail of the illness. The same was true of the picking at the sheets, which was in the care plan, which meant it was known, which meant it was unremarkable. And the wetness, which appeared as a single line in the nursing record as incontinence, was read as the incontinence that everybody expects in a woman of eighty-four in a ward.

What Maeve Donnelly did was go back to the home's own notes, because the home kept a careful record of continence, and the last entry in it said that Evelyn Hart had been dry and continent. The wetness in the ward was new. She asked the simplest question a nurse asks, and it turned out to be the one nobody on the team had asked, which was when had Evelyn Hart last passed urine. The answer, which came from the night staff and then from Marion Attwood, was that she had not passed urine since the morning before the fall, and that what was soaking the bed at night was not a bladder that had given up but a bladder that had filled up and overflowed. She wrote it down as a single line, and it is the line the whole case turned on, because she had asked one more question than the plan required of her.

She examined her, and the abdomen was hard and full and the bladder could be felt above the pubic bone. She passed no urine at all through the following afternoon, and the fluid balance chart she had started without being asked to start showed a total that was tiny. Her mouth was dry, her tongue was furred, and she had not eaten for the better part of a week. A bladder filled to the brim in a person of eighty-four is a finding to be acted on, and the act is a catheter, and the insertion of a catheter is painful, and pain in a confused older person is a cause of agitation. The volume that can accumulate in a bladder, and the length of time a person can go without passing urine before harm is expected, are illustrations of reasoning, and the thresholds that matter belong to the current guideline and the local protocol.

The second thread was the medicine. Maeve Donnelly had written out the whole medication history from the daughter: seven regular medicines and one new one, and three of the regular ones had effects on bladder and bowel that added up to a problem in a woman with dementia. The new blood pressure medicine had been started three days before the fall, and the home had recorded the next day that she had been dizzy on standing, and nobody joined that to the fall, because the fall had happened to a different woman in their minds. Imogen Clarke said the obvious thing: a medicine begun three days before a fall cannot be called unrelated, and if it was not the cause of the fall then it was a candidate for the confusion. Eli Sanderson answered that her pressure was high, and that in a patient who has fallen and is confused the pressure matters less than the falling, and the tablet was held.

The third thread was the leg, and it took longest because nobody examined it. Evelyn Hart had been admitted because of a fall, and the fall had produced a graze and a bruise, and the urgent service had sent her for a film of the pelvis, and the report said that no fracture was seen and that a fracture of the neck of the femur might not be visible if it was not displaced. Nobody had put a hand on the left hip, because she was agitated and could not be asked where it hurt. The nurse had asked, though, and the answer she got was a scream. On the second afternoon, when the team went back and read the notes without the frame they had brought to them, Maeve Donnelly asked for the leg to be examined, and the examination found what the fall had produced, a fracture of the neck of the femur with the leg shortened and turned outward.

When the fracture was found, the team went back through the first day with the new knowledge in their hands, and the picture rearranged itself without much argument. The agitation was pain. The agitation was a full bladder and a full bowel. The agitation was the new medicine and the dizziness and the fact that she had not eaten for a week and had not passed urine for days. The agitation was a woman of eighty-four lying in a strange bed at night in an unfamiliar room, which is the setting delirium requires and which a diagnosis of behavioural disturbance has no room for at all. Imogen Clarke wrote in the record that the differential had been ranked correctly and read incorrectly, because a diagnosis of behavioural disturbance is not an explanation of behaviour, it is a description of it.

The treatment that changed everything was the reverse of the treatment that had been given. The antipsychotic was stopped, because it was deepening the delirium, because in older people with dementia it carries real risk, and because the agitation it had suppressed was not a problem to be suppressed but a report of one. The bladder was drained with a catheter and the bowel was cleared, and a fluid plan was begun, and her mouth was looked at and treated, and the bed was moved so that she could see daylight in the morning. The new blood pressure medicine was not restarted, and her pressure was allowed to be high rather than let her fall again. And the hip was handed to the orthopaedic team, because a fracture of the neck of the femur in a woman of eighty-four is an operation and not a ward decision, and it happens quickly, because the pain of waiting is itself a cause of delirium.

Evelyn Hart went to theatre on the second day and came back with the fracture fixed, and the nursing that followed was the nursing of any older patient after hip surgery, except that it was done with the knowledge of what had nearly happened. Maeve Donnelly ran a chart that was as much about the clock as about the numbers, because the pattern was the clue and the pattern was in the timing. She recorded what Evelyn Hart knew and when she knew it, and she recorded her fluid balance, her mouth, her bowels, her skin and her pain, and she wrote the first hour of every night in detail, because the night was when this went wrong and it would be when it went wrong again. Mobilisation began the day after the operation, and the first walk of twenty metres took a quarter of an hour and was done in three pieces.

The change in Evelyn Hart was neither gradual nor subtle. On the morning after the operation she was lucid, and she asked for a cup of tea, and she wanted to know where her daughter was, and she was not asking for the dog. Her appetite came back over the next two days and her continence came back with it, because a woman who cannot reach a toilet will be wet and a woman who is dry will not be agitated about it. She was moved into the chair by the window by the fourth day and was eating with the other residents by the end of the week. The confusion did not vanish entirely and it was not expected to, because dementia does not go away, but the part of it that had been manufactured by pain, by a full bladder, by an empty bowel, by a new drug and by a dark room at night did go away.

Two things were written down after that week, and they were written as rules rather than lessons, because lessons fade and rules get argued with. The first was about the clock, and any new confusion in an older person was to be recorded with the time of day at which it appeared, and anyone who had not seen the patient in daylight was to say so before offering an opinion about her mental state. The second was about the body, and a confused older patient was to have her abdomen, her skin, her feet and her legs examined by a nurse given the time to do it, and the examination was to be written in words, and whoever found something unusual was to be thanked rather than questioned. The home was told what had happened, and the conversation was difficult, because the home had been describing behaviour for two years and had been right about it. Gillian Rooke took over the medication box at home, which had been the source of many small errors, and came in every day for the first fortnight.

What Maeve Donnelly watches for now has changed, and the change is the point of the case. She watches for the clock and writes down the time of every change in behaviour, because a fluctuation that comes and goes is a finding a treatment failing cannot explain. She watches for the wetness, because a woman who has been dry for eighty-four years and is suddenly wet has a bladder problem until somebody has proved otherwise. She watches the legs, asks a patient with dementia to stand, watches her walk, and holds the frame while she does it. She watches the medicines and asks what has changed and when, and she looks for the drug that was begun in the last week, because the week before the fall is the week she asks about.

The first lesson is that agitation in an older person is a symptom and not a diagnosis, and the two are confused because the symptom is visible and the diagnosis is invisible. The second is that a treatment which calms a patient is not thereby right, and that a patient who becomes less agitated because she has been given a drug that reduces her awareness has not been treated but made harder to examine. The third is that the word which ends a sentence, whether it is sundowning or the behaviour that comes with dementia, is the word that hides the most, because it makes everybody feel that the hard work has been done. The fourth is that in a person who cannot give a history the examination is not a formality but the only history available.

It is worth saying how much earlier this could have been caught, because the point of a teaching case is the turning point, not the post mortem. On the night of the fall, the question that would have changed everything is simple to say and hard to do, because nobody thinks it at two in the morning, and it is that an old woman who has fallen onto her hip and cannot say where it hurts has a hip that has to be examined and a film repeated if the pain continues. On the first morning on the ward, the question that would have changed everything is that a woman who has been continent and is suddenly wet has a bladder that has to be looked at, and the answer took one telephone call. And on the first afternoon, the question that would have changed everything is that a new medicine in an old woman is a precipitant until somebody has proved that it is not.

No individual in this case did anything wrong, and that is worth saying because it is what makes the case worth teaching. Imogen Clarke, Eli Sanderson and Maeve Donnelly were each reasoning correctly inside a frame built from a reasonable first answer, and the frame was reinforced every hour by a label everybody agreed on, by a family who wanted to believe that the drug had worked, and by a home that had been managing the same description for two years. Any team meeting an agitated woman of eighty-four from a nursing home would have built a version of the same frame, and any team shown the leg, or the bladder, or the chart would have changed it. What the ward changed was not the people in it but the things it wrote down.

To summarize, Evelyn Hart was admitted for agitation and was given a drug that deepened the confusion it was meant to control, because several things were happening to her at once. The decisive observation was made by a nurse who asked when a continent woman of eighty-four had last passed urine, and the answer explained the wetness, the agitation and the hardness of the abdomen, and it was in the record because the record was kept in words. The other findings, the clock, the new medicine, the legs that had stopped carrying her, and the fracture the first film did not show, were all there for anyone who went back and read. The treatment that worked was the reverse of the treatment that was given, because the agitation was never the illness. Every number in this chapter is an illustration of reasoning and must be confirmed against the current guideline and the local protocol, and this chapter is educational material rather than medical advice, so the patient in front of you always outranks the page.

# Chapter 20 — The Urine That Was Not an Infection

The most dangerous result in an emergency department is the one that makes everybody feel better. Joyce Fairweather was eighty-two, she had fallen at home in the early morning, and the dipstick taken from her urine at triage showed nitrites and white cells, and that result turned a worried room into an orderly one. The story that follows is a composite written for teaching, and no part of it describes or identifies a real person. The case is worth telling because the diagnosis was not wrong in the way a wrong diagnosis is usually wrong, and because everything that was true about it was also true about the right diagnosis. The urine result answered the question the team had asked, and nobody had asked the question that mattered.

This chapter is educational material and not medical advice, and it is not a protocol or a substitute for training, supervision, or the judgment of the clinician who is with the patient. Every number in it is an illustration of reasoning, and a dipstick result read on its own is exactly the kind of finding that has to be checked against the current guideline and the local protocol before anybody builds a plan upon it. The reasoning that organizes the story is a single question: what would have to be true for this dipstick to mean an infection, and had anybody established that? Nothing in the history supported it, and the leg had been in the department for the best part of a day and a half.

Joyce Fairweather lived alone in a ground-floor flat with the bathroom downstairs, and she had memory problems that were mild enough for her to manage and severe enough for her daughter to worry about. Sandra Bell, who came in several mornings a week to help with shopping and the heavy work, found her on the bathroom floor with the cold tap running. She could not get herself up, and she had been there a while, and she was cold and wet through and had been sick twice. Joyce Fairweather had a urinary catheter in place, which had been changed by the district nurse a few days earlier after a spell in hospital with a urinary infection, and it was that catheter, and the way she now lived, that made her urine so interesting to the department.

Her daughter, Hazel Fairweather, had telephoned the ambulance and then the department, and what she said on the telephone was that her mother had been muddled for a day or two, had not been eating, and had become unsteady on her feet. That account was accurate about the eating and the unsteady walking, and it was wrong about the muddling. Joyce Fairweather was not confused when the nursing staff first spoke to her. She gave her name, she knew she was in hospital, she knew that she had fallen, and she could describe her discomfort, which she located in her hip. She said she had thought the room was turning and that she had caught the edge of the sink on the way down. The family had used the word confusion because the family had been told, at some point in the past year, that memory trouble was a form of confusion.

The nurse who took her history was Verity Macalister, and she did the triage examination the way she always did it, which is to say slowly and without the assumption that the answer was already known. She asked about the urine, and Joyce Fairweather said she had no pain on passing it, that she had not noticed herself going more often than usual, and that she had noticed nothing at all about her urine because she could not feel that she was passing it, which is what happens to a person with a catheter in place. She asked about the fall, and about the days before it, and about eating and drinking. Then she took the sample from the catheter, and the dipstick was positive for nitrites and for white cells, and she wrote the whole thing down, and the line that was to matter was the one she wrote next.

The note read, in full: no urinary symptom of any kind, no frequency, no pain, no urgency and no blood, the catheter in situ and draining, the specimen taken from the catheter, and the family reporting muddling over a day or two. Above that she had written that the patient was alert and oriented and knew where she was. The dangerous alternative to a urinary infection was not a remote one, and it was not far to seek. It was the hip. An old woman who has fallen and cannot get up may have broken it, and a fracture in a person who cannot report pain properly, or who has been labelled confused, may be the reason for the fall rather than a consequence of it, and the finding that would have revealed it is pain when the hip is moved and a leg that is short and turned outward.

Joyce Fairweather was dry rather than flushed, and her mouth was dry, and she had been drinking poorly for a day or two. Her temperature was 36.9 degrees Celsius, her pulse was 62 beats per minute, and her blood pressure was 174 over 92 millimeters of mercury. A heart rate that should worry you in a woman of this age, and a blood pressure that should make you look for something rather than reassure you, are set by the current guideline and the local protocol in the unit you work in, and the point of these figures is not their separate values but what they do together. She was alert, she was oriented, she was not flushed, she was not tachycardic and she was not febrile. That combination is the opposite of the picture the team went on to carry in their heads for the best part of a day and a half.

The team was an emergency department registrar, Elena Ruiz, a nurse practitioner, Nnamdi Obi, and the nurse who had taken the history. They met at the bedside with the triage note, the observations and the first results, and they did what teams do, which is to build a differential and rank it twice. In order of likelihood they put a urinary tract infection, a dehydration from poor intake and a chest infection, and then a fracture of the hip, a stroke, a bowel obstruction and a metabolic disturbance. In order of danger they put the sepsis, the fracture of the hip and the bowel obstruction, and the honest difficulty was that the same two things, the reported confusion and the positive urine, were doing all the work in both lists at once.

The ranking of sepsis deserves a word of its own, because the team did not dismiss it, and that is a point in their favour. A normal temperature does not exclude sepsis in a person of this age, and neither does a slow pulse, and what your own service counts as a fever in an older adult is a matter for the current guideline and the local protocol. The difficulty was not that the team lacked a threshold but that the dipstick had supplied them with a cause, and a cause is the most reassuring thing a clinician can be given at two in the morning. Having settled on infection as the explanation, they no longer had to decide separately whether she was septic, because the two questions had collapsed into one, and the question that was lost was the one about the leg.

The working diagnosis was a urinary tract infection with confusion, and the reasoning was audible. She was eighty-two, she had fallen at home, the family reported muddling, she had a catheter that had been changed recently, and the urine had nitrites and white cells in it. Nnamdi Obi said that in an older patient with a new change in behaviour and a positive dipstick the infection was the most likely explanation and the most treatable, and that to be fair to the dipstick it had answered the question that had been asked of it. The plan was antibiotics, a fluid plan and a urine culture to be sent, with observations and a fluid balance chart, and the plan for the confusion was to treat the infection and watch. It was a reasonable plan. It was aimed at the right disease and the wrong patient.

The strength of that plan was that it explained everything that had been noticed, and the weakness of it was exactly the same thing. A catheter that has been in place is a foreign body in the bladder, and urine from such a catheter very often grows something, and a dipstick read on that urine is very often positive. The specimen that was sent came from the catheter rather than from a fresh sample, and it grew a mixture of organisms rather than one, and when that result came back the team read it as an infection that was not responding to the antibiotic they had chosen rather than as a specimen that was never going to mean anything. A result that answers a question nobody asked is the most persuasive kind of evidence in medicine, because it looks like evidence and it is not.

The nursing was where the day and a half were actually spent. Verity Macalister kept the observations, and she kept a fluid balance chart that recorded every drink taken and every void, and she did a pain assessment on a regular cycle, and she did the thing that mattered most, which was to examine her again. Not to look at her, which is what happens on a busy afternoon, but to examine her: the chest, the abdomen, the feet, the skin over the sacrum, the mouth and both legs. She had a reason for that and she had held it for years. A confused older person in an emergency department cannot give a history, so the examination is the history, and if nobody examines her, then nobody knows anything about her that was not written in the triage note.

The first day passed without drama, and that was part of the problem. Joyce Fairweather took her fluids, and she passed urine, and the fluid balance chart filled in with reasonable numbers, and she was oriented and polite to the nursing staff and asked for a newspaper. She was eating little, but she had eaten little for a week, and the family had said as much. Nnamdi Obi came and reviewed her twice and wrote in the notes that she was no worse and that the antibiotic would be changed if she had not improved by the following morning. Verity Macalister wrote something shorter and less comfortable, which was that she remained alert and oriented, that she had asked twice when she was going home, and that the family were still describing her as confused.

She got worse in the ordinary, unremarkable way that older people get worse. She slept a great deal and was hard to rouse for the breakfast. She refused the food and then refused the drink, and she turned her face to the wall when the staff tried to wash her. Her temperature stayed normal and her pulse stayed slow, and she was no more flushed than she had been the day before, and the family were told that the infection was not settling and that the treatment was being changed. Elena Ruiz felt the pull towards a second agent and towards imaging, and she felt the pull towards the phrase that was making everybody comfortable, which was that an old woman with infection in her urine and a fracture she cannot feel is a common and well recognised combination. And she also knew, in a way she could not put in a note, that she had never examined the legs.

The family were told, kindly and wrongly, that the infection was proving difficult to treat, and Hazel's relief at having an explanation that named something visible was almost audible on the telephone, and that relief was the most expensive thing in the case. Meanwhile the whole team was busy, because there was a real job to do in keeping alive a woman who was not eating and was drifting, and the job of a comfortable diagnosis is that it makes the discomfort invisible. Nobody was unkind. Nobody was careless. Everybody had a reason. And a day and a half of it is enough for a team to become completely fluent in a story that is not true.

The nurse who found the answer did it in the most ordinary way possible, by reading her own note again. Verity Macalister had written it at triage, and she read it the way you read a note when the story it tells has stopped working, which is slowly, and without the frame you brought to it. The first line said that there were no urinary symptoms of any kind. The dipstick was positive. Both of those things had been true for the best part of a day and a half, and nobody had put them next to each other, because to do so would have required someone to say out loud that the dipstick might be telling them nothing, and nobody wants to be the person who says that about a test that has already been acted upon.

And then the examination was done, and it took four minutes. Joyce Fairweather's left leg was short. The foot was turned outward, so that the toes pointed across the midline, which is the posture a hip takes when the neck of the femur breaks and the muscles above it go into spasm. The hip was painful when it was moved, and she said so, and she had not said so before because nobody had asked in a way that let her answer. She would not put weight on it at all, and she had not walked since she came in, and nobody had noticed that she had not walked since she came in, because a patient with an infection is not expected to walk and a patient who is confused is not asked.

The fracture explained the fall, and it explained the failure to eat and the failure to drink, and it explained the slow pulse and the raised pressure and the dry mouth and the pain that the chart had recorded as one out of ten because the question had been asked at the moment she was being washed and she had answered about the wash. The score that should have raised concern depends on the instrument your service uses, and the way pain is assessed in a person with memory problems is worth setting against the current guideline and the local protocol. The fracture explained the confusion as well, in the way a broken hip explains confusion in an older person, through pain, through the stress of the injury, through the fever that sometimes accompanies it, and through the plain arithmetic of a person lying still in a strange bed who cannot get to the toilet. A hip fracture in an older adult is a medical event as much as a surgical one, and the confusion is not a separate problem to be treated alongside it but part of the same event.

The film confirmed it, a displaced fracture of the neck of the femur, and the appearance of the leg had been the evidence for a day and a half while nobody read it. The treatment was surgical, and the surgical team came early and involved the team looking after her, and the decision was made quickly, because a fracture of the neck of the femur left untreated and then repaired late carries its own complications of immobility, and because every hour of waiting is an hour of pain and an hour of delirium. The two teams planned the operation together, because the decisions that decide whether an older person gets well after a broken hip are made before the operation as much as during it, and because a hip that is repaired but that is then nursed as though the surgery were the whole of the problem is a hip that will not do well. The antibiotics were stopped, because there was no infection to treat, and the specimen already sent for culture was allowed to finish and was read for what it was, which was the flora of a catheter and not the cause of anything.

Before she went to theatre she was made as ready as an eighty-two-year-old with a broken hip can be made. Her fluid plan was rebuilt around what she actually drank, and her mouth was looked after, and she was moved onto the regular ward where the light was better and the night staff knew her. Pain was controlled properly, because pain that is untreated in an older adult produces delirium, and because a hip that is not comfortable cannot be moved. An electrocardiogram, or ECG, was recorded, and the team looked at the anaesthetic question and the question of her blood pressure together, because a woman with a raised pressure and a slow pulse is not a woman to be hurried into theatre without thinking about it.

She came back from theatre with the hip fixed and with a plan that had been written before she went, and the nursing that followed was organised around three things, which were pain, movement and the night. Pain was assessed at rest and on movement rather than at rest only, because the patient who is comfortable lying still and screaming when turned is the patient whose chart says the pain is under control and whose thigh is not. Movement began the next morning and was done with the frame and with two people, and it was done before lunch, because patients who are sat up in the morning are less confused in the evening. And the observations were recorded with the times of day written beside them, which was the same discipline that the triage chart had used against them, turned now to good account.

The nights were planned as carefully as the days, because the night is where this kind of case comes apart. A clock was in the room, and the curtains were open, and somebody sat with her for a while, and the order of the medication and the observations was arranged so that she was not woken unnecessarily in the small hours. She was helped to the toilet and was never asked to use a bedpan in the dark, and the reason for that was written on the chart, because the reason for it was the whole case. Joyce Fairweather had gone to the toilet alone at home, had fallen, and had spent the best part of a day and a half in a hospital bed while a positive dipstick was given the credit for it.

She stood within a couple of days, and she walked to the end of the ward, and she went home with a frame and with a plan and with the knowledge that her hip had been broken and not infected. Her confusion went with the pain and the darkness and the hunger, and it went quickly, which is itself the evidence that it was never the infection. The urine in her catheter remained positive, because it always had been and always would have been, and by the time she was discharged nobody was treating it. A result that stays positive in a patient who is well is a result that has been reinterpreted, and reinterpretation is a clinical act rather than an administrative one. Hazel Fairweather was taught, before her mother went home, that a catheter left in place will nearly always produce a positive result, and that a result is a question about a sample rather than a verdict about a person, and Sandra Bell was asked to report any change in the way Joyce Fairweather walked.

What Verity Macalister watches for now is different, and the change is the point of the case. She watches for pain that is absent from the chart but present in the face, and she watches the record of what was asked rather than only what was answered. She watches the legs of every older person who comes through the department, and she counts them, and she asks when they last walked, because the number of steps a patient has taken since admission is a sign written nowhere and worth more than most of the results on the chart. She watches the dipstick, and she asks where the specimen came from and how long the catheter had been in place, because a sample taken in the wrong way carries no information at all.

Two things changed in the service afterwards, and neither of them was about antibiotics. Every confused older person in the emergency department now has a documented examination that includes the legs, the feet, the skin and the mouth, written in words by the nurse who did it, and every such patient is asked to stand or to walk to the toilet before the end of the first hour, and the answer is written down whether it is yes or no. And every urine result from a patient with a catheter in place is now discussed as a specimen and not as a diagnosis, because the sample and the patient are two different things, and the result belongs to the sample. The reason for both changes was not a rule about urine at all but a rule about looking, and the team that wrote them had been taught that lesson over a day and a half by a woman who could not teach it any other way.

The first lesson is the plain one: a positive dipstick is not an infection. It is a piece of paper with a few coloured squares on it, and what it means depends on where the urine came from, whether the patient has a catheter, whether she can report her symptoms, and what else might be going on. The second is that a family who says a person is muddled is giving a description and not a finding, and the person in front of you may well be perfectly alert, and the disagreement between the two is itself a sign to examine rather than to treat. The third is that nobody asks an older patient to stand, because standing is not a test anybody orders, and because the answer is written on a chart that nobody reads.

It is worth asking how much earlier this could have been found, because the value of a teaching case lies in the turning point rather than in the outcome. At triage, the question that would have changed everything is the one the nurse had already answered in writing, which is whether there were any urinary symptoms, and the answer was no, and a diagnosis that requires symptoms needs them to be looked for. At triage, the second question is whether the leg looks right, which takes seconds and which nobody asked, because she had come in with a fall and the team had found a diagnosis to put on the fall. And on the first morning, the third question is whether she has walked since she arrived, and the answer was that she had not, because nobody had asked.

No individual in this case did anything wrong, and that matters, because the mistake here was not a mistake of care but a mistake of frame. Elena Ruiz, Nnamdi Obi and Verity Macalister were reasoning correctly inside a story that the triage note had started and that every subsequent observation fitted, and the reason it fitted is that a woman who has broken her hip and is lying still will have a positive dipstick and no fever and a slow pulse and a dry mouth and a frightened family. Any team seeing those five things together would have had difficulty seeing past them, and any team who had looked at the legs would have found the answer in a minute. What changed was not the people but the order of the questions.

To summarize, Joyce Fairweather came to the emergency department with a broken hip and was treated for a urinary infection, and for the best part of a day and a half the infection was the reason she was not asked to stand. The clue was in the notes from the first ten minutes, in a sentence written by the triage nurse that said there was no urinary symptom of any kind, and it went unread because a positive result and a frightened family make a comfortable pair. The nurse who found it did the least dramatic thing available and did it twice, reading her own note again and then examining a leg, and the fracture that explained the fall, the poor intake, the confusion and the constant urine was found. The treatment that worked was surgery, adequate analgesia, hydration, early mobilisation and the stopping of an antibiotic that was never indicated, and every figure in this chapter is an illustration of reasoning to be confirmed against the current guideline and the local protocol, and this chapter is educational material rather than medical advice, so the patient in front of you always outranks the page.

# Appendix A — Ten Ways a Reasonable Team Gets It Wrong

This appendix is a teaching synthesis rather than a case. It draws the failures of the twenty chapters together, names the ten patterns behind them, and sets out the countermeasure for each. The cases are constructed composites and describe no real patient. The material is educational only: it is not medical advice, it is not a protocol, and it does not replace training, supervision, or the judgment of the clinician with the patient. Any figure in it illustrates reasoning rather than a standard, and must be confirmed against the current guideline and the local protocol. The clinician with the patient outranks the page.

The twenty chapters failed in twenty different ways, and it is the shape of the failure that makes them worth reading. Beneath them are ten recurring shapes of error, and an error that can be named is an error a team can look for. Naming does not make a team safe, since a frame is comfortable to hold. It does make a team likelier to notice the moment when the frame has stopped describing the patient. What went wrong in every case was not care but attention, and attention is arranged by the shape of a service.

The first pattern is the early and cheap explanation that fits the complaint and then governs everything afterwards. There is nothing stupid about it, because the cheap explanation usually is the common one. The mechanism is that the questions asked afterwards become the ones the explanation calls for, and the question that would have threatened it is quietly never put. In The Asthma That Would Not Budge a diagnosis made in childhood was still governing the examination long afterwards, so a noise made in the throat was heard as a wheeze in the chest. In The Girl with the Stomach Ache a young woman with pain and vomiting was given gastroenteritis, and nearly enough was allowed to be everything.

The countermeasure has to happen early, while the diagnosis is still a possibility rather than a fact. Name the working diagnosis out loud, write down the two findings that would prove it wrong, and go and look for them. Ask the question the complaint makes inconvenient, even when the answer seems unlikely, because the cost of a cheap explanation is paid on the day nobody asks it. The Fits That Stopped When She Was Distracted is the sharpest illustration, because nobody described what was being watched; the team had been handed a word, and a word selects the observations a team will make. In every chapter of this book the findings that would have changed the plan would have fitted on one line.

The second pattern is the diagnosis that becomes the frame through which every later finding is read. A frame is not an opinion a person holds but a set of expectations about what will be seen next, and it works quietly, because every new observation is filed as confirmation rather than as evidence. The Acidosis That Was Called Sepsis is the purest example: the label was reasonable enough to act on, and then it explained a normal temperature, a normal blood sugar and a deep steady breathing pattern. The Swelling That Was Not Heart Failure failed the same way, with a swollen body read as a failing heart. The trap is that a frame makes the observer feel more secure rather than less, so a frame must be broken by a person.

The third pattern is the symptom that is explained away as part of the working diagnosis. It differs from the frame in being handled one finding at a time, always locally and always reasonably, which is why no individual can be shown to have done anything wrong. The Indigestion That Would Not Settle is the clearest case, because a weight that fell steadily was attributed to a busy season, to worry about a daughter and finally to a successful new diet. The countermeasure is to ask of every finding what it would mean if the working diagnosis were wrong, because a symptom explained away has not been explained but tolerated.

The fourth pattern is the observation that was written down and then filed. This is the most expensive of the ten, because the decisive finding existed in writing, in the correct words, and the only thing missing was a reader. The Breathless Woman After the Flight has a nurse's measurement of a swollen calf written in the lower part of a triage sheet that the medical review read from the top. The Stone That Was Not a Stone has a report of gallstones in notes from years earlier that was signed, filed and never looked at again. In none of these was the finding hidden; the system had no way of moving a piece of paper to where a decision would be taken.

The countermeasure is to make observation travel, and it is easier to arrange than it sounds. A finding written where the decisions are made is a finding that will be read, and a finding passed on in words has crossed a gap that a form cannot. The Indigestion That Would Not Settle shows the other half of the problem: the district nurse who watched the weight fall had nowhere to send the observation, so she stopped being heard rather than stopping noticing. The service that fixed it wrote a rule that an unexplained loss of weight in an older adult is a finding that travels rather than a note that waits. Every ward in this book eventually found a version of that rule, and every version was about the shape of the service and not about the diligence of the people inside it.

The fifth pattern is the reflex to treat anything that looks like sepsis. The reflex is understandable: sepsis is the commonest cause of a rapidly unwell patient in an emergency department, it kills quickly, and a team that waits for certainty may be too late. The failure is in what the word then absorbs: once sepsis is named every later finding is folded into it, and the questions become questions about antibiotic choice rather than about the patient. In The Acidosis That Was Called Sepsis a nurse recorded a deep steady breathing pattern with a normal temperature, which is a pattern of acidotic breathing and not of infection, and nobody read it as the question it was. The countermeasure is to name the label and then, once a day, say out loud what the label does not explain.

The sixth pattern is the assumption that a treatment which has not worked by morning must be the wrong treatment rather than the wrong diagnosis. It is also the most costly in time, because each escalation narrows the field without reopening it, and a patient can spend a long day receiving a second version of a treatment for a disease they do not have. The Stone That Was Not a Stone shows it in its purest form, with analgesia increased through the night and a scan left on the morning list while a man who had stopped passing urine waited. The Cough That Outlasted the Antibiotics shows the same shape with the drug changed more than once, and The Asthma That Would Not Budge shows what happens when perfect technique and perfect adherence change nothing.

The countermeasure is to treat a failure to improve as a finding of equal standing to a result, and to ask what the observation of no improvement would have to be for the working diagnosis to survive. That question is uncomfortable, because a treatment that fails is often felt as a statement about the team, and a question about the diagnosis is felt as a statement about the patient. The interval at which a patient who has not improved should be reassessed belongs to the current guideline and the local protocol rather than to the mood of a ward, and so does the interval after which a diagnosis should be reopened. A useful way to put it is to ask what result would have been expected by now, to look for that result, and then to notice if it is not there.

The seventh pattern is the appeal to a test result that answers a question nobody asked. A laboratory does not think; it returns whatever the request implied it was looking for, so a result can be true and still answer a question nobody posed. In The Bruises That Were Not Bruises the film was examined for blasts because leukaemia was in the clinician's mind, and the sentence about fragmented red cells was never read as an answer, because nobody had asked for it. The Migraine That Was Treated as a Stroke offers the mirror image, in which a normal computed tomography, or CT, scan of the head stood in for the absence of a cause in general.

The countermeasure is to decide the question before the request, to write it down, and then to read the report for the thing that was actually asked. This costs a few seconds and changes the meaning of everything that follows, because a report read against a question nobody asked will always look reassuring in the middle and alarming at the edges. The same discipline applies to bedside observation, since an observation chart is a report too, and the questions asked of it are the questions the ward decided to ask. In The Cough That Outlasted the Antibiotics a sputum culture that reported no growth was read as a laboratory that had not caught up, and the answer came only when the earlier specimens were recalled and examined again by a polymerase chain reaction, or PCR, test nobody had thought to request.

The eighth pattern is the examination that was not done because the patient was admitted on a diagnosis. The reasoning is short and sounds impeccable, which is why it survives: the diagnosis is established, the patient is admitted, and an admission on a diagnosis is read as permission to stop looking. In The Urine That Was Not an Infection a woman was admitted with a urinary infection and confusion, and the leg later shown to be broken was never examined, because a dipstick had said what she had. In The Agitation in a Lady of Eighty-Four a woman admitted as behaviourally disturbed was never asked to stand or to have her hip touched, because she could not say where it hurt. In The Hot Ankle the sole of a foot was never looked at, because the complaint was the ankle.

The countermeasure is to write the admission diagnosis as a hypothesis rather than an answer, and to make examining the parts of the body that were not the complaint a standing part of the first day. A person who cannot give a history needs to be examined rather than asked, because then the examination is the history, and a patient admitted with a label is the one most likely to be carrying something that was never looked for. The teams that corrected these cases did it with the simplest tools in the building: a nurse who lifted a blanket and looked at a leg, and a nurse who asked when a woman had last passed urine. None of that requires a grade or a permission, and any of those examinations could have been made on the first day by anybody who thought to make it.

The ninth pattern is the epidemiology that was never asked for, about age and exposure and risk and season. It is the cheapest of the ten to correct and the most often missed, because the questions are conversational rather than technical and nobody thinks of them as clinical. The Cough That Outlasted the Antibiotics failed on this point alone, with a mother who had coughed for years, a brother who had been unwell, a classroom of children and a country of origin all available to anyone who asked. The Breathless Woman After the Flight needed a family history of clot, a contraceptive and a long journey, and The Girl with the Stomach Ache needed a question asked of every woman of reproductive age. The Five Days of Fever turned on the age of the child, on the fact that she had never been vaccinated, and on a cluster of cases in her district, because the causes of a fever that persists are not the same at four years as at forty.

The countermeasure is to ask about people rather than about places, because the history of exposure is almost always a history of people. The Cough That Outlasted the Antibiotics rebuilt its exposure form around the people in the house and the people in the classroom, and asked about anyone with a long cough or anyone treated for a chest illness. Age belongs in the same list, and season belongs with it, and so does risk: the contraceptive, the medicine, the occupation, the journey, the previous episode, the family. None of these is a diagnosis, and each changes the weight of all the others, which is why a history taken without them is not a shorter history but a differently weighted one.

The tenth pattern is the discharge that assumed the improvement would continue on its own. It is often the most consequential, because it converts an unresolved question into a settled one and hands the patient the risk without anyone noticing. In The Indigestion That Would Not Settle a man was given a review date and a printed sheet of warning signs that he folded into his wallet behind a bus pass and never read again. In The Woman Who Fainted in the Garden a reassurance letter was the reason the patient stopped telling her family that she felt faint, and the silence was taken for improvement. In The Woman Who Woke Up Shaking a period of freedom was written up as a good outcome, and nobody asked what would happen when the tablet was no longer doing part of the work.

The countermeasure is to treat the discharge as a second decision layered on the first, and to build it out of the specific things this patient must watch for, a number to telephone, and a plan for when the plan fails. The woman who fainted was taught to report a pulse that was slow at rest rather than a feeling of faintness, and the man who was losing weight was taught to weigh himself and to ring rather than wait. The Cough That Outlasted the Antibiotics ended with weeks of teaching the family what would change and what must not be decided alone, and The Stone That Was Not a Stone ended with a written plan for the gallstones, deferred until the patient was well enough to have them treated. None of that is a kindness added at the end. It is the treatment.

Looked at together, the ten patterns are not ten accidents but ten properties of ordinary attention under pressure, and they arrive in combination rather than singly. A cheap explanation produces a frame; the frame explains away a symptom and files an observation; the filing makes a test look more reassuring than it is; and the reassurance removes the examination that would have reopened the question. The order is predictable, which is good news, because a predictable sequence can be interrupted at any of its joints. None of what has been described was a failure of care: the people involved were working hard and reasoning correctly on the evidence they had, and any team meeting the same patient would have built a version of the same frame. The defences against these failures are therefore procedural rather than personal: a question asked in a different form, a number read by somebody else, a rule written down before the next shift.

To summarize, the twenty chapters in this book failed in ten recurring ways, each of them nameable: a cheap first explanation that fits the complaint and then governs everything, a diagnosis that becomes the frame for later findings, a symptom explained away inside that frame, an observation written down and then filed, a reflex to treat anything resembling sepsis, an assumption that a failed treatment means the wrong treatment not the wrong diagnosis, a test result answering a question nobody asked, an examination skipped because the patient was admitted on a diagnosis, epidemiology never asked for, and a discharge that assumed the improvement would continue on its own. Every countermeasure is cheap, and every one is a change in process rather than a change in effort. None of these failures was a failure of care, and the defences against them are procedural rather than personal, which means a team can adopt them on any given morning. This material is educational and not medical advice, it is not a protocol, and it does not replace training, supervision or the judgment of the clinician who is with the patient. And because a treatment that fails is information, the patient who does not improve has not disappointed the clinician but has corrected them, and this book argues for listening to that correction.

# Appendix B — The Nursing Observation That Opened the Case

This appendix is a teaching synthesis rather than a case, and the twenty stories in this book have been told already. It sets the twenty decisive nursing acts side by side and asks what they have in common, since they are not the same act repeated twenty times. One is the pulse counted during a collapse, and another is the calf measured on the triage sheet with a tape, at the same point, by the same person. Another is a specimen produced at the right hour and described on the request, and another is a question about a period nobody thought to ask. What the twenty share is a quality of attention rather than a technique, and such a quality can be taught and defended.

Before the substance, the ground rules, said as they are said at the front of this book. This is educational material. It is not medical advice, it is not a protocol, and it is not a substitute for training, supervision, or the judgment of the clinician who is actually with the patient. Every case below is a constructed composite assembled for teaching, no detail of any of them identifies a person living or dead, and real patients are messier than any illustration of this kind. Nothing here should be used to decide the care of a real patient without that judgment and without the rules that the current guideline and the local protocol require.

What the twenty acts have in common is that each one is specific, each one is written in words rather than in ticks, and each one is carried to the team by somebody prepared to be asked why it matters. These three qualities are separable, and separating them matters, because a service can improve one without the others and improve nothing at all.
